When punishment intensity is high, bribery undermines the maintenance of punishment and can reduce the contribution level. The effect of bribery is highly sensitive to the synergy coefficient (analogous to MPCR), punishment fine, and bribe amount. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the prevalence of strategies and contribution rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment and bribery mechanisms interact to affect cooperation in spatial PGGs, but does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by proxy, efficiency) is moderated by the possibility of bribery and the parameters of the game (synergy coefficient, punishment fine, bribe amount). In environments where punishment is weak, bribery can increase cooperation; where punishment is strong, bribery can undermine it. However, since efficiency is not directly measured, these results should be used cautiously for predicting efficiency outcomes. The model is also spatial and evolutionary, which may differ from typical lab PGGs.

- source: Zhang, Y. Q., Zheng, Z. H., Zhang, X. M., & Ma, J. L. (2025). Dynamic punishment-reputation synergy drives cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a reputation-based cost reduction for punishers in a spatial public goods game with dynamic probabilistic punishment greatly enhances the evolution and stability of cooperation. Higher increments to punisher reputation (T) further promote cooperation and accelerate convergence to full cooperation. The model demonstrates that cooperation can be sustained even when the cost of punishment exceeds the fine imposed on defectors, and that higher initial punishment probabilities speed up the emergence of cooperation. The effect of the maximum reputation threshold is minimal. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial public goods games with dynamic punishment and reputation-based cost reduction, punishment mechanisms can robustly increase cooperation rates, even under adverse cost conditions. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation rates and not efficiency or group payoff, the paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The payoff structure is specified, so in principle, efficiency could be inferred, but the paper does not report these results. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides only indirect support, suggesting that conditions which increase cooperation rate (such as reputation-based cost reduction for punishers) are likely to also increase efficiency, but this is not demonstrated directly.

- source: Qian, J., Zhang, T. D., Zhang, Y. F., Chai, Y. T., Sun, X., & Wang, Z. (2023). The construction of peer punishment preference: how central power shapes prosocial and antisocial punishment behaviors. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The model demonstrates that the effectiveness and efficiency of central power (e.g., the Rule of Law) determine the prevalence of antisocial punishment in a population, while prosocial punishment remains stable. Antisocial punishment is highest at intermediate levels of central power and suppressed when central power is either very strong or very weak. The population can be decomposed into three basic types (Collaboration Enthusiast, Fence Sitter, Rational Bystander), and the central power shifts the proportions of these types, which in turn determines the observed punishment and contribution behaviors. The model is validated at the 'phenomenal level' by comparison to cross-regional experimental data, but does not directly report efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how institutional context (central power) shapes the behavioral landscape of punishment and cooperation in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions and control efficiency. It is most useful for predicting how changes in central authority or social norms might shift the prevalence of antisocial punishment and, indirectly, contribution rates. For direct efficiency prediction, empirical or simulation studies that report group payoff or efficiency are needed.

- source: Odouard, V. V., Smirnova, D., & Edelman, S. (2023). Polarize, catalyze, and stabilize: how a minority of norm internalizers amplify group selection and punishment. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The presence of norm internalizers in the strategy space (even as a small minority, around 10% of the population) leads to higher mean cooperation rates, longer and more stable periods of high cooperation, and fewer long periods of low cooperation, compared to conditions with only group selection and punishment. This effect is most pronounced at intermediate benefit-to-cost ratios, where cooperation is possible but not guaranteed. The mechanism is indirect: norm internalizers polarize groups (increasing inter-group variance), catalyze spikes in cooperation when global cooperation is low, and stabilize high-cooperation states. The effect is not due to norm internalizers themselves cooperating more, but to their influence on the behavior of other agents. The paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes, so the direct effect on efficiency is not established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in agent-based models of public goods games with group selection and imperfect punishment, adding a minority of norm internalizers can substantially increase mean cooperation rates, especially at intermediate benefit-to-cost ratios. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation rates and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as indirect evidence for predicting efficiency: higher cooperation rates likely imply higher efficiency, but the mapping is not quantified here. The model assumes punishment is imperfect (not all defections are observed), and punishment is implemented as withholding benefits rather than costly punishment. The results are robust across two different model structures (abstract and spatial/naturalistic). For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of norm internalization mechanisms can amplify the effect of punishment and group selection on cooperation, but does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency.

- source: Guo, P., Wang, X. N., & Zhang, D. (2023). Optimization of supervision mechanism for public cooperation considering limited supervision ability and heterogeneous preferences. *Journal of Intelligent & Fuzzy Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that targeted punishment via the HPS mechanism increases the fraction of cooperators compared to random supervision, especially at higher synergy factors and with well-chosen exemption list parameters. Increasing supervision ability, penalty severity, and exemption list capacity generally increases cooperation rates. Moderately long observation periods and short removal times also promote cooperation. The results are robust across different network structures. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that targeted punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in public goods games, and that design parameters such as supervision ability, penalty severity, and exemption list management are important moderators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results may be useful for predicting the direction of change in cooperation rates under different punishment regimes, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Zhang, G., Yao, Y. C., Zeng, Z. Y., Feng, M. Y., & Chica, M. (2025). The evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods game with tolerant punishment based on reputation threshold. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing punishment targeted at defectors in low-reputation groups, especially with a high reputation threshold and strong punishment, substantially increases the cooperation rate in spatial public goods games. The synergy of reputation and punishment mechanisms is key: when both are present and the environment is strict (high threshold, high punishment), cooperation dominates. When punishment is absent or the reputation threshold is low (lenient environment), defection dominates. The model demonstrates that the structure and targeting of punishment (via group reputation) can be critical for sustaining cooperation. However, the findings are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial PGGs, the presence and targeting of punishment (especially when combined with reputation mechanisms) can dramatically increase cooperation rates. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results suggest that punishment targeted at low-reputation groups is likely to increase efficiency if cooperation and efficiency are closely linked. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, the direct quantitative effect on efficiency cannot be inferred. The findings are most useful for understanding how punishment design and reputation interact to affect cooperation, which may be a proxy for efficiency in similar models.

- source: Battu, B. (2021). Evolution of altruistic punishments among heterogeneous conditional cooperators. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated public goods game with a population of heterogeneous conditional cooperators, the evolution of altruistic punishment (where more willing cooperators are also more willing to punish) can stabilize high levels of cooperation. The effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation depends on the inflicted cost of punishment and the frequency with which punishment opportunities arise. When punishment is frequent and the inflicted cost is high, the population evolves toward more cooperative and punitive types, and donation rates are high. When punishment is infrequent or the inflicted cost is low, cooperation collapses. The model also shows that it is not necessary to punish all free riders equally; targeting persistent (selfish) free riders is sufficient. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but higher donation rates are interpreted as indicative of higher group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment in repeated public goods games with conditional cooperators can substantially increase cooperation rates, especially when punishment is sufficiently costly to the target and opportunities to punish are frequent. However, because the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, its quantitative value for predicting efficiency is limited. The main actionable insight is that punishment is most effective when it is frequent and sufficiently severe, and that selective (generous) punishment targeting persistent free riders is sufficient to stabilize cooperation. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (with high inflicted cost and frequent opportunities) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, but the magnitude cannot be directly inferred from this paper.

- source: Niu, H., Chen, Y. Y., Ye, H., Zhang, H., Li, Y., & Chen, S. (2020). Distinguishing punishing costly signals from nonpunishing costly signals can facilitate the emergence of altruistic punishment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game, if costly signaling is effective and/or if altruistic punishment is treated as a distinctive and more valuable signal than nonpunishing cooperation, then altruistic punishment can emerge and support cooperation. When punishment is not distinguished as a special costly signal, or when the efficacy of costly signaling is low, cooperation and punishment do not emerge. The results are robust across a range of parameters, including different initial population compositions and values of the marginal per-capita return (mpcr). However, the study does not report direct effects on group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the evolutionary stability and frequency of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by clarifying that the emergence and stability of punishment (and thus, by implication, higher efficiency) in public goods games depends on whether punishment is socially recognized as a distinctive and valuable costly signal. The findings suggest that, in environments where punishment is not socially salient or is not treated as a special signal, enabling punishment may not improve cooperation or efficiency. However, if punishment is socially valued, it can support cooperation even when other mechanisms are weak. The paper does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency, so its value for direct efficiency prediction is limited, but it is informative about the conditions under which punishment is likely to be effective.

- source: Du, K., Fan, R. G., Xue, H., Wang, Y. T., & Bao, X. G. (2023). Evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods game based on heterogenous social preferences and dynamic punishment. *Journal of Intelligent & Fuzzy Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that (1) heterogeneous social preferences (especially altruism) promote higher cooperation rates in spatial PGGs; (2) dynamic punishment mechanisms are more effective than static punishment in increasing cooperation and addressing second-order free-riding; (3) the enhancement factor (r_e, analogous to MPCR) is a key moderator—when it is low, cooperation is hard to sustain even with punishment, but when it is high, cooperation is high even without punishment; (4) inequality aversion slightly inhibits cooperation, and reciprocity has a mixed effect depending on r_e. All findings are based on simulation of agent-based models, with the main outcome being the proportion of cooperators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task: it suggests that enabling punishment (especially dynamic punishment) increases cooperation rates in spatial PGGs, particularly when the enhancement factor is moderate. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes. The findings are most useful for understanding how punishment and social preference heterogeneity affect cooperation rates, which may correlate with efficiency in some settings. The model's focus on spatial structure and dynamic punishment is relevant for games with similar features.

- source: Spitzer, M. L. (2016). Preferences over Punishment and Reward Mechanisms in Social Dilemmas. *Journal of Institutional and Theoretical Economics-zeitschrift Fur Die Gesamte Staatswissenschaft*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper does not present new empirical findings but critiques the interpretability of Kocher and Matzat's experimental results. It notes that subjects' preferences over punishment and reward mechanisms are difficult to interpret due to the multiplicity of possible utility functions and equilibria. The comment also observes that the effects of punishment on contribution are sensitive to the details of the punishment structure, and that the presence of a constitutional convention stage (where subjects choose the institutional mechanism) adds further complexity. The author suggests that simpler experimental designs would make it easier to interpret the effects of institutional choices on cooperation and contributions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-good games. However, it offers important cautionary notes: (1) the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency is highly sensitive to the details of the punishment mechanism (e.g., cost, impact, available choices); (2) subject beliefs and the complexity of institutional choice can obscure the relationship between mechanism and outcome; (3) the presence of multiple equilibria under different utility functions makes it difficult to infer efficiency effects from observed preferences or voting patterns. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that careful attention must be paid to the specific design dimensions of the game, as small changes can have large effects on outcomes.

- source: Ogaki, M., & Tanaka, S. C. (2017). Social Preferences. *Behavioral Economics: Toward a New Economics by Integration with Traditional Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The chapter reviews evidence that, in public goods games, the introduction of costly punishment opportunities (as in Fehr and Gächter 2000) leads to higher contribution rates and prevents the typical decay of cooperation seen in repeated games without punishment. It explains these results using models of social preferences, especially inequality aversion. However, the chapter does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes directly. The main findings are that punishment increases cooperation (contribution rates) and that social preference models can explain both altruistic and selfish behaviors depending on the context (e.g., presence of competition).
  prediction_guidance: This chapter provides indirect support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation, which is often associated with higher efficiency, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, the chapter supports the qualitative expectation that punishment increases cooperation, but does not allow for precise prediction of efficiency changes. The evidence is most useful for understanding mechanisms and for qualitative directionality, not for quantitative prediction.

- source: Zachník, V. (2023). Social Norms and Agent Types: Bridging the Gap Between the Theoretical Models and Their Applications. *Philosophy of the Social Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes a refined theoretical model of social norms that distinguishes between norm subjects, enforcers, and audience, and analyzes their mutual influences and behavioral possibilities. It argues that the presence of enforcers (who punish norm violations) is crucial for sustaining cooperation, referencing public goods game experiments and the literature on punishment. The paper also discusses how group identity, audience effects, and the structure of enforcement (centralized vs. distributed) can affect cooperation and norm stability. It introduces the concepts of 'misplacement' (enforcers avoiding punishment duties) and 'identification avoidance' (subjects avoiding norm-triggering situations) as subtle forms of norm violation. However, the paper does not present new empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes; its contribution is conceptual and integrative.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and mechanistic insights into how punishment, enforcement roles, and group structure may affect cooperation and norm stability in public goods game-like environments. It affirms, based on cited literature, that punishment can increase cooperation and that the structure of enforcement (centralized vs. distributed) can moderate these effects. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates for predicting efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions. Its value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of agent roles, group identity, and enforcement structure as potential moderators, but it does not supply empirical data for model training or calibration.

- source: Levy, D. A. (2022). Optimizing the social utility of judicial punishment: An evolutionary biology and neuroscience perspective. *Frontiers in Human Neuroscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that the human tendency to punish non-cooperators, even at a personal cost, is rooted in evolutionary and neural mechanisms. It reviews evidence from public goods games and other economic experiments showing that punishment can increase cooperation and, in some cases, average group profit. The author discusses the adaptive value of punishment, the neural reward mechanisms involved, and the importance of institutionalizing punishment to maintain social order and cooperation. The paper also highlights complexities and limitations, such as the costliness of punishment, the potential for retaliation, and the need for centralized enforcement in large societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and review-based support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation in public goods games and, under some conditions, improve group outcomes. It references empirical studies (e.g., Fehr & Gächter, Gächter et al., 2008) that find punishment can increase average profit, but also notes limitations and boundary conditions (e.g., costliness, retaliation, institutional context). For the prediction task, this paper supports the general mechanism by which punishment can improve efficiency, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence or dimension-specific effect sizes. It is most useful for contextualizing why and when punishment might be effective, rather than for precise prediction.

- source: Vincent, A. (2017). Punishment and status in collective action: How status hierarchies foster optimal punishment use. *Sociology Compass*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that status hierarchies can endogenously coordinate punishment in public goods games, leading to more optimal (i.e., less costly and more effective) use of punishment. High status actors are more likely to be expected to punish when punishment is seen as group-oriented, and their punishment is more likely to promote subsequent cooperation and reduce the need for further punishment. The author suggests that these dynamics can help groups realize public goods more efficiently, but this is a theoretical claim rather than an empirically demonstrated effect. The paper also discusses why typical experimental designs may fail to observe these effects, due to their suppression of status emergence and communication.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical arguments and literature synthesis suggesting that the presence of status hierarchies and the assignment of punishment responsibility to high status actors could improve the efficiency of punishment in public goods games by reducing unnecessary or counterproductive punishment and fostering cooperation. However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that game designs allowing status differentiation, communication, or leadership roles may see more efficient punishment use, but this is not quantified. The paper is less useful for direct prediction of efficiency from game design dimensions in the absence of empirical data.

- source: Duong, M. H., Durbac, C. M., & Han, T. A. (2024). Cost Optimisation of Individual-Based Institutional Reward Incentives for Promoting Cooperation in Finite Populations. *Bulletin of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the expected total cost to the institution for achieving a target cooperation level in finite-population PGGs depends strongly on the threshold for incentive application (t), the strength of selection (β), mutation rate (μ), and the type of incentive (reward, punishment, hybrid). For small thresholds (t=1), the cost function is always non-decreasing in the per-capita incentive cost, and explicit formulas are derived. For larger thresholds, the cost function can exhibit non-monotonic behavior and phase transitions, especially at high selection strength. Numerical results show that both reward and punishment incentives can be optimized for cost, but the optimal cost and its behavior depend on the game parameters and incentive scheme. The paper provides a framework for predicting the institutional cost required to achieve a desired cooperation level, given the game design and population parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and numerical evidence on how the cost of institutional incentives (reward, punishment, hybrid) required to achieve a target cooperation level in PGG-like environments depends on game design dimensions such as player count, group size, marginal per-capita return, and the structure of the incentive scheme (threshold t, cost per unit, effectiveness). While the main outcome is the institutional cost rather than group efficiency, the results are directly relevant for predicting how changes in punishment or reward design (e.g., cost, threshold, effectiveness) will affect the efficiency of institutional intervention. The paper is most informative for settings where the goal is to optimize the cost of achieving cooperation, rather than to maximize group payoff per se. It provides explicit formulas and numerical results that can be used to inform predictions about the efficiency of punishment or reward interventions, conditional on game design and baseline cooperation levels.

- source: Jia, D. Y., & Wang, X. F. (2024). Synergistic effects of global exclusion and mutation on replicator dynamics of public cooperation. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that global exclusion (a centralized exclusion mechanism) in the public goods game leads to higher group efficiency and can robustly promote cooperation, even in the presence of unbiased mutation. Theoretical analysis and agent-based simulations show that, for reasonable exclusion costs, the stationary level of global excluders (and thus group payoff) is higher than in standard PGGs. Mutation (strategy exploration) can synergistically interact with global exclusion, further increasing the emergent level of cooperation and efficiency, with an optimal mutation rate maximizing this effect. The model also identifies bifurcation structures and regions of parameter space where global exclusion is most effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling a centralized exclusion mechanism (a form of punishment) in a PGG-like environment increases group efficiency, especially when the cost of exclusion is not too high. The effect is robust to the presence of unbiased mutation (strategy exploration), and there is an optimal mutation rate that maximizes efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, given a control game without exclusion/punishment, enabling a global exclusion mechanism (with reasonable cost) will increase average efficiency, and that the effect is further enhanced by moderate levels of mutation/exploration. The results are most directly applicable to infinitely well-mixed, large-population, one-shot or non-repeated PGGs with centralized exclusion and no second-order free-riding. The model does not address peer punishment, reward, or repeated interactions.

- source: Wang, X. J., Ding, R., Zhao, J. H., & Gu, C. L. (2022). The rise and fall of cooperation in populations with multiple groups. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of inequity-averse individuals (both punishers and rewarders) can promote the survival of contributors and the elimination of defectors in spatial public goods games, but the effectiveness depends on the synergy factor (r), the cost (alpha), and the intensity (beta) of punishment/reward. First-order inequity aversion (punishment) is more effective at low r, while second-order (reward) is more effective at high r. Increasing the cost of punishment/reward (alpha) undermines the effect of punishment more than reward. Increasing the intensity (beta) generally promotes cooperation, but can also lead to cyclic dominance phases where defectors persist. The model shows that the traditional result that punishment is more effective than reward does not always hold when punishment/reward is based on payoff inequity rather than strategy. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so implications for efficiency are indirect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment and reward mechanisms, when based on payoff inequity rather than strategy, affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment and reward depends on the synergy factor, cost, and intensity, and that the relationship is non-monotonic and context-dependent. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are most relevant for understanding how design dimensions like mpcr (r), punishment/reward cost and effectiveness, and the presence of reward interact to affect the prevalence of cooperation, but not directly the efficiency.

- source: Yang, L. H., Ning, Y. P., & Zhang, L. Z. (2025). Reconstruction of micro-dynamics characterizing human decision-making behavior in repeated social dilemmas. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishing the minority strategy in each group (regardless of whether it is cooperation or defection) can strongly promote the emergence of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. As the punishment fine increases, the fraction of cooperators rises, and for high punishment, the system can abruptly transition from full defection to full cooperation as the multiplication factor increases. Theoretical analysis shows that the payoff difference between cooperators and defectors increases with punishment severity, favoring the expansion of cooperator clusters. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the effect of this punishment rule on overall welfare or efficiency remains unclear.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishing the minority strategy in a spatial PGG can increase the fraction of cooperators, especially with higher punishment fines. However, because the main outcome is cooperation rate and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency outcomes. Theoretical payoff differences are provided, but without explicit efficiency or welfare analysis, it is unclear whether the increased cooperation translates to higher group efficiency, especially given the costs of punishment. The paper is most informative for predicting cooperation rates under minority punishment rules, but less so for efficiency predictions required by the downstream task.

- source: Chen, Q., & Chen, T. (2020). Group rewarding can promote cooperation and save costs in public goods games. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that group rewarding, when combined with a blame/shame feedback mechanism, can promote high levels of cooperation and total contributions in PGGs, often at a much lower cost than traditional individual rewards. The effectiveness and cost-efficiency of the mechanism depend on the synergy factor (mpcr equivalent), the cost of being an initiator, and the reward adjustment coefficient. There is an optimal region for the reward parameter where contributions are maximized and costs minimized. The model demonstrates that, in large populations, group rewarding can be a scalable and cost-effective way to promote cooperation. The presence of the blame/shame mechanism is critical for maximizing the effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that group-based reward mechanisms, especially when combined with non-monetary social feedback (blame/shame), can increase efficiency in PGG-like environments. While it does not directly study punishment, the findings suggest that soft social sanctions can substitute for costly punishment in promoting cooperation. For prediction tasks, the results indicate that enabling group reward (with social feedback) in a PGG with known control efficiency will likely increase efficiency, especially if the reward parameters are tuned to an optimal intermediate value. The model's focus on cost-effectiveness is particularly relevant for predicting the net efficiency impact of interventions. However, direct predictions about monetary punishment are not possible from this paper.

- source: Zhang, X. Y., Chen, T., Chen, Q., & Li, X. Y. (2020). Will you cooperate in case the payoff can be guaranteed?. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that introducing an insurance-like incentive mechanism in a PGG leads to higher cooperation rates and increased group efficiency (net profit, NP), provided that the extra-charge (insurance premium) and incentive rate (compensation multiplier) are set at moderate levels. Too high an extra-charge or too low an incentive rate reduces the effectiveness of the mechanism. The model demonstrates that the mechanism can eliminate second-order free riders and allows organizers to save on incentive costs compared to standard reward mechanisms. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that insurance-like incentive mechanisms can increase efficiency in PGGs, especially when the parameters are set appropriately. While it does not study punishment directly, it offers insight into how alternative incentive mechanisms (distinct from punishment) can affect efficiency. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that introducing a well-calibrated incentive mechanism (with moderate cost and effectiveness) can increase group efficiency relative to a control without such mechanisms. However, the findings are based on simulation, not empirical data, and may not transfer directly to real-world or experimental PGGs with punishment.

- source: Yang, L. H., Xu, Z. J., Zhang, L. Z., & Yang, D. X. (2018). Benefits of intervention in spatial public goods games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional intervention, modeled as a central authority forcing some defectors to contribute, can increase the efficiency (average total payoff) of a spatial PGG, especially when the synergy factor is high and the intervention cost is controlled. Weak intervention can paradoxically reduce cooperation and efficiency, but moderate intervention can lead to higher group incomes than the standard PGG. Excessive intervention may reduce cooperation. The effect of intervention is distinct from peer punishment, as the cost is shared by all contributors and there is no direct punisher. The findings are based on simulation results and analysis of average payoffs and cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in spatial PGGs, the introduction of an institutional intervention mechanism (analogous to centralized punishment) can increase group efficiency, but only under certain parameter regimes: moderate intervention degree/intensity and high synergy factor (mpcr). The effect is non-monotonic: weak or excessive intervention can reduce efficiency. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of punishment-like interventions on efficiency, one should consider the strength and cost of the intervention and the underlying mpcr. The results are most applicable to settings with spatial structure, no peer punishment, and institutional (not individual) enforcement.

- source: Dong, Y. L., Gavrilets, S., Qin, C. Z., & Zhang, B. Y. (2024). Kinship can hinder cooperation in heterogeneous populations. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods and multi-player PD games with heterogeneous cost-benefit ratios, increasing genetic relatedness does not always increase cooperation or efficiency. For low relatedness, direct reciprocity (punishment via GRIM) can sustain cooperation and high efficiency. For high relatedness, cooperation is a dominant strategy and efficiency is high. However, for intermediate relatedness, some individuals become unconditional cooperators (AllC), which undermines the effectiveness of punishment and allows others to defect (AllD), making full cooperation and maximal efficiency unsustainable. This results in a non-monotonic, U-shaped relationship between relatedness and efficiency. The findings are supported by both subgame perfect equilibrium analysis and agent-based evolutionary simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in repeated public goods games with heterogeneity in cost-benefit ratios, the effect of mechanisms analogous to punishment (i.e., direct reciprocity via GRIM) on efficiency depends critically on the population structure and relatedness. When relatedness is low, punishment can sustain high efficiency; when relatedness is high, cooperation is self-sustaining. However, at intermediate relatedness, the threat of punishment is undermined, and efficiency drops. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of heterogeneity and the degree of relatedness (or analogous parameters) can cause non-monotonic effects of punishment on efficiency, and that punishment is most effective at low relatedness. The results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated PGG-like games with heterogeneity, but less so for one-shot games or games with explicit costly punishment mechanisms.

- source: Kristensen, N. P., Chisholm, R. A., & Ohtsuki, H. (2025). Many-strategy games in groups with relatives and the evolution of coordinated cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that kin selection (homophily) can facilitate the initial invasion of coordinated cooperation in public goods games with nonlinear (sigmoidal) benefits, even when such cooperation cannot invade in a well-mixed population due to cognitive or coordination costs. Once established, coordinated cooperation can persist as homophily declines, provided that the benefit function is sufficiently nonlinear and the switching gain from non-contribution to contribution at the coordination point exceeds the cost. If this condition is not met, some level of homophily must be maintained to prevent the invasion of defectors or 'liars' (who commit to contribute but do not follow through). The framework provides analytical and numerical tools for predicting evolutionary outcomes (including efficiency and group payoff) as a function of group size, relatedness, benefit function nonlinearity, and strategy set. The results are directly relevant to predicting efficiency in PGGs with kin structure and nonlinear payoffs, but do not address punishment or sanctions directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a general theoretical framework for predicting efficiency and group payoff in public goods games with arbitrary group size, number of strategies, and non-additive (e.g., sigmoidal) benefit functions, under varying levels of kin structure (homophily). It is directly relevant for predicting the efficiency of control games (without punishment) as a function of game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, MPCR (via the benefit function), and group structure. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it analyze the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The framework could, in principle, be extended to include punishment as a discrete strategy, but this is not done in the present paper. Therefore, the paper is highly informative for predicting baseline efficiency in PGGs with kin structure and nonlinear payoffs, but provides only indirect or contextual support for predicting the effect of punishment.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Zhang, J. L., & Weissing, F. J. (2014). The role of emotions in the maintenance of cooperative behaviors. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a stress-based cost for defectors in a public goods game can make cooperation evolutionarily stable if the stress intensity exceeds a calculable threshold. This threshold is lower for larger populations and higher public goods multipliers, and higher for larger group sizes. The model provides explicit formulas for the fixation probability of cooperation and the conditions under which cooperation can invade and dominate a population of defectors. The findings are directly relevant to efficiency, as they show under what conditions the group can achieve higher payoffs through sustained cooperation, even in the absence of explicit external punishment mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that internalized costs for defection (modeled as stress or moral emotions) can substitute for explicit punishment in promoting cooperation and group efficiency in public goods games. While it does not study explicit punishment, it shows that any mechanism that imposes a cost on defection (whether internal or external) can shift the evolutionary dynamics toward cooperation, provided the cost is above a threshold determined by game parameters (group size, population size, and MPCR). For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of any effective cost for defection—whether via punishment or internalized emotion—can increase efficiency, and that the required cost decreases with larger populations and higher MPCR. However, the model does not address explicit punishment technology, cost, or identification, so its direct applicability to those dimensions is limited.

- source: Khatun, K., Shen, C., & Tanimoto, J. (2025). Optional participation only provides a narrow scope for sustaining cooperation. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with optional participation, cooperation can only be sustained (and thus group efficiency improved) when nonparticipants have positive outside payoffs and do not harm the public good. The classic 'loner' model is a special case of this. If nonparticipants harm the public good or have negative outside payoffs, cooperation collapses and efficiency is low. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and thus does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings highlight the limitations of bottom-up, voluntary participation schemes for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. However, it is highly informative about the baseline efficiency and cooperation levels in optional participation (loner) variants of the public goods game, which are sometimes considered as alternatives to punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper can inform the expected efficiency in control conditions where only optional participation is available, but not the effect of enabling punishment. It also clarifies that optional participation alone only supports cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) under narrow conditions.

- source: Dong, Y. T., He, Z. X., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Constructive agents nullify the ability of destructive agents to foster cooperation in public goods games. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game, destructive agents (who harm others at a cost to themselves) can induce cyclic dominance among strategies (cooperation, defection, destruction), which can sustain cooperation and thus increase group efficiency relative to the baseline. However, when constructive agents (who benefit others at a cost to themselves) are introduced, they do not by themselves promote cooperation or efficiency. If both types are present, constructive agents can nullify the ability of destructive agents to sustain cooperation if their benefit exceeds the harm from destructive agents. The efficiency outcome thus depends on the relative magnitude of constructive and destructive effects. The paper provides explicit replicator dynamics and invasion analyses to support these findings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with additional positive or negative externalities (constructive or destructive agents), but only indirectly relevant for classic punishment-based games. The 'destructive agent' is adjacent to punishment: it is a non-participant who harms others, but not a targeted, strategic punishment mechanism. The efficiency effects of destructive agents are positive (via cyclic dominance) when their harm is moderate, but are nullified if constructive agents' benefits are larger. The findings suggest that the presence and magnitude of positive and negative externalities can strongly moderate the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms. However, because the model is theoretical and does not include classic peer punishment (where players pay to reduce the payoff of specific others in response to their actions), direct quantitative predictions for standard punishment treatments should be made with caution. The results are most informative for settings where punishment is implemented as a non-strategic, non-targeted externality.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Quan, J., Ma, S. T., & Zhao, J. H. (2025). Evolutionary dynamics of spatial public goods games with insurance mechanisms. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that insurance mechanisms in spatial PGGs can both promote and inhibit cooperation and group efficiency, depending on the synergy coefficient, insurance premium, coverage, and the structure of the population. At low synergy coefficients, insurance (especially when purchased by defectors) can sustain cooperation through self-organized cyclic states, increasing group efficiency relative to baseline defection. However, at high synergy coefficients or when insured cooperators are prevalent, insurance can hinder cooperation due to indirect territorial competition. When insurers are allowed to adapt their strategies (two-layer network), the cooperation-promoting effect of insurance is often reduced, as insurers may refuse to transact with defectors, limiting the spread of insurance and altering the evolutionary dynamics. The model provides detailed phase diagrams mapping these effects, with efficiency interpreted as the prevalence of cooperative strategies and the resulting group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence on how insurance-like risk management mechanisms (adjacent to punishment) affect efficiency in spatial PGGs. While not a direct study of punishment, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in games with sanctioning or risk-mitigation features. The results suggest that the effect of such mechanisms on efficiency is highly sensitive to game parameters (e.g., synergy coefficient, insurance cost/benefit, network structure) and the ability of third parties (insurers) to adapt. For prediction tasks, the paper highlights the importance of considering not just the presence of a sanctioning or insurance mechanism, but also who can access it, its cost-effectiveness, and the possibility of coevolution with external agents. The evidence is most directly applicable to spatial, structured populations with risk-mitigation options, and less so to standard punishment in classic PGGs.

- source: Wei, M., Wang, X., Liu, L. Z., Zheng, H. W., Jiang, Y. S., Hao, Y. J., Zheng, Z. M., Fu, F., & Tang, S. T. (2025). Indirect reciprocity in the public goods game with collective reputations. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with collective reputation and indirect reciprocity, all of the leading-eight social norm strategies can sustain cooperation in social dilemmas when the group assessment criterion (social strictness) is set at a moderate level. Too relaxed or too strict assessment criteria undermine cooperation. The evolutionary success of different strategies and assessment criteria depends on the payoff environment: stricter criteria are favored in low-payoff (high-dilemma) environments, while relaxed criteria are favored as payoffs increase. The model shows that collective reputation can mitigate social dilemmas and that the interplay between game structure and reputation structure is crucial for the evolution of cooperation. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task by showing that, in public goods games with indirect reciprocity and collective reputation (but without explicit punishment), cooperation and expected payoffs are maximized at moderate levels of social strictness. The findings suggest that the structure of reputation assessment (a design dimension not in the standard list) can play a similar role to punishment in sustaining cooperation, but the absence of explicit punishment means the results cannot be directly transferred to predict the effect of enabling punishment. The paper is most useful for understanding how non-punitive, reputation-based mechanisms affect cooperation and payoffs, and for contextualizing the importance of social norm design. It does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2024). Evolutionary Dynamics of Preguidance Strategies in Population Games. *Ieee Transactions on Computational Social Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper introduces and analyzes two forms of preemptive guidance strategies (peer and pool guidance) in a one-shot public goods game with a pregame negotiation phase. Both forms of guidance can reduce the prevalence of defectors and second-order free riders (cooperators who do not guide). Pool guidance is more effective than peer guidance, especially at higher guidance costs. The main reported outcomes are the steady-state frequencies of each strategy, not group efficiency or payoff. The model does not include standard punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on group payoff or efficiency as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation evidence that preemptive guidance strategies can reduce free riding and increase the prevalence of guidance strategies in PGGs, especially when guidance costs are low. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in PGG-like environments. The guidance strategies studied are adjacent to, but not the same as, standard punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. The findings may be useful for understanding the dynamics of pre-commitment or preemptive intervention, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency outcomes from punishment in standard PGGs.

- source: Battu, B. (2023). Co-evolution of conditional cooperation and social norm. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model finds that high initial cooperation and low contribution costs favor the emergence and stability of high cooperation and moderate norm sensitivity. When contribution costs are high or initial cooperation is low, cooperation fails to establish. The noise parameter (imperfect information about past cooperation) can modulate but not destroy cooperation unless it is very high. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward actions; instead, norm enforcement is internalized as psychic costs for free-riding, which depend on the number of cooperators, the cost of contribution, and individual norm sensitivity. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are not directly translatable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how internalized norm enforcement (psychic costs) and conditional cooperation can sustain cooperation in repeated PGGs, especially under low contribution costs and high initial cooperation. However, it does not model or report the effects of explicit punishment mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of initial conditions, contribution cost, and norm sensitivity for the emergence of cooperation, but not for quantifying efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Hu, D., Liu, J. Z., Liu, C., & Chu, C. (2025). Dynamic incentives and environmental feedback in public goods games: Promoting cooperation through critical thresholds. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing dynamic, group-level environmental feedback (bonuses or penalties based on collective contribution thresholds) in a spatial public goods game strongly promotes cooperation, especially when the synergy factor is low. The effect is not due to individual punishment or reward, but to a group-level adjustment of payoffs. The main reported outcomes are cooperation rates and the distribution of strategies and incentives. The model shows that higher expectation thresholds for group contributions can, counterintuitively, make cooperation more likely, and that the system transitions from defection to cooperation as the dynamic incentive increases. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment mechanism. Instead, it models a group-level dynamic incentive (environmental feedback) that is not targeted at individuals and is not costly to assign. The findings may be indirectly relevant for understanding how group-level feedback mechanisms (distinct from punishment) can promote cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled versus disabled. The results are most relevant for adjacent mechanisms (dynamic group-level incentives) rather than direct punishment or sanctions.

- source: Sun, X. P., Zhu, H. R., Kang, H. W., Bi, Y. Z., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2025). The impact of memory reputation-induced tax and reward allocation on spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing a reputation-driven mechanism for adjusting local synergy factors in a spatial public goods game can significantly promote cooperation. The effect is strongest when the maximum possible synergy factor is high, the minimum is low, the adjustment step size is small, and the reputation threshold is moderate. Both excessively high and low reputation thresholds hinder cooperation. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward actions; instead, reputation indirectly affects payoffs by modulating the local multiplier. The main reported outcome is the proportion of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of explicit punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment mechanism. However, it is relevant for understanding how endogenous, reputation-driven changes to the payoff structure (via the synergy factor) can affect cooperation rates. The findings suggest that indirect, reputation-based incentives can substitute for or complement explicit punishment/reward mechanisms in promoting cooperation, but the results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides only adjacent, indirect evidence.

- source: Morsky, B., & Akçay, E. (2021). False beliefs can bootstrap cooperative communities through social norms. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that inaccurate, overly optimistic beliefs about the level of cooperation can bootstrap and sustain high cooperation in communities governed by conditional cooperation norms, even in anonymous settings. The model shows that the dynamics of joining, learning, and leaving the community can create bistability, abrupt transitions, or cycles in cooperation rates and community size. High cooperation can be achieved and maintained if naive (optimistic) individuals are present in sufficient numbers, and if learning the true (lower) level of cooperation is slow or if disillusioned individuals can easily leave. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward, but discusses how mechanisms that increase transparency or allow for punishment may interact with these dynamics. The findings are relevant for understanding how social norms and belief management can foster cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how belief management and social norm dynamics can affect cooperation rates in public-goods-game-like environments, especially in the absence of explicit punishment or reward. It suggests that mechanisms which slow learning about true cooperation levels or allow for easy exit can paradoxically increase cooperation by maintaining a pool of optimistic, conditionally cooperative individuals. However, because the model does not include punishment, reward, or explicit efficiency/payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The discussion of punishment is conceptual and highlights possible trade-offs between transparency (which may reduce cooperation by revealing low compliance) and incentive mechanisms (which may increase or decrease cooperation depending on beliefs). For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding the role of belief dynamics and social norms as moderators, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.
