  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that persistent cooperation mechanisms in continuous public goods games can substantially increase the average cooperation level, especially when agents participate in multiple groups and allocate investments preferentially to more cooperative groups. Multi-group games outperform single-group games, and non-even investment with preference yields the highest cooperation levels. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff directly, but higher average cooperation levels are interpreted as beneficial for the group. The mechanisms are presented as alternatives to punishment and reward, which are not modeled or compared directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctions as a treatment or variable. Instead, it analyzes alternative mechanisms (persistent cooperation) that promote cooperation without punishment. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how non-punitive mechanisms can enhance cooperation, but they do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, except as a conceptual contrast to punishment-based interventions.

- source: Morison, C., Fic, M., Marcou, T., Mohamadichamgavi, J., Antón, J., Sayyar, G., Stein, A., Bastian, F., Krakovská, H., Krishnan, N., Pires, D. L., Satouri, M., Thomsen, F. J., Tjikundi, K., & Ali, W. (2025). Public Goods Games in Disease Evolution and Spread. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical and modeling work on PGGs in disease and cancer, highlighting how cooperation can be sustained or undermined depending on game parameters, network structure, and the presence of mechanisms like social norms or sanctions. It reviews how public health interventions (e.g., vaccination, mask mandates) can be conceptualized as public goods dilemmas, and how individual decision-making, social structure, and perceived costs/benefits shape compliance. In cancer, it discusses how cooperation among cancer cells can be modeled as a PGG, with implications for treatment strategies. However, the paper does not provide quantitative findings on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze the effects of punishment or sanctions on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual background on how PGGs are used to model cooperation in health-related contexts and discusses mechanisms (including sanctions and incentives) that can promote cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative results on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in PGGs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper may inform the qualitative understanding of how design dimensions (e.g., network structure, cost-benefit ratios, threshold effects) can influence cooperation, but it does not supply data or direct evidence to calibrate predictions of efficiency with or without punishment. Its discussion of sanctions is high-level and not tied to specific game parameters or outcomes.

- source: Muñoz-Herrera, M., & Nikiforakis, N. (2019). James Andreoni and the quest for others in our utility functions. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper reviews the development of experimental economics in the context of public goods and prosocial behavior, highlighting Andreoni's work on impure altruism, crowding out, and the rationality of giving. It discusses how early experiments showed voluntary contributions to public goods, the decline of contributions over repeated rounds, and the theoretical and empirical challenges to the 'free rider' hypothesis. The review emphasizes the importance of motives (e.g., warm-glow giving) and the incomplete crowding out of voluntary contributions by government intervention. It also highlights the rationality of prosocial choices as demonstrated by Andreoni and Miller (2002). However, the paper does not analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency effects in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence or data for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is useful for understanding the historical context and theoretical motivations behind public goods experiments, especially regarding voluntary contributions and prosocial motives. However, it does not analyze or report on punishment mechanisms, their design dimensions, or their effects on efficiency or payoff outcomes. For the downstream prediction task, this paper offers only background context and no actionable evidence.

- source: Kang, H. W., Jin, Z. Y., Li, M. Y., Wang, M., Sun, X. P., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2024). Discreet investors exert greater influence on cooperation in the public goods game. *Chinese Physics B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The introduction of discreet investors—who pay a cost to obtain information about their neighbors' cooperation and adjust their investments accordingly—leads to higher average payoffs and more robust cooperation compared to standard cooperators, especially at low synergy factors (r). As the cost of information (C_DI) increases, the advantage of discreet investors diminishes, and at high synergy factors, standard cooperators can survive and dominate. The model does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main outcome is average payoff, which is a direct analog to efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it does provide evidence that mechanisms allowing players to condition their contributions on local information (at a cost) can increase average payoffs and cooperation, especially when the cost is low. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant because it does not manipulate or analyze punishment. It may be useful as a conceptual analog for the effects of costly information acquisition or heterogeneity in investment, but not for punishment effects.

- source: Wang, S., Xu, Z. J., & Zhang, L. Z. (2018). Non-Fixed Investment in Voluntary Public Goods Games. *Journal of the Korean Physical Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that allowing non-fixed investment in voluntary PGGs leads to complex rock-scissors-paper dynamics among cooperators, defectors, and loners. Increasing the maximum possible investment ratio can initially promote cooperation and increase average payoff, but further increases may reduce cooperation due to increased temptation to defect. Higher interest rates (MPCR) and lower loner payoffs promote both cooperation and higher average payoffs. The memory length (sensitivity to past payoffs) also moderates outcomes: moderate memory promotes higher average payoffs and cooperation, while very short or very long memory is detrimental. The system always converges to a stable equilibrium regardless of initial conditions. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present or discussed as interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGG-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the model or analysis. It is therefore not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide detailed evidence on how other design dimensions (investment flexibility, MPCR, loner payoff, memory) affect efficiency and cooperation in voluntary PGGs without punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not relevant.

- source: Park, C. (2022). Network Characteristic Control of Social Dilemmas in a Public Good Game: Numerical Simulation of Agent-Based Nonlinear Dynamics. *Processes*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary PGGs, the average payoff and the prevalence of cooperation depend strongly on the participation cost, the incentive (interest rate), mutation rate, and the structure of the social network (network density, eigenvector centrality). High participation costs or low incentives lead to nonparticipation and lower average payoffs. High network density can mediate the effects of mutation, sometimes stabilizing cooperation. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, so it does not address the effects of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs or related environments. It is useful for understanding how network structure, mutation, and incentive parameters affect efficiency and cooperation in PGGs without punishment. It cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is modeled or analyzed.

- source: Wang, J. W., Yu, F. Y., He, J. L., Chen, W., Xu, W. S., Dai, W. H., & Ming, Y. X. (2023). Promotion, Disintegration and Remediation of group cooperation under heterogeneous distribution system based on peer rating. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: Redistribution rules that reward willingness to cooperate (high alpha) and are neutral with respect to initial endowment (beta near zero) lead to the highest group efficiency (average payoff) and lowest inequality. Redistribution that favors the poor (negative beta) or the rich (positive beta) increases inequality and does not maximize group payoff. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning; all effects are due to redistribution of payoffs after the public goods game. The findings challenge the assumption that subsidizing the poor in such games always promotes cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it is highly informative about how redistribution mechanisms (distinct from punishment) affect efficiency and cooperation in heterogeneous populations. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it does provide a useful contrast: redistribution based on willingness to cooperate can increase efficiency, while redistribution based on endowment does not. The game design dimensions are well specified for redistribution, but not for punishment.

- source: Chessa, M., & Loiseau, P. (2024). Enhancing voluntary contributions in a public goods economy via a minimum individual contribution level. *Public Choice*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that introducing a minimum individual contribution level (while still allowing for full free riding) in a voluntary public goods game can strictly increase the total contribution and social welfare at equilibrium, provided the minimum is not set too high. The analysis covers both heterogeneous and homogeneous cost cases, and characterizes the equilibrium structure as a function of the minimum. The results show that there is an optimal range for the minimum contribution: if set just above the lowest equilibrium contribution in the baseline game, it increases total contributions without causing contributors to drop out. If set too high, some contributors become free riders and total contributions can decrease. The paper provides operational guidance for setting the minimum but does not address punishment, reward, or other sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not study punishment or sanctions. However, it is highly informative about the effects of restricting the contribution set by imposing a minimum, which is a different intervention. The findings may be useful for understanding how changes to the action space (such as minimums or defaults) affect efficiency, but cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Zhao, L., Ye, X. J., Huang, Z. G., Sun, J. T., Yang, L., Do, Y., & Wang, Y. H. (2010). Promotion of cooperation in the form C0C1D classified by 'degree grads' in a scale-free network. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in scale-free networks, cooperation can persist even when the PGG enhancement factor r < 1, due to a mechanism where high-degree ('hub') cooperators (C0) are imitated by their neighbors (C1), who may receive negative payoffs but still support the hub. The average payoff of agents is analyzed as a function of their network position (degree grads), showing that only the hubs (C0) receive positive payoffs, while most other cooperators (C1) have negative payoffs. The system-level average payoff increases with r, but the persistence of cooperation at low r is due to network structure and imitation, not payoff incentives. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is useful for understanding how network structure and imitation can sustain cooperation and affect payoff distribution, but it does not inform predictions about the impact of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task (treatment efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is not directly relevant, as punishment is not present or discussed as a mechanism.

- source: Windmann, S., Binder, L., Bender, C., Langenbucher, K., Vollmeyer, R., & Schultze, M. (2024). Facets of altruism in study majors and volunteering groups. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study demonstrates that allowing heterogeneous investment and dynamic feedback on the multiplication factor in spatial public goods games can substantially increase both cooperation and group-level success (measured by average payoff and group success). High feedback strength and moderate group-preference/half-success effort are optimal for group success and individual welfare. The model recovers the standard spatial PGG when feedback is disabled. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are included or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for understanding how dynamic feedback and heterogeneous investment affect efficiency and payoff in public goods games, but it does not inform the effect of punishment or sanctions. Its findings may be useful for predicting efficiency in PGGs with dynamic returns, but not for the specific prediction task of punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency. It provides no evidence on punishment, so should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects.

- source: Li, X. Y., Chen, T., Chen, Q., & Zhang, X. Y. (2020). The impact of retention time of donation list on cooperation in public goods game. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that publishing a donation list with a moderate retention time and listing a moderate proportion of players can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators and the total sum of contributions in a public goods game. Instant or very frequent list updates are less effective than lists with longer retention times (but not excessively long). Listing too many or too few players is also suboptimal; an intermediate proportion maximizes contributions. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the sum of contributions is used as a proxy for public goods provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it does provide informative evidence about the effects of reputation-based mechanisms (public listing, retention time, and partial disclosure) on cooperation and total contributions. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding the effects of non-punitive social incentives. The sum of contributions (S_c) is a close but not exact proxy for efficiency.

  findings: The study finds that in public goods games simulated with deep Q-learning agents, the rate of cooperation increases as group size increases, and also as MPCR increases. However, the cooperation rate plateaus at around 50% for large group sizes or high MPCR. The model does not include punishment, reward, or communication mechanisms. The outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff. The results are consistent with some human experiments, but the model is limited to binary (all-or-nothing) contribution choices and does not address payoff-based efficiency directly.

For the downstream prediction task, this paper may help estimate control (no-punishment) efficiency in all-or-nothing, no-punishment, no-reward, no-chat settings, but provides no evidence on the effect of punishment or moderators of punishment's effect.

- source: Yang, Z. Q. L., Li, C. C., Wang, X., & Tian, Y. L. (2025). PPO-ACT: Proximal policy optimization with adversarial curriculum transfer for spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.

  findings: The PPO-ACT algorithm, which combines proximal policy optimization with adversarial curriculum transfer, enables higher and more robust cooperation rates in spatial public goods games compared to standard PPO, Q-learning, and Fermi update rules. PPO-ACT achieves stable all-cooperator states at lower enhancement factors (r) than other algorithms, and is robust to challenging initial conditions (e.g., all-defector starts). The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; no explicit analysis of group efficiency or total payoff is provided. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.

Its findings are relevant for understanding how advanced learning algorithms affect cooperation rates in spatial PGGs, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The design dimensions and results may be useful for modeling baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment, but not for the downstream task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Shi, Z. Y., Wei, W., Zheng, H. W., & Zheng, Z. M. (2023). Bidirectional supervision: An effective method to suppress corruption and defection under the third party punishment mechanism of donation games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when the weight of reputation in the strategy update rule is moderate, expanding the influence range of high-reputation individuals promotes cooperation (higher fraction of cooperators). When the reputation weight is low, expanding the influence range actually reduces cooperation. The effect of expanding influence range has diminishing returns (marginal effect). The model is a spatial PGG on a square lattice, with no punishment or reward mechanisms. Payoff is only discussed for subgroups and not as a main outcome. The findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are about the effect of reputation-based influence range on cooperation rates in a spatial PGG. While it provides insight into how information and influence structures can affect cooperation, it is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Wang, S., Li, Z., Li, W. D., & Jiang, H. (2022). Continuous Spatial Public Goods Game Based on Particle Swarm Optimization with Memory Stability. *Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in a continuous spatial PGG with PSO-based learning, the memory stability coefficient (α) has a complex effect on the average cooperation level. At low enhancement factors (r), high memory stability (large α) helps maintain a moderate level of cooperation, while reducing memory stability (lower α) leads to a collapse of cooperation. At high r, reducing memory stability (lower α) allows the system to forget low-investment strategies and promotes the formation of high-cooperation clusters, increasing the average cooperation level. The effect of memory stability is more pronounced when players rely more on their own history (high ω) than on neighbor imitation. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are included in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present. The findings are relevant for understanding how learning rules and memory affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The results may be indirectly useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in spatial, continuous PGGs with PSO-type learning, but cannot inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Shi, D. M., & Wang, B. H. (2017). Critical mass of public goods and its coevolution with cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation is maximized at an intermediate value of the critical mass threshold (cm), with both too low and too high thresholds leading to lower cooperation. The nature of the public good (consumable vs. reusable) and the depreciation rate parameter (Gamma) affect the optimal threshold and the dynamics of cooperation. The results are robust across different network structures. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or any sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings about critical mass and cooperation density may be contextually informative for understanding cooperation dynamics, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Kang, H. W., Jiang, C., Shen, Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2025). Neighbor-aware reinforcement learning fosters cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that integrating neighbor-aware reinforcement learning (NI) and reputation mechanisms in a spatial public goods game model can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators and promote the formation of stable cooperator clusters. The effect is especially strong when agents can observe a wider neighborhood (larger perceptual range). Reputation alone can promote cooperation if it is sufficiently weighted in the reward function, but the combination of NI and reputation produces a synergistic effect. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms in the sense of costly peer or institutional sanctions/rewards. The main dependent variable is the cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how social learning, reputation, and local information can promote cooperation, but not for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment. The model and results may be useful for understanding moderators of cooperation in spatial and learning-based PGGs, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Stock, E. V., Valverde, P., González-Avella, J. C., Iglesias, J. R., Gonçalves, S., & da Silva, R. (2025). Cyclical oscillations and absorbing-state probabilities in optional public goods games: Interplay of reward and group size. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the OPGG exhibits rich dynamical behavior, including cyclic dominance among strategies and multistability in absorbing states, depending on the multiplication factor (r) and group size (S). For r < 2, loners dominate; for r > S, cooperators dominate; and for 2 < r < S, the system can end in any of the three absorbing states, with the likelihood and duration of cycles depending on S and r. Larger group sizes and higher r values tend to favor cooperation, but the model does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide detailed evidence on how group size and the multiplication factor affect the stability and dynamics of cooperation, defection, and abstention in optional public goods games. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide useful context for understanding baseline dynamics in the absence of punishment.

- source: Xiao, Y., & Hua, D. Y. (2012). Promotion of Cooperation in a Spatial Public Goods Game with Long Range Learning and Mobility. *Chinese Physics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the evolution of cooperation in a spatial public goods game is strongly affected by the interplay of population density, learning range, and mobility range. When the learning range is larger than the mobility range, small local groups at low population density support high cooperation. At moderate densities, mobility allows cooperators to escape defector invasions, supporting stable cooperation. When the mobility range is larger than the learning range, high population density enables the formation of compact cooperator domains, which also promotes cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; no efficiency or group payoff results are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. It is relevant for understanding how spatial structure, mobility, and learning range affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based efficiency outcomes or the effect of punishment. Therefore, it should not be weighted heavily in predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in the context of punishment.

- source: Lv, S. J., Li, J. Y., & Zhao, C. H. (2025). Reinforcement learning in spatial public goods games with environmental feedbacks. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The study finds that both higher synergy coefficient (r) and stronger environmental feedback (alpha) promote higher levels of cooperation in the SPGG. The effect of the Q-learning discount factor (gamma) on cooperation is non-monotonic and depends on the values of r and alpha. When r or alpha is low, higher gamma (more future-oriented agents) promotes cooperation; when r and alpha are high, lower gamma (more myopic agents) is more favorable for cooperation. The results are based on agent-based simulations and focus on the fraction of cooperators and spatial clustering, not on payoff or efficiency outcomes. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant for understanding how environmental feedback and reinforcement learning affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based outcomes or for the effect of punishment. The game design dimensions covered (e.g., player count, mpcr, rounds, absence of punishment/reward) may be useful for contextualizing baseline cooperation in spatial PGGs with environmental feedback, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

However, it provides informative context on how game design parameters such as group size, threshold, and synergy factor affect cooperation rates in threshold public goods games. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in threshold PGGs without punishment.

  findings: The study finds that persistent imitation—where players stick to imitating a reference neighbor for multiple rounds—substantially increases the fraction of cooperators in a spatial public goods game, especially when the proportion of persistent imitators and the maximum imitation duration are both high. The effect is robust across synergy factors (r). However, the paper does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings on persistent imitation and cooperation may be contextually relevant for understanding mechanisms that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. It should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Wang, J. W., Chen, W., Yu, F. Y., Zhou, S. Y., He, J. L., Xu, W. S., & Dai, W. H. (2024). The emergence of cooperation in the context of prior agreement with threshold and posterior compensation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial public goods game where only the top V individuals by cumulative wealth can participate and must pay a participation cost, the effect of participation cost on cooperation depends on V. For small V, increasing participation cost can promote cooperation up to a point but increases wealth polarization. For large V, increasing participation cost slightly inhibits cooperation but reduces polarization. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff. However, it may provide indirect context for how participation costs and selection mechanisms affect cooperation and wealth distribution. It is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Wu, Z. H., Huang, H., & Liao, Q. H. (2021). The study on the role of dedicators on promoting cooperation in public goods game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The presence of dedicators in a PGG model can strongly promote and stabilize cooperation, leading to higher total contributions. Dedicators' willingness to donate is more important than the amount they donate. Emotional effects—whereby others are inspired to cooperate by dedicator actions—further enhance cooperation. The system is robust to several parameter changes, but a too-high budget requirement can collapse cooperation. No punishment or sanctioning is modeled or analyzed. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in PGG-like environments, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome. It is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide indirect evidence that mechanisms other than punishment (such as the presence of prosocial 'dedicators' and emotional contagion) can promote cooperation and higher contributions. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency outcomes in the required sense.

- source: Yan, S. Q. (2017). The evolution of human mobility based on the public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that human migration (mobility) can promote cooperative behavior and lead to a more balanced population distribution across regions. Increasing the migration rate leads to greater population balance. Higher cooperation costs reduce the number of cooperators. The model is based on a public goods game, but does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff. The outcomes are primarily the density of cooperators and the distribution of population across regions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. It may provide some indirect context for how migration and cooperation costs affect cooperation rates, but is not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Alfaro, G., & Sanjuan, M. A. F. (2022). Time-dependent effects hinder cooperation on the public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game model, moderate levels of habitat destruction increase the fraction of cooperators, while high levels cause cooperation to collapse. The multiplication factor (r) increases cooperation monotonically. The model does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators in the population.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff. It may provide some indirect context about how environmental stressors (like habitat destruction) affect cooperation rates, but it is not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Lu, Z. Y., Hua, S. J., Wang, L. C., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Hybrid reward-punishment in feedback-evolving game for common resource governance. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a feedback-evolving common resource game (a close variant of the public goods game), the introduction of punishment (or a hybrid of reward and punishment) by a third-party institution significantly increases the likelihood of achieving both full cooperation and sustainable resource levels, which are direct analogs to high efficiency. Pure reward is less effective than punishment or hybrid schemes, especially when the resource growth rate is moderate. Increasing the per capita incentive (tax) and allocating more of the incentive budget to punishment (lower w) both make the ideal outcome (full cooperation and resource sustainability) more likely. When the resource growth rate is too low, no incentive scheme is effective. The results are robust across a range of parameter values and are supported by both analytical and numerical phase diagrams.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in environments where resource renewal is not too slow, adding punishment (or a hybrid of reward and punishment) increases the likelihood of achieving high efficiency (full cooperation and resource sustainability) compared to reward alone or no incentives. The effect is stronger when the per capita incentive is higher and when more of the incentive budget is allocated to punishment. The model is deterministic and infinite-population, but the qualitative insights about the superiority of punishment (or hybrid) over pure reward for efficiency are likely to generalize to similar institutional public goods settings. The paper does not address stochasticity, finite groups, or experimental data, but its results are directly relevant for theoretical prediction.

- source: Garrido, C. M., Santos, F. C., Domingos, E. F., Nunes, A. M., & Pacheco, J. M. (2025). A new electoral bottom-up model of institutional governance. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a collective risk dilemma (CRD) modeled as a threshold public goods game, the introduction of local, bottom-up institutions—created when a minimum number of group members contribute to their formation, and whose nature (reward or punishment) is decided by majority vote—substantially increases group efficiency (η_G) compared to no institution, reward-only, or punishment-only institutions. Reward-only institutions are generally more efficient than punishment-only, but the electoral (mixed) institution is superior to both, especially at lower risk levels. The model shows that the critical risk threshold for achieving a given level of group efficiency is lower under the electoral institution, and the transition from defection to cooperation is steeper. The model is theoretical and does not use empirical or experimental data, but provides direct predictions for how institutional design affects efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in threshold public goods games with risk of group failure, the presence and design of institutional punishment and/or reward mechanisms have large effects on group efficiency. Specifically, enabling local, flexible institutions that can implement either punishment or reward (as decided by group vote) leads to higher efficiency than fixed punishment-only or reward-only institutions. The model quantifies these effects using the η_G metric, which is directly analogous to efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence of a flexible, locally decided institution (with both punishment and reward possible) should be expected to yield higher efficiency than either punishment-only or reward-only, and much higher than no institution. The model also provides parameterizations for group size, number of rounds, risk, and institutional costs, which can inform predictions when these dimensions are known.

- source: Gao, S. P., & Liang, J. L. (2020). The effects of resource limitation and consensual incentives on cooperation. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a nonlinear multiplayer public goods game with a threshold and resource limitation, the introduction of consensual incentives (punishment or reward, decided collectively) can promote cooperation and increase group payoff (efficiency), provided that the population's preference for incentives is sufficiently high. Without incentives, the system always evolves to full defection (zero efficiency). The effect of incentives is not monotonic in resource abundance: there is an intermediate resource level where the positive effect of incentives is minimized, and both scarcity and abundance of resources can promote cooperation when incentives are present. The model also shows that a lower consensus threshold for employing incentives and a higher preference for incentives both increase the basin of attraction for full cooperation. Consensual reward can be more effective than punishment in promoting cooperation when preferences for incentives are high.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment (or reward) in a nonlinear, threshold-based public goods game can shift the system from full defection (zero efficiency) to full cooperation (high efficiency), provided that the population's preference for incentives is above a critical value. The effect is sensitive to the consensus threshold for employing incentives, the abundance of resources, and the type of incentive. For prediction, the presence of a punishment mechanism (especially if it is collectively decided and the population is willing to use it) is likely to increase efficiency dramatically compared to the control (no punishment) case. However, the effect may be minimized at intermediate resource levels. The model is analytic and does not provide empirical effect sizes, but the qualitative direction is clear: enabling punishment (or reward) increases efficiency in this class of games.

- source: Vinayak, R. K. (2025). FARMER'S GAME: A GAME THEORETIC APPROACH TOWARDS BURNING OF CROP RESIDUE. *Journal of Dynamics and Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that, without intervention, individual incentives lead to inefficient outcomes (universal burning of crop residue), analogous to the tragedy of the commons. Social pressure can sometimes induce cooperation, but is often not strong enough. The introduction of government punishment (penalty per burned plot) and/or reward (per non-burned plot) can shift the Nash equilibrium to full cooperation (no burning), maximizing group payoff and efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions for when this shift occurs: when the sum of penalty and reward per plot exceeds the net private benefit of burning minus the greener alternative and pollution cost. Simulations confirm these theoretical results, showing that moderate levels of punishment and reward are sufficient to achieve efficient, cooperative outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that introducing punishment (and/or reward) in a public-goods-like environment with negative externalities can shift the equilibrium from inefficient (all defect) to efficient (all cooperate), provided the policy parameters are set so that the penalty plus reward per unit exceeds the net private benefit of defection. The model is directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency: if the punishment is sufficiently strong (relative to the private incentive to defect), efficiency will increase dramatically, potentially reaching full efficiency. The findings are most applicable to settings where punishment is implemented as a per-action cost, is known to all, and is not so severe as to cause practical or political infeasibility. The model does not address empirical implementation issues, but provides clear guidance on the direction and mechanism of efficiency change.

- source: Wang, L. C., Hua, S. J., Liu, Y. Y., Zhang, L., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Threshold incentive mechanisms for the sustainable management of public resources. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that threshold-based punishment and reward mechanisms can effectively promote cooperation and sustainable management of public resources, leading to higher efficiency (resource sustainability and group payoff) at lower cost than continuous incentives. Punishment is only triggered when the number of cooperators exceeds a threshold, targeting defectors, while reward is triggered when cooperation is low or resources are depleted. These mechanisms can stabilize the system at higher resource levels and higher cooperation rates, but their effectiveness is limited if the resource's natural growth rate is too low or the initial resource stock is too small. The models also show that increasing population size can reduce equilibrium resource levels, and that the interplay of reward/punishment thresholds and strengths is important for optimal outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that threshold-triggered punishment (and reward) mechanisms in public-goods-like resource games can increase efficiency relative to no incentives, especially when the resource can regenerate and initial conditions are not too dire. The effect of punishment is more positive for efficiency when it is targeted (threshold-based) rather than universal, and when combined with reward mechanisms. The model's explicit tracking of average payoffs and resource levels makes its results directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in games with similar design dimensions. However, the results are theoretical and may not capture all real-world behavioral nuances.

- source: Wang, J. F., & Shen, A. Z. (2024). The synergy of elimination and zero-determinant strategy on dynamic games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that implementing endogenous reward and punishment policies in a regional public goods game increases both cooperation and total social welfare (W_t), especially when the intervention is targeted (partial intervention) rather than universal. The efficiency gains from punishment are largest when the cost of policy implementation is low or the punishment is strong. There is often an optimal fraction of groups to intervene in, beyond which additional intervention yields diminishing or negative returns due to policy costs. Size-based selection of groups for intervention is generally superior to random or degree-based selection. Allowing non-intervened groups to spontaneously adopt the policy can further boost cooperation and welfare, but only if the additional costs are offset by increased benefits. Terminating intervention leads to rapid collapse of cooperation and welfare, indicating the need for sustained policy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical/simulation evidence that enabling punishment (and reward) in a PGG-like environment increases efficiency (total social welfare), but the magnitude and even the sign of the effect depend on the cost of policy implementation, the strength of punishment, and the fraction of groups covered. For prediction, the model suggests that punishment will generally increase efficiency relative to control, especially when the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is not too low and policy costs are not too high. However, if policy costs are high or the environment is resource-poor (low mpcr), the efficiency gain may be small or even negative. The method of targeting intervention (e.g., size-based) can further moderate the effect. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG variants with spatial or regional structure and endogenous institutional punishment.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2024). Impact of dynamic compensation with resource feedback on the common pool resource game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that (1) static punishment (compensation) above a critical threshold leads to full cooperation, resource sustainability, and maximum group payoff; (2) dynamic punishment with resource feedback can promote cooperation and efficiency even when static punishment is insufficient, especially at low initial compensation intensity; (3) the relationship between punishment parameters and efficiency is non-monotonic and context-dependent, with oscillatory or convergent dynamics depending on parameter regions; (4) the proportion of fines used for ecological compensation (vs. other uses) should be tuned to maximize efficiency, with all fines used for compensation when feedback is high or initial intensity is high, and none when both are low.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that increasing punishment intensity (compensation) above a critical threshold sharply increases efficiency (group payoff) in common pool resource games. Dynamic, feedback-based punishment can further improve efficiency, especially when static punishment is weak. The results suggest that, for prediction, the presence and strength of punishment, as well as whether it is dynamically adjusted based on resource feedback, are key moderators of efficiency gains. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially for settings with resource feedback, variable punishment intensity, and allocation of fines to resource restoration.

- source: Cooney, D. B. (2025). Exploring the Evolution of Altruistic Punishment with a PDE Model of Cultural Multilevel Selection. *Bulletin of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that altruistic punishment, when combined with group-level selection, can promote higher levels of cooperation and increase group efficiency compared to scenarios without punishment. However, the effect of punishment on efficiency is sensitive to the cost structure (per-interaction vs. fixed), the strength of punishment, and the form of group-level competition. In some models, increasing punishment strength or cost leads to higher cooperation but lower efficiency, due to the collective cost of punishment outweighing its benefits. The paper provides analytical expressions for the threshold group selection strength needed to sustain cooperation and for the steady-state average payoff, showing that these can depend non-monotonically on punishment parameters. The results highlight that the design of punishment mechanisms and the structure of group competition are critical moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, mechanistic, and quantitative guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments with group structure. It shows that the efficiency impact of punishment depends on the cost and strength of punishment, the type of cost (per-interaction or fixed), and the form of group-level competition. For prediction, the results suggest that: (1) punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when costs are per-interaction rather than fixed; (2) increasing punishment strength generally increases cooperation, but may decrease efficiency if costs are high; (3) the effect of punishment on efficiency can be non-monotonic, especially under group-level competition rules that normalize by maximum/minimum group payoffs; (4) the threshold group selection strength required for cooperation is lower with stronger or cheaper punishment. The paper's analytical formulas and numerical results can be used to estimate treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design parameters, especially in settings with group-structured populations and multilevel selection.

- source: Bolle, F. (2021). Deterrence by Collective Punishment May Work against Criminals but Never against Freedom Fighters. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that collective punishment (CP) is ineffective or counterproductive in threshold public good games where resistance is individually costly but can produce a public good (e.g., protests, strikes). In these cases, increasing the threat or severity of CP does not increase compliance and may even increase resistance. In contrast, in threshold public bad games (e.g., pollution, crime), CP can sometimes increase compliance, but only when the individual benefit from misbehavior is small compared to the social cost. The paper provides formal equilibrium analysis and discusses equilibrium selection, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for predicting the behavioral effects of punishment in public-goods-like environments, especially regarding when punishment is likely to deter undesirable behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on when collective punishment is likely to be effective in public-goods-like games. It suggests that in threshold public good games (where cooperation is costly and produces a group benefit), punishment is unlikely to increase efficiency or compliance, and may even be counterproductive. In threshold public bad games (where defection is individually profitable but socially costly), punishment can increase compliance and, by implication, efficiency, but only when the individual incentive to defect is not too large. The paper does not provide quantitative predictions for efficiency, but its qualitative results can inform predictions about the likely direction of punishment effects given the structure of the game (public good vs. public bad, cost/benefit ratios). However, because the paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff directly, its support for efficiency prediction is indirect and should be supplemented with empirical or simulation evidence.

- source: Whitmeyer, J. M. (2004). The group control catastrophe. *Sociological Perspectives*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in a simulated environment where an external agent can sanction group members (individually or collectively) to induce compliance (a public good), increasing monitoring efficacy does not always monotonically increase compliance. Instead, for intermediate monitoring efficacy, compliance can collapse to zero, creating a bifurcation with two possible equilibria (full compliance or full defection), depending on initial conditions (e.g., the number of initial cooperators). The 'catastrophe' is robust across a range of group sizes, sanction levels, internal control efficacies, and production function shapes (except linear). The equilibrium with high compliance yields higher payoffs for group members than the low-compliance equilibrium. The cost to the agent of obtaining compliance can be minimized by using only one type of sanction (individual or collective) and by leveraging initial cooperation to reach the high-compliance equilibrium, especially at moderate monitoring efficacy. However, the model does not directly report efficiency in the sense of group payoff relative to the maximum possible.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment (sanctions) on group compliance and payoffs in public-goods-like environments is highly non-linear and can depend critically on monitoring efficacy, initial conditions, and the structure of sanctions. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency or group payoff: under some parameter regimes (especially intermediate monitoring efficacy), punishment can lead to catastrophic drops in compliance and thus lower payoffs. The model also highlights the importance of initial cooperation and the potential for multiple equilibria. However, because the paper does not directly report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff, and because it is a simulation/theory paper rather than an empirical study, its findings should be used to inform qualitative expectations about non-monotonic and path-dependent effects of punishment, rather than to provide quantitative predictions.

- source: Tang, T. Z., & Ye, H. (2016). The Blessing of Sexuality: Evolution of Altruism with Mating Preference. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The simulation results show that altruistic punishment alone does not sustain high levels of cooperation; defectors tend to dominate unless sexual attractiveness (mating preference for altruists) is present. When both punishment and sexual attractiveness are enabled, altruists (cooperators and punishers) become dominant, and the mean altruistic investment approaches its maximum. Variable population size and individual variation in investment further amplify the prevalence of altruism. The model is a close variant of a public goods game, but the main outcomes are population composition and mean investment, not efficiency or group payoff. Thus, the findings are most relevant for predicting the conditions under which punishment can sustain high cooperation, but less directly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment, when combined with additional incentives (such as sexual attractiveness or mating preference for altruists), can lead to high levels of cooperation in public-goods-like environments. However, in the absence of such additional incentives, punishment alone may not sustain cooperation. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes from punishment. Instead, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency may depend on the presence of complementary mechanisms that reward altruists. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment's positive effect on efficiency is conditional and may be weak or absent without additional incentives.

- source: Yan, R. Y., Xu, M. Q., Liu, L. J., & Hua, S. J. (2024). Evolution of cooperation under pure institutional reward and punishment in collective-risk social dilemma games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing institutional pure punishment or pure reward into a collective-risk social dilemma game can promote cooperation, but with important differences. Pure reward leads to stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, with the domain of coexistence expanding as reward intensity increases. Pure punishment produces more complex dynamics, including monostable, bistable, tristable, and quadristable outcomes, and can more effectively suppress free-riding when punishment intensity is high or the implementation threshold is low. In finite populations, lower thresholds for implementing incentives (reward or punishment) promote cooperation, while higher thresholds favor defection. The cost and intensity of incentives modulate these effects. However, the paper does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the evolutionary stability and prevalence of cooperative strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effect of institutional punishment on cooperation in collective-risk public goods games, especially regarding how punishment intensity, cost, and implementation threshold affect the prevalence of cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence on group efficiency or total payoff, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited to inferring that higher cooperation rates (under certain punishment regimes) are likely to be associated with higher efficiency, assuming the mapping from cooperation to efficiency is known. The results are most relevant for settings with institutional (not peer) punishment, nonlinear threshold public goods, and no direct cost of cooperation for punishers/rewarders. The findings suggest that lower punishment thresholds and higher punishment intensity (relative to cost) are more likely to promote cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency if cooperation is strongly linked to group payoff.

- source: Zhang, W., Zhao, D. K., Jin, X., Zhang, H. Z., An, T. B., Cui, G. H., & Wang, Z. (2025). Q-learning facilitates norm emergence in metanorm game model with topological structures. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a metanorm game with punishment and metapunishment, agents using self-regarding Q-learning can achieve high and robust cooperation rates (over 90% in many cases), even when agents lack information about others' payoffs. Punishment and metapunishment are essential for maintaining cooperation and suppressing defection and non-punishment. The model is robust to changes in network topology (scale-free, random, small-world), temptation payoff, population size, and observability. Highly connected agents are more likely to adopt punitive strategies. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this work.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment and metapunishment mechanisms can sustain high cooperation rates in social dilemma environments with local interactions and limited information, especially when agents use reinforcement learning. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The results are most useful for understanding the behavioral and dynamic effects of punishment and metapunishment, and for anticipating that high cooperation is possible under a wide range of design parameters, but not for quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Kido, Y., & Takezawa, M. (2024). Coevolution of norm psychology and cooperation through exapted conformity. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that cooperation can evolve in collective action dilemmas when injunctive norms strongly encourage punishment, but not when they only encourage cooperation. Conformity (descriptive norm psychology) alone can support intermediate cooperation, but is unstable and easily undermined. When conformity is exapted (pre-existing), it can expand the conditions under which punishment-based cooperation evolves, acting as a scaffold for the emergence of stable, high-cooperation societies. The model identifies two stable types of cooperative societies: those based on conformity (unstable, lower cooperation) and those based on punishment (stable, higher cooperation). However, all outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation and punishment rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the importance of punishment norms (and the cost/benefit structure of punishment) in enabling high cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially when conformity is present. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective than conformity at sustaining high cooperation, and that the presence of conformity can expand the parameter space where punishment-based cooperation emerges. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment mechanisms (especially when normatively encouraged) are likely to increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Qian, J., Sun, X., Zhang, T. D., & Chai, Y. T. (2022). Authority or Autonomy? Exploring Interactions between Central and Peer Punishments in Risk-Resistant Scenarios. *Entropy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that both central and peer punishment can promote group collaboration in risk-resistant (threshold) scenarios, with peer punishment generally having a stronger effect, especially as risk increases. Central punishment can suppress the effect of peer punishment at all risk levels, while peer punishment only constrains central punishment at high risk. Resource wastage from punishment is generally small compared to the loss from group failure, but increases with peer punishment intensity and risk. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff in the standard sense, focusing instead on collaboration rates and resource wastage.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It supports the idea that punishment (both central and peer) increases group collaboration in threshold public goods-like environments, and that the effectiveness and interaction of punishment types depend on risk level. However, because the main outcome is collaboration rate (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the model is theoretical/simulation-based rather than empirical, its direct predictive value for efficiency is limited. The resource wastage analysis suggests that high peer punishment can lead to diminishing returns or inefficiency at high risk. The model's all-or-nothing threshold structure and explicit manipulation of punishment cost and effectiveness are informative for mapping to game design dimensions.

- source: Chica, M., & Santos, F. C. (2023). Seeding leading cooperators and institutions in networked climate dilemmas. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in networked collective-risk dilemmas with local punishment institutions, seeding a small fraction of punishers (institution supporters) at random or in network hubs can shift the system from low to high group achievement and institution prevalence, even when initial conditions are otherwise unchanged. Seeding cooperators is much less effective. The presence of punishment institutions is necessary for high cooperation; network structure alone does not suffice. Seeding network hubs is more expensive but not more effective than random seeding. The main outcomes are group achievement and institution prevalence, not direct efficiency or payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing punishment institutions (via seeding punishers) in a networked public-goods-like environment can strongly increase group-level cooperation outcomes (group achievement and institution prevalence), even when the control (no punishment) condition yields low cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, its findings should be used as informative but indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The results suggest that, in similar networked games, enabling punishment (especially via institutional mechanisms) is likely to increase efficiency if group achievement is a good proxy for efficiency. The paper also highlights that the effectiveness of punishment depends on both network structure and the presence of institutional mechanisms, and that seeding punishers is more effective than seeding cooperators.

- source: Solferino, N., Solferino, V., & Taurino, S. F. (2018). The economics analysis of a Q-learning model of cooperation with punishment and risk taking preferences. *Journal of Economic Interaction and Coordination*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated public-goods-like game modeled with Q-learning, punishment can induce a stable cooperative equilibrium, but only if individuals have high enough exploration rates (i.e., are willing to try new strategies rather than always exploiting the best-known one). The required punishment must be proportional to the temptation to free-ride and increases with the exploration rate. If individuals are highly exploitative (low exploration), punishment is less effective unless intrinsic motivation for cooperation is high. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but higher cooperation rates would generally be associated with higher efficiency in standard public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation in public-goods-like environments, but its effect depends on agents' willingness to explore new strategies (exploration rate/risk preference). The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in settings where learning and exploration are important. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it provides only indirect evidence for the prediction task. The model suggests that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when exploration rates are high, but does not quantify efficiency outcomes.

- source: Teraji, S. (2016). Why Do People Obey Norms?. *Evolving Norms: Cognitive Perspectives in Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper presents theoretical arguments and formal models showing that costly punishment (including decentralized, altruistic punishment) can sustain norm compliance and cooperation in social dilemmas similar to public goods games. It also models the interaction between punishment and reputation as mechanisms for norm enforcement. The models predict multiple possible equilibria (high or low compliance/cooperation), with path dependence based on initial conditions. While the models are closely related to public goods games, the main outcomes are norm compliance and cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff. The models do not provide direct evidence or predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in public-goods-like environments can increase norm compliance and cooperation, especially when reputation effects are weak. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper suggests that punishment is likely to increase cooperation and norm compliance, which may translate into higher efficiency, but the models do not quantify this effect. The paper is most useful for understanding the mechanisms and conditions under which punishment sustains cooperation, rather than for making direct efficiency predictions.

- source: García, J., & Traulsen, A. (2025). Picking strategies in games of cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the strategy set and the microeconomic assumptions in evolutionary models of cooperation (including public goods games with punishment) can have a large impact on the predicted prevalence of cooperation. For example, allowing for antisocial punishment or for loners to be punished can lead to the collapse of cooperation, while more restrictive strategy sets can support high levels of cooperation. The paper emphasizes that these modeling choices can bias results and that more systematic, unbiased inclusion of strategies is needed for robust predictions.
