However, the paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on the abundance of cooperation and the evolutionary stability of different strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical caution for interpreting the effects of punishment in public goods games: the predicted effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, efficiency) can depend strongly on the details of the strategy set and microeconomic assumptions. If a model or experiment restricts who can punish or be punished, or omits antisocial punishment, it may overestimate the positive effect of punishment on cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not provide direct quantitative support for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of modeling assumptions and the potential for qualitative shifts in cooperation prevalence, which may translate to efficiency in some settings.

- source: Itao, K., & Kaneko, K. (2025). Self-organized institutions in evolutionary dynamical-systems games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a minimal two-player harvesting game with environmental feedback, the evolution of decision-making functions leads to the self-organization of social institutions (shared norms and punishment rules). Players evolve to monitor both their own and their opponent's states, and punish selfish overharvesting by retaliatory overharvesting, which degrades the environment and reduces both players' payoffs. This punitive mechanism stabilizes cooperation and leads to the emergence of efficient, periodic harvesting patterns (modes) that maximize group fitness. The model shows that the most efficient (highest fitness) cooperative modes are only evolutionarily stable if players' strategies are sufficiently plastic to punish defectors. The results are robust across a range of parameter values. The paper provides a formal framework for understanding how punishment and norm evolution can sustain high efficiency in common-pool resource settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (here, context-dependent punitive overharvesting) in a repeated common-pool resource game will increase group efficiency, provided that players can monitor each other's states or actions. The model shows that the emergence of punishment mechanisms is necessary for the stability of efficient cooperative outcomes, and that the efficiency gains are robust to parameter changes. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, binary-action resource games with environmental feedback, but the underlying principles are likely to generalize to other public goods or common-pool resource games with similar structure. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of punishment, monitoring, and plasticity in strategies are key moderators of efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Asgharpourmasouleh, A., Sadeghi, A., & Yousofi, A. (2017). A Grounded Agent-Based Model of Common Good Production in a Residential Complex: Applying Artificial Experiments. *Sage Open*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The agent-based model simulates a common good game in a residential complex, with agent strategies grounded in qualitative fieldwork. The model shows that in blocks with more defectors, common good production and payoffs collapse. Artificial experiments demonstrate that (1) introducing punishers in place of defectors, (2) increasing all agents' desirability for the common good, and (3) combining both interventions, all increase common good production and total payoffs. The most dramatic improvement occurs when both punishers are introduced and desirability is maximized, resulting in all agents adopting punishing strategies and achieving the highest payoffs and common good production. Thus, punishment mechanisms, especially when agents value the common good, can transform a low-efficiency, low-payoff equilibrium into a high-efficiency, high-payoff one.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (and increasing the number of punishers) in a public-goods-like environment can increase efficiency and group payoffs, especially when agents also value the common good. The model is not a standard PGG but is structurally similar, with continuous contributions, punishment, and payoff functions analogous to PGGs. The results suggest that, all else equal, introducing punishment will increase efficiency, particularly in settings where baseline cooperation is fragile or low. The effect is amplified if agents' intrinsic motivation (desirability) for the common good is high. For prediction tasks, this paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low.

- source: Wang, L. C., Liu, Y. Y., Guo, R. Q., Zhang, L., Liu, L. J., & Hua, S. J. (2024). Cooperation and resource sustainability in coupling social-ecological systems with dynamic growth rates. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that a peer rating-based heterogeneous distribution system (PRHDS) can promote cooperation in a structured population, but is fragile to random exploration (strategy noise). The main threat is the invasion of egoistic cooperators, which undermines prosocial clusters. Introducing monetary punishments for minority raters (either as a fixed fine or as a reduction in distribution share) can restore and stabilize cooperation, making the system robust to low levels of noise. Non-monetary punishment (reducing the voice of minority raters) is ineffective. The effect of the system depends on the matching of the public goods multiplier and the weight of ratings in the distribution formula. The model is all-or-nothing in both contribution and rating, and the main reported outcomes are behavioral (cooperation rates, strategy fractions), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It models a close variant of the public goods game with a peer rating-based distribution system, which acts as a combined reward/punishment mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. However, the results suggest that monetary punishment mechanisms (with explicit costs or share reductions) can stabilize cooperation in noisy environments, while non-monetary punishments are ineffective. The model is all-or-nothing, with no chat, and the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is a key parameter. The findings suggest that, in similar designs, enabling monetary punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates (and thus, by implication, efficiency), especially when the system is subject to noise. The absence of direct efficiency outcomes limits the strength of inference for the prediction task.

- source: Sigmund, K., & Hilbe, C. (2011). Darwin and the Evolution of Human Cooperation. *Principles of Evolution: from the Planck Epoch to Complex Multicellular Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The chapter synthesizes theoretical models showing that cooperation in public goods games is difficult to sustain without mechanisms for targeted punishment or reward. When such mechanisms are available, cooperation can be stabilized at high levels. The chapter also reviews the evolutionary logic behind kin selection, reciprocity, and group selection, and discusses the cognitive and informational demands of indirect reciprocity and moral systems. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical data on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment and reward mechanisms can stabilize cooperation in public goods games, suggesting that their presence is likely to increase cooperation rates. However, because the chapter does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it cannot directly inform quantitative predictions of efficiency changes due to punishment. Its value lies in contextualizing why and when punishment might be effective, rather than in providing parameter-specific guidance for efficiency prediction.

- source: Otten, K., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2024). Social norm dynamics and cooperation in changing groups. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that group changes, such as the entry of newcomers, tend to reduce cooperation in the short term because newcomers contribute less than incumbents. Over time, newcomers adapt to the incumbents' norms and increase their cooperation. Punishment mechanisms can induce newcomers to conform to group norms, but the review does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The evidence is based on both observational data from an online game (Ikariam) and lab experiments with public goods games and peer punishment. The review emphasizes the need for more causal and longitudinal research, especially on the role of normative differences and the potential for two-way adaptation between newcomers and incumbents.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by summarizing how group composition changes and punishment mechanisms affect cooperation and norm conformity in public goods game-like environments. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so its value for predicting treatment efficiency is limited. The review suggests that punishment can help align newcomer behavior with group norms, which may support higher cooperation, but the lack of payoff data means that efficiency effects must be inferred indirectly from behavioral outcomes. The paper is most useful for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment and group dynamics interact, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Chiba-Okabe, H., & Plotkin, J. B. (2024). Can institutions foster cooperation by wealth redistribution?. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) A prosocial institution (maximizing average population payoff) can increase cooperation and efficiency, but only if it is sufficiently forward-looking (low discount rate); if it is myopic, it fails to intervene and the outcome is the same as anarchy. (2) A pro-cooperator institution (maximizing cooperator payoff) always increases cooperation and efficiency above anarchy, even if it is myopic. (3) A selfish institution (maximizing its own revenue) can sometimes increase efficiency above anarchy if it is forward-looking, but if it is myopic, it exploits the population and reduces efficiency below anarchy. The model provides explicit analytical conditions for when each regime leads to higher or lower efficiency than the no-institution baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of institutional interventions (taxation and redistribution) on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It shows that the effect of 'punishment' or institutional intervention is not universally positive or negative for efficiency; rather, it depends on the institution's objective and its ability to value future outcomes (discount rate). For prediction tasks, the model suggests that institutional efficiency (analogous to punishment/reward effectiveness), the institution's foresight, and the alignment of institutional objectives with group welfare are critical moderators. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the 'punishment' is implemented as a tax-and-redistribute mechanism, rather than direct costly punishment between individuals.

- source: Skarzhinskaya, Y. M., & Tsurikov, V. I. (2021). Organizational change: from community to firm. *Rossiiskii Zhurnal Menedzhmenta-russian Management Journal*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper develops mathematical models of a self-governed collective where individual effort generates aggregate income, distributed by pre-negotiated shares. In the baseline, self-interested behavior leads to an inefficient Nash equilibrium. When a coalition forms and coordinates effort, the coalition's and its members' payoffs increase. If the coalition offers contracts to non-members (who then forego their share in exchange for a reward per effort), the organization transforms into a hierarchical structure, and under certain conditions, all agents' payoffs increase, reaching the communal optimum. The paper provides theorems showing that expanding the coalition always increases the sum of payoffs for coalition members and the new joiner, and can also increase payoffs for non-coalition members. The analysis is entirely theoretical, with no empirical or experimental results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that mechanisms enabling coordination, selective incentives, or contracts (analogous to institutionalized punishment or reward) can increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While it does not model explicit punishment, it analyzes how selective incentives and contracts can overcome inefficiency. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that introducing mechanisms that align individual and group incentives (such as contracts or selective rewards) can increase group efficiency, even in the absence of explicit punishment. The findings are most relevant for settings where coalition formation or contracting is possible, and less directly informative for standard PGGs with simple punishment options.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2023). Evolution of spite in an n-player game with an opting-out option. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated n-player games with an opting-out option, spiteful (costly harming) strategies can sometimes persist, but only under restrictive conditions: low cost-to-harm ratio, small group size, and specific opting-out rules. As group size increases, it becomes harder for spiteful strategies to achieve higher payoffs than nonspiteful strategies, and the basin of attraction for all-nonspiteful populations grows. The opting-out rule (how easily groups disband) is a key moderator: when homogeneous groups (all spiteful or all nonspiteful) are forced to disband, spite can persist more easily, but this is a somewhat artificial rule. The model is mathematically explicit about when spiteful strategies can dominate, and these conditions almost always correspond to lower group efficiency compared to all-nonspiteful populations. The analysis is directly about payoff and efficiency, not just behavior.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like n-player games with an opting-out mechanism, the introduction of costly punishment (here, spiteful harming) generally reduces group efficiency unless the cost-to-harm ratio is extremely low and the group size is small. As group size increases, the conditions for punishment/spite to persist become more restrictive, and group efficiency is more likely to be maximized when punishment/spite is absent. The details of the opting-out rule (e.g., whether homogeneous groups are forced to disband) can moderate this effect, but in most realistic settings, punishment/spite is not efficiency-enhancing. The model is not an exact PGG with standard punishment, but the payoff structure and evolutionary logic are closely related, and the results are directly about payoff/efficiency. Thus, the paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment/spite in large groups with standard cost structures will not increase efficiency, and may reduce it.

- source: Fontanari, J. F., & Santos, M. (2024). Solving the prisoner's dilemma trap in Hamilton's model of temporarily formed random groups. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in the 'others-only' n-person prisoner's dilemma (a strong altruism scenario), cooperation cannot evolve under asexual reproduction or sexual reproduction with selfing, but can evolve and become stable under biparental sexual reproduction if the group benefit multiplier (r) is sufficiently large relative to group size (n) and the cost of cooperation (c) is not too high. The model does not include punishment or reward, and the main mechanism enabling cooperation is the genetic structure imposed by biparental reproduction. The main outcome is the probability that the population reaches the all-cooperator state (fixation probability), which is a direct measure of group efficiency or welfare. The results are summarized in phase diagrams showing regions where cooperation is stable, bistable, or unstable, as a function of c, r, and n. The findings are robust to demographic noise and extend to dioecious populations. The paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment, communication, or other institutional design features.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments that lack punishment or reward mechanisms, especially when the game structure is the 'others-only' variant of the n-person prisoner's dilemma. It shows that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency (measured as fixation of cooperation and group payoff) is only achievable under specific genetic and demographic conditions—namely, biparental sexual reproduction, sufficiently high group benefit multiplier (r), and not-too-large group size (n) or cost (c). The results suggest that, without punishment, efficiency is generally low unless these conditions are met. However, the paper does not provide any evidence about the effect of enabling punishment or sanctions, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency difference between control and punishment-enabled conditions. Its main value is in establishing a baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency is generally low except under the specified genetic/demographic conditions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment.

- source: Sugaya, T., & Wolitzky, A. (2023). Bad apples in symmetric repeated games. *Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in large repeated public goods or similar games, the possibility of sustaining cooperation and high efficiency depends critically on the ability to punish defectors specifically. If the only available incentives are rewards or withdrawal of provision (i.e., voluntary mechanisms), then cooperation collapses and efficiency is low when there are even a few commitment types (bad apples) who always defect. Involuntary punishment (such as fines) that can be targeted at defectors restores the possibility of cooperation and high efficiency. The main technical result is that if a 'population dominant' action exists (i.e., defecting yields a higher payoff than the population average, regardless of others' actions), then equilibrium efficiency converges to the inefficient outcome as the population grows. If no such action exists, and punishment can be targeted, then high efficiency is possible. The results are general and apply to a wide class of symmetric repeated games, including public goods games with or without transfers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods and related games. It implies that enabling targeted, involuntary punishment (e.g., fines for non-contributors) is necessary for sustaining high efficiency in large groups with possible commitment types. If only rewards or voluntary mechanisms are available, efficiency will be low in the presence of even a small fraction of defectors. The paper's results are most directly applicable to settings where the game is symmetric, the population is large, and there is a possibility of commitment types. For the prediction task, the presence or absence of targeted punishment (punishment_tech), the nature of the incentive (reward_exists, punishment_cost), and the ability to distinguish defectors are critical moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) will be low in large groups with commitment types unless involuntary punishment is available; enabling such punishment can dramatically increase efficiency.

- source: Tverskoi, D., Ember, C. R., Gelfand, M. J., Jones, E. C., Skoggard, I., Toutee, L., & Gavrilets, S. (2024). Cultural tightness and resilience against environmental shocks in nonindustrial societies. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that group efficiency (average payoff) and cooperation are maximized at intermediate levels of cultural tightness when facing severe environmental shocks. Loose cultures (low tightness) are more resilient and efficient under mild, slow-onset shocks, while very tight cultures often fail to adapt to new shocks and experience lower efficiency. However, very tight cultures can maintain high efficiency in the face of regular, severe shocks if cooperative norms are already established. Increasing group size undermines cooperation and efficiency unless benefit parameters scale accordingly. Synergistic effects between different types of collective action (public goods) enhance both cooperation and efficiency. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning, but norm compliance is enforced via conformity in the utility function.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like environments with norm-based conformity (but no explicit punishment), efficiency is maximized at intermediate levels of norm strength (tightness) under severe shocks, and at low tightness under mild shocks. The absence of explicit punishment means the results are not directly transferable to settings where punishment is enabled or disabled, but the findings are highly informative for predicting efficiency as a function of group size, benefit structure, and norm strength. The model's structure and results are most relevant for public goods games with strong norm compliance or conformity-based incentives, and less so for games where punishment is the main enforcement mechanism. The model also shows that increasing group size reduces efficiency unless benefits scale up, and that synergy between collective actions increases the range of conditions for high efficiency.

- source: Pedroso, M. (2021). Blind Cooperation: The Evolution of Redundancy via Ignorance. *British Journal for the Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that redundancy in group cooperation can evolve without targeted punishment or coercion if individuals are ignorant of others' strategies and/or face harsh environments. In a threshold public goods game, when players do not know what others will do, they cooperate with an intermediate probability, leading to more cooperators than strictly necessary (redundancy). Harsher environments (higher group-level cost of failure) increase the probability of cooperation and thus redundancy. In contrast, if individuals are well-informed about others' strategies, redundancy does not evolve without punishment. The paper does not provide empirical or simulated data on efficiency or group payoff, but discusses these concepts in relation to the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how information availability and environmental harshness affect cooperation and redundancy in threshold public goods games. It suggests that punishment is not strictly necessary for redundancy or intermediate cooperation rates if players are ignorant of others' actions. However, it does not provide direct evidence or predictions about efficiency or group payoff with or without punishment, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment parameters. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding the role of information and environmental context as moderators, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Billard, E. A. (1996). Evolutionary strategies of stochastic learning automata in the prisoner's dilemma. *Biosystems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that cooperation in resource dilemmas (including public goods and commons dilemmas) is shaped by individual value orientation (pro-social vs egoistic), situational constraints (such as group identification, communication, and social norms), and the degree and type of uncertainty (social and environmental). It proposes that pro-social individuals are more likely to cooperate regardless of uncertainty, while egoistic individuals are more likely to defect, especially under uncertainty. Situational factors (including the possibility of sanctions or rewards) can constrain egoism and promote cooperation. The paper reviews prior findings that rewards increase cooperation and sanctions decrease defection, but notes that the costs of such interventions may outweigh collective gains and that effects may not persist when interventions are removed. The analysis is theoretical and hypothesis-generating, not empirical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual background on the psychological mechanisms that may moderate the effect of punishment, reward, and uncertainty on cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that punishment and reward can increase cooperation, but may not always improve efficiency due to their costs, and that their effects may not persist. The paper does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of situational constraints (including punishment/reward), group identification, communication, and uncertainty, and in warning that interventions may have costs that offset their benefits. However, it does not provide direct evidence for the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Chen, W., Zhu, Q. L., & Wu, T. (2023). Unfairness promotes the evolution of cooperation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to reject unfair resource allocations (interpreted as a form of punishment) leads to higher levels of cooperation, especially when moderate unfairness is present. Both extreme fairness and extreme unfairness are suboptimal for cooperation. The mechanism is explained by cyclic dominance among strategy types. In continuous strategy settings, cooperators tend to act fairer than defectors, and clusters of cooperators can resist exploitation. The model is theoretical and simulation-based, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but rather focuses on the fraction of cooperators and the evolution of fairness/unfairness strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the ability to reject unfair offers (a form of costly punishment) can promote cooperation in public-goods-like games, especially when resource allocation is neither perfectly fair nor extremely unfair. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation rates and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is also theoretical and does not manipulate or report most of the game design dimensions used in the prediction task. Thus, while the paper supports the general idea that punishment for unfairness can promote cooperation, it does not provide direct quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency outcomes in empirical PGG experiments.

- source: Janssen, M. A., DeCaro, D., & Lee, A. (2022). An Agent-Based Model of the Interaction between Inequality, Trust, and Communication in Common Pool Experiments. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial common-pool resource game with four players and no punishment or sanctioning, communication (chat) between rounds leads to a substantial increase in group efficiency and resource sustainability. Trust is modeled as a key mediator: communication increases trust, which reduces overharvesting and increases group earnings. The agent-based model, calibrated to experimental data, shows that a population with a majority of conditional cooperators (who respond to trust and inequality) best explains observed group outcomes. The model and data both show that communication leads to higher group payoffs and more sustainable resource use, even after communication ceases, due to persistent increases in trust. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, enabling communication (chat) in a common-pool resource game with four players and nine rounds leads to a large increase in group efficiency and resource sustainability. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of chat/communication on efficiency, but not for predicting the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning is present in the design. The model and empirical results suggest that communication alone can drive high efficiency via trust, but do not inform the marginal effect of adding punishment to such a game. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it is highly informative for the effect of communication and trust in similar environments.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Zhao, J. H., Gu, C. L., & Chen, W. M. (2024). Evolutionary dynamics in spatial public goods games with environmental feedbacks. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study models a spatial public goods game with environmental feedback, where individuals can be cooperators (who pay a cost to improve local environmental quality) or defectors (who do not pay and degrade the environment). The model explores how the ability of cooperators to repair the environment (delta), the destructiveness of defectors (gamma), and the cost of cooperation (c) affect the prevalence of cooperation. Key findings include: (1) Cooperation can only dominate if cooperator repair ability slightly exceeds defector destructiveness; (2) Increasing cooperator repair ability beyond a threshold can paradoxically reduce cooperation due to defector invasions; (3) Both very low and very high defector destructiveness can undermine cooperation; (4) Increasing cooperation cost generally reduces cooperation, but at low defector destructiveness, higher cost can sometimes help cooperation by reducing isolated cooperators and making clusters more robust. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide detailed theoretical insights into how environmental feedback, spatial structure, and cost parameters affect the evolution of cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide contextual understanding of how non-punishment mechanisms and environmental feedbacks shape cooperation dynamics in spatial PGG variants.

- source: Zhou, Y. F., Zheng, X. M., & Wu, W. W. (2015). Effect of local information within network layers on the evolution of cooperation in duplex public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a D-PGG where agents allocate all their resources to one of two network layers, the use of local information for imitation (i.e., agents observe and imitate based on payoffs from a single layer) generally promotes the prevalence of cooperative strategies compared to global information. The extent of this promotion depends on the network structure and the similarity between the two network layers. The study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how information structure and network multiplexity affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff or efficiency outcomes, nor for the effect of punishment. It should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency, but may provide contextual background on how information and network structure can moderate cooperation.

- source: Sotnik, G., Choporov, S., & Shannon, T. (2023). The Role of Social Identity in a Population's Adoption of Prosocial Common-Pool Behavior. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The model shows that social identity, especially when combined with high group commitment, expands the parameter space (pressure-synergy combinations) where prosocial common-pool behavior is fully adopted. At low population density, both high- and low-commitment social identity models increase adoption and speed to full adoption compared to the baseline. At high density, only high-commitment agents show a strong positive effect; low-commitment agents may even reduce adoption. The model does not include punishment, reward, or explicit payoff/efficiency outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral adoption rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It is relevant for understanding how social identity and group commitment might affect the adoption of prosocial behavior in PGG-like environments, but not for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The model does not manipulate or include punishment, nor does it report group payoff or efficiency. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Antoci, A., & Zarri, L. (2015). Punish and perish?. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary success and efficiency impact of punishment in a prisoner's dilemma with explicit punishment and reward options depend on the type of strong reciprocity present. Altruistic punishment of defectors (SNR) can support cooperation and high average payoff, but only if punishers are sufficiently numerous; otherwise, defectors dominate and efficiency collapses. Altruistic rewarding (SPR) leads to persistent coexistence of types and fluctuating efficiency. Symmetric strong reciprocity (punishing defectors and rewarding cooperators) is maladaptive: it leads to the extinction of cooperators and punishers, resulting in universal defection and minimal efficiency. Punishing non-punishing cooperators (PNP) or both defectors and non-punishing cooperators (HSNR) can robustly enforce cooperation and maintain high efficiency, with the PNP strategy being especially robust. The presence of unconditional cooperators who do not punish can undermine the evolutionary stability of punishment-based cooperation. The model shows that the efficiency effect of punishment is not universally positive and can be negative or fragile depending on the sanctioning structure and population heterogeneity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency effect of punishment in public-goods-like environments is highly contingent on the details of the punishment and reward mechanisms and the behavioral heterogeneity of the population. In prediction tasks, the presence of explicit reward options, the type of punishment (who is punished), and the initial population composition are critical moderators. Punishment of defectors alone can increase efficiency if enough punishers are present, but this equilibrium is fragile. Adding reward can undermine the effectiveness of punishment and reduce efficiency. Punishing non-punishing cooperators (second-order free riders) is more robust in sustaining cooperation and efficiency. Thus, when predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, it is essential to consider not just the presence of punishment but also the structure of available sanctions and rewards, and the possibility of second-order free riding. The findings caution against assuming that enabling punishment will always increase efficiency; under some conditions, it can be maladaptive.

- source: Janus, T., & Lim, J. J. (2009). Sticks and carrots: Two incentive mechanisms supporting intra-group cooperation. *Economics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper proves that in a repeated three-player prisoner's dilemma, the introduction of either collective punishment (Nash reversion by all after any defection) or reward mechanisms (transfers contingent on cooperation) can sustain full cooperation as a subgame perfect equilibrium, even when bilateral incentive compatibility constraints are violated. This means that group efficiency (welfare) can be maintained at the cooperative level under broader conditions than in the absence of such mechanisms. The results are general and apply to settings where players interact repeatedly and can observe each other's actions, and where the game is either infinite or has an unknown end point.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment (or reward) mechanisms in repeated social dilemma games can increase group efficiency by sustaining cooperation, even when cooperation would not be sustainable without such mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (especially collective punishment with Nash reversion) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment, provided players are sufficiently patient (high discount factor) and can observe actions. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it is a repeated three-player prisoner's dilemma), so the results are most directly applicable to small-group, repeated, fully observable settings. The paper does not provide empirical effect sizes or parameterized predictions, but it does establish that the direction of effect is positive for efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: Jia, S. H., & Wang, Z. Y. (2025). An evolutionary game model with reputation threshold and reputation score to promote trust in the sharing economy. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that implementing a dynamic, reputation-based reward and punishment mechanism in a trust game on a square lattice network increases the proportion of trustworthy participants and can increase total group revenue (efficiency) compared to a baseline without such mechanisms. The effect depends on the reputation threshold: moderate thresholds maximize both trust and total revenue, while very high thresholds reduce revenue due to excessive punishment. The model demonstrates that, for a wide range of initial conditions, appropriate punishment/reward settings can drive the system to full trust and higher efficiency. The results are robust to population size. The model is not a standard PGG but is adjacent, and the efficiency outcome is directly comparable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in networked trust games with dynamic reputation-based punishment and reward, enabling punishment (and reward) can increase group efficiency compared to a baseline with no sanctions, provided the punishment/reward parameters are set appropriately. The effect is non-monotonic: too much punishment (high threshold) reduces efficiency, while moderate levels maximize it. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence and calibration of punishment/reward mechanisms are critical moderators of efficiency gains from sanctions. The model is adjacent to PGGs but the efficiency outcome is directly relevant.

- source: Zhou, X., Belloum, A., Lees, M. H., van Engers, T., & de Laat, C. (2022). Costly incentives design from an institutional perspective: cooperation, sustainability and affluence. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma with institutional (third-party) incentives, pure punishment is always sustainable and, when sufficiently strong, increases both the market size and the accumulated wealth of participants and the third party. Light punishment can lead to market shrinkage or collapse. Mixed incentives (reward plus punishment) can maximize the sum of accumulated wealth for both parties at moderate strength, but their sustainability depends on both incentive strength and agent rationality. Pure reward increases participant wealth but is unsustainable for the third party in this model. The results are robust across different levels of agent rationality, though higher rationality makes it easier to achieve high cooperation and affluence.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in repeated pairwise social dilemmas with institutional punishment, increasing the strength of punishment (i.e., higher fines for defection) leads to higher efficiency (measured as accumulated wealth and market size), provided the punishment is not too light. Light punishment can reduce efficiency by shrinking the market. Mixed incentives can further optimize total efficiency at moderate strength. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar institutional settings, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to defection. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, so results should be transferred with some caution.

- source: Mohlin, E., Rigos, A., & Weidenholzer, S. (2023). Emergence of specialized third-party enforcement. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that specialized third-party punishment can enable and sustain high-efficiency cooperative equilibria in social dilemmas where direct and indirect reciprocity fail. When enforcers are incentivized (via reputation and meta-enforcement) to punish defectors, producers cooperate, and both groups achieve higher payoffs. The efficiency gain from punishment depends on the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the cost of information, and the destructiveness of enforcer conflict. If punishment is too costly or weak, or if enforcer conflict is not destructive enough, the system collapses to a low-efficiency defection equilibrium. The model also shows that the cooperative equilibrium is robust to the presence of alternative (non-cooperation-enforcing) reputation systems, but only if the key parameter thresholds are met.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment by specialized third-party enforcers can substantially increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, but only if the punishment is sufficiently severe, not too costly, and enforcers are themselves disciplined (e.g., via meta-enforcement and reputation). The model clarifies which game design dimensions (punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, information cost, severity of punishment, and the structure of enforcement interactions) are critical moderators of the efficiency effect. For prediction, the presence of third-party punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline, provided the relevant costs and incentives are favorable. However, if punishment is weak, costly, or undisciplined, efficiency gains may not materialize.

- source: Friehe, T., & Tabbach, A. (2018). A comparison of simple action-based and outcome-based policies for emergency-like situations. *Mathematical Social Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: Theoretical analysis shows that action-based punishment/reward policies are superior to outcome-based policies in generalized volunteer's dilemma settings (where more than one volunteer is needed and costs are private). Action-based policies can uniquely induce the efficient (second-best) outcome and prevent the no-participation equilibrium, while outcome-based policies always allow for a no-participation equilibrium, which is stable and potentially focal. The required level of punishment/reward under action-based policies is lower than under outcome-based policies. Combined policies that include punishment for non-participation can also rule out the no-participation equilibrium. The analysis is grounded in social cost minimization and group welfare, not just participation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that punishment (when implemented as action-based or combined with action-contingency) will have a more positive effect on efficiency (group welfare, social cost minimization) than outcome-based punishment in public-goods-like environments where more than one participant is needed. The results suggest that the design of the punishment mechanism (action-based vs. outcome-based) is a critical moderator: action-based punishment can uniquely induce efficient outcomes and prevent social traps, while outcome-based punishment may fail to improve efficiency if the no-participation equilibrium is selected. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, especially in settings with binary (all-or-nothing) participation, private costs, and no coordination. However, the results are theoretical and based on a generalized volunteer's dilemma, which is adjacent but not identical to standard PGGs.

- source: Mihm, M., & Toth, R. (2020). Cooperative networks with robust private monitoring. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment mechanisms can sustain efficient cooperation in networked repeated games, but the effectiveness of punishment—and thus the efficiency of equilibrium outcomes—depends critically on the monitoring structure. With perfect or partial monitoring (players can observe at least their neighbors' neighbors), punishment can be immediate and efficient networks are possible. With local (private) monitoring (players only observe their own ties), punishment is delayed and only highly clustered networks (with triadic closure) can robustly support cooperation. The paper provides formal incentive constraints (PIC, LIC, RIC) that characterize when efficient or near-efficient networks can be sustained. Efficiency is maximized when monitoring allows for rapid punishment, and is reduced when monitoring is local and punishment is delayed or less credible.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how the monitoring structure (analogous to 'show_other_summaries' and 'show_punishment_id'), network clustering, and the immediacy/effectiveness of punishment affect the efficiency of public-goods-like games. If punishment is possible and monitoring is sufficiently rich (players can observe others' actions beyond their own ties), then enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency or at least sustain cooperation. If monitoring is strictly local, only highly clustered (triadically closed) networks can sustain cooperation, and efficiency gains from punishment are limited. The paper also shows that the cost and effectiveness of punishment (punishment_cost, punishment_tech) interact with network structure to determine whether efficient outcomes are possible. The results are most directly applicable to networked repeated games with bilateral ties, but the logic extends to public goods games with networked or local interaction structures.

- source: Matsushima, H. (2012). FINITELY REPEATED PRISONERS' DILEMMA WITH SMALL FINES: THE PENANCE CONTRACT. *Japanese Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper proves that with a suitable contract that imposes a fine for deviation in the final period, full cooperation (i.e., maximal efficiency) can be uniquely sustained in finitely repeated prisoners' dilemma games. The required fine can be small relative to total payoffs if the game is long enough. The penance contract is robust to renegotiation, unlike trigger contracts. The result generalizes to other two-player games with similar payoff structure. The findings are theoretical and show that the design of the punishment mechanism (timing, size, and contingency) is crucial for achieving efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that, in two-player finitely repeated social dilemma games, the introduction of a well-designed punishment mechanism (specifically, a contract that imposes a fine for final-period deviation) can move the group from inefficient (all-defect) equilibria to fully efficient (all-cooperate) equilibria. The effect is robust to renegotiation if the punishment is contingent on final-period behavior and the fine is large enough to outweigh the gain from deviation. The result is directly relevant for predicting that enabling such a punishment mechanism will increase efficiency to the maximum possible, provided the contract is enforceable and the fine is sufficiently large. However, the result is limited to two-player, finitely repeated, contractible games and does not address larger groups or public goods games directly.

- source: Camera, G., & Gioffré, A. (2017). Asymmetric social norms. *Economics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games with heterogeneous players (random productivity), efficient outcomes may require some players to defect in certain matches. In such settings, standard public monitoring is insufficient to sustain efficiency, but contagious punishment strategies—where players switch to permanent defection after observing a deviation—can support high payoffs if the group is sufficiently patient, punishment is severe enough, and productivity differences are not too large. The paper provides explicit conditions under which these strategies are equilibrium and efficiency is achieved, and derives closed-form expressions for continuation payoffs. Thus, punishment (in the form of contagious, community-based sanctions) can enable high efficiency even in complex, asymmetric environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms (specifically, contagious punishment) can increase efficiency in repeated social dilemma games, even when the efficient outcome involves some defections due to heterogeneity. The results are most directly applicable to repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with random productivity, but the mechanisms and equilibrium conditions are relevant for predicting when punishment will improve efficiency in public-goods-like environments with heterogeneity. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or direct mappings to standard PGG parameters, but it does clarify that punishment can sustain high efficiency if players are patient, punishment is strong, and heterogeneity is moderate. For prediction, this suggests that enabling punishment in heterogeneous repeated games is likely to increase efficiency, provided the punishment is sufficiently severe and the group is not too heterogeneous.

- source: Corriveau, L. (2012). Game theory and the kula. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in a repeated, circular n-player game (the 'game of kula'), the unique Nash equilibrium of the one-shot game is inefficient (all defect). However, when the game is repeated indefinitely and players are sufficiently patient, efficient (fully cooperative) equilibria can be sustained by social norms that attach punishment to deviations. The model formalizes three types of punishment-based norms: direct reciprocity (punished by the harmed party), indirect reciprocity (punished by a third party), and mixed reciprocity (punished by both). The mixed norm is shown to be the most robust, existing under the widest range of parameters. The analysis is entirely theoretical, but it provides formal conditions under which punishment enables efficient cooperation, and shows that the presence of punishment strategies can transform an inefficient game into an efficient one.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in repeated, multi-player social dilemma games (with circular or networked structure) can increase efficiency, provided players are sufficiently patient and punishment is credible. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, and the results generalize the repeated prisoner's dilemma. The findings suggest that, in environments where punishment is possible and observable, and where players have high discount factors, the introduction of punishment mechanisms can shift the equilibrium from inefficient (defection) to efficient (cooperation), thus increasing group payoff. The paper also clarifies that the structure of punishment (direct, indirect, mixed) affects the robustness of cooperation, with mixed norms being most robust. For prediction tasks, this paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated, networked social dilemmas, especially as the number of players increases and as the possibility of both direct and indirect punishment exists.

- source: Tao, J., Zhong, X., & Lu, Y. F. (2011). Restrictive mechanism of flow control among non-cooperative Internet users. *Science China-information Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated flow control games, the use of punishment strategies (including trigger, tit-for-tat, and finite punishment) can deter selfish deviations and sustain cooperation among users, leading to optimal or near-optimal system utility. In the absence of such mechanisms, the equilibrium is inefficient. The FCAR algorithm, which operationalizes finite punishment, is shown via simulation to effectively regularize user behavior and maintain high system utility, with only temporary utility losses during punishment. The effectiveness of punishment depends on the number of users and the length of punishment. In finitely repeated games, cooperation is harder to sustain, especially in the final stage, but rational punishment strategies can still maintain cooperation for most of the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment in repeated resource allocation games (adjacent to public goods games) increases efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust to the number of users, provided the punishment is sufficiently strong and the game is sufficiently long or indefinite. The findings support the prediction that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment will increase average efficiency, especially when users are patient (high discount factor) and punishment is coordinated. The results are most directly applicable to repeated, continuous-action, non-anonymous settings with full information about others' actions and payoffs.

- source: Lancia, F., & Russo, A. (2019). Sustaining Cooperation Through Strategic Self-Interested Actions. *B E Journal of Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that organizations can sustain higher efficiency and cooperation by conditioning punishments (and rewards) not only on collective output but also on observable outside activities, even if those activities are not directly productive. Strategic distortion of privately beneficial effort (either increasing or decreasing it, depending on whether it is a complement or substitute to collective effort) reduces the short-run gain from defection, thus lowering the required severity and frequency of punishment. This mechanism increases the expected output and intertemporal utility of members compared to settings where only output is monitored. The result is robust to extensions with more generations and noisy monitoring of outside activities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in repeated public-goods-like environments with imperfect monitoring, the ability to condition punishment on additional observable actions (beyond just output) increases efficiency by making cooperation easier to sustain. If a game design allows punishment to be based on richer or more informative signals (e.g., monitoring of side activities), the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be higher than in designs where only output is observable. The model also predicts that the efficiency gain from punishment is larger when monitoring is more informative and when organizations can strategically distort observable but privately beneficial actions. However, the model is not an empirical test and does not provide quantitative effect sizes.

- source: Gioffré, A., & Tampieri, A. (2025). Community enforcement and the cost of cooperation. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that community enforcement via grim punishment can sustain efficient cooperation in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with random matching, provided the cost of cooperation is not too low or too high. Specifically, if the cost-to-benefit ratio exceeds a threshold (less than 0.5), punishment is always credible and cooperation is an equilibrium for any population size and discount factor. For any environment, there exists a 'target cost of cooperation' that enables coordination on cooperation and makes punishment effective. When the cost of cooperation is endogenous, players can use it as a coordination device to achieve efficient outcomes. The results are entirely theoretical and provide explicit payoff-based conditions for when punishment increases group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma environments with random matching and private monitoring, the introduction of credible punishment (grim trigger) can increase group efficiency to the cooperative level, provided the cost of cooperation is within a specific range. The key moderators are the cost-to-benefit ratio, population size, and discount factor. The results suggest that, for a wide range of parameter values, enabling punishment will move efficiency from the defection baseline to the cooperative optimum. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially the cost of cooperation (analogous to MPCR in PGGs), population size, and the presence of punishment. However, the model is not a literal public goods game but an adjacent repeated PD, so some caution is warranted in direct quantitative transfer.

- source: Murase, Y. (2025). Costly punishment sustains indirect reciprocity under low defection detectability. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of costly punishment in sustaining cooperation depends critically on the detectability of defection. When defection is difficult to detect (i.e., high probability that defection is misperceived as cooperation), social norms that include costly punishment (CP norms) can be the most efficient ESS, achieving high cooperation and high average payoff. When defection is easy to detect, punishment is rarely efficient and often reduces payoffs. The model provides explicit boundaries for when punishment is efficient, and shows that punishment is most effective when it is rarely executed but strongly deters defection. Execution errors and high punishment costs shrink the parameter space where punishment is efficient. The results challenge previous theoretical work that found only a narrow efficiency margin for punishment, showing that under asymmetric detection errors, punishment can be broadly efficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, parameterized theoretical predictions for the effect of punishment on efficiency in indirect reciprocity games with pairwise donation structure. While not a standard public goods game, the model is adjacent and the efficiency results are highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in environments where defection is hard to detect. The explicit formulas and phase diagrams allow mapping from game design dimensions (punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, error rates) and baseline efficiency to the expected efficiency with punishment enabled. The main moderator is the detectability of defection: when it is low, punishment can substantially increase efficiency; when high, punishment is unnecessary or even harmful. The results are most transferable to settings with pairwise interactions, reputation, and indirect reciprocity, but provide strong mechanistic insight for broader PGG-like environments.

- source: Jindani, S. (2020). Community enforcement using modal actions. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated random matching games where players are matched with multiple partners and can observe modal actions, any efficient (strictly individually rational) payoff can be supported as a strict, sequential equilibrium. This includes full cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma and similar games. The mechanism relies on community punishment: players who deviate (i.e., are non-modal) are sanctioned by others, and those who fail to sanction are also sanctioned. The equilibrium is robust to trembles and the presence of noisy players, and the efficiency loss due to noise is linear in the noise rate. However, the mechanism is not robust to observation errors unless these are rare relative to other noise. The informational burden is low: in the prisoner's dilemma, only one period of memory is needed to sustain cooperation. The results generalize to a wide class of games and show that community enforcement with punishment can achieve high efficiency under realistic informational constraints.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in repeated random matching games with multiple partners per period and observable modal actions, enabling community punishment can raise efficiency to the full cooperative level, provided players are sufficiently patient and the population is large. The results are robust to some forms of noise, and the informational requirements are minimal. For prediction tasks, the presence of multiple matching, observable modal actions, and community punishment mechanisms should be expected to enable high efficiency, even in large groups. However, if observation errors are frequent or if players cannot observe modal actions, efficiency may not be sustained. The findings are most directly applicable to settings structurally similar to the model (random matching, multiple partners, observable actions, and community punishment), and less so to standard public goods games with fixed groups and no community enforcement.

- source: Berger, U., & De Silva, H. (2021). Evolution of deterrence with costly reputation information. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study shows that in a repeated two-player deterrence game (with roles of challenger and defender), the possibility of costly punishment (retaliation) can lead to higher defender payoffs and social welfare, but only when reputation information is minimal (e.g., only the last action is observable and costly to obtain). When reputation information is rich (e.g., challengers can observe a defender's empirical fighting frequency over many rounds), deterrence breaks down, punishment loses its deterrent effect, and defender payoffs collapse. Agent-based simulations confirm that defender payoffs decline as the amount of reputation information increases, with a sharp drop when information becomes too rich. Thus, the efficiency effect of punishment is context-dependent: positive under minimal information, negative or null under rich information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the efficiency effect of punishment in public-goods-like environments depends critically on the information structure: punishment increases efficiency (defender payoffs) when reputation information is minimal and costly, but not when information is rich and easily available. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the design dimension 'information about others' past actions' (analogous to 'show_other_summaries' or 'show_punishment_id') is a key moderator: enabling rich, low-cost information can undermine the efficiency benefits of punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player deterrence games, but the mechanisms may generalize to PGG variants where punishment and reputation interact.

- source: Evans, R., & Thomas, J. P. (2001). Cooperation and punishment. *Econometrica*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated common interest games (with no discounting), the mere presence of cooperative (nondraconian) strategies in the set of possible strategies does not guarantee efficient (Pareto-optimal) outcomes in equilibrium. However, if the set of possible strategies includes at least one 'draconian' strategy (i.e., a strategy that is willing to punish noncooperation to the maximum extent, such as min-maxing the opponent forever after a deviation), then all Nash equilibria are efficient. The paper also shows that this result is robust to certain computability restrictions and discusses the limits of these results under discounting and other technical assumptions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in repeated games with a structure similar to public goods or common interest games, the availability of sufficiently severe punishment (i.e., the ability to impose maximal punishment at low or no cost) is both necessary and sufficient for punishment to induce efficient outcomes in equilibrium. If the game design allows for draconian punishment strategies, then enabling punishment should lead to high efficiency, regardless of the control efficiency. If only mild or 'cooperative' punishment is possible, efficiency gains are not guaranteed. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, infinite-horizon, undiscounted games, but the logic is relevant for understanding the role of punishment severity and credibility in other settings.

- source: Ezeigbo, I. C. (2017). How costly punishment, diversity, and density of connectivity influence cooperation in a biological network. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study uses agent-based models of repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games on networks (18 players, 1000 rounds per game) to examine how costly punishment, diversity, and network connectivity affect cooperation and average payoff. Costly punishment (paying 1 unit to reduce another's payoff by 4 units) reduces defection but also significantly reduces average payoff compared to no-punishment conditions. Decreasing network connectivity (lower density of connections) increases average payoff when punishment is absent, but not when punishment is present. Diversity (randomizing connections and neighbors) can promote cooperation, but only in synergy with low connectivity. The main efficiency result is that costly punishment, while reducing defection, comes at a substantial cost to group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that enabling costly punishment in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like network games reduces group efficiency (average payoff), even though it reduces defection. The effect is robust across different network densities and diversity conditions. If the control (no-punishment) game has higher efficiency, enabling punishment is likely to lower efficiency, unless other mechanisms (like diversity and low connectivity) are also present and interact synergistically. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in networked social dilemmas with similar payoff structures.

- source: Camera, G., & Gioffré, A. (2025). Cooperation in temporary partnerships. *Journal of Economic Dynamics & Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with random matching and temporary partnerships, combining direct punishment (within partnerships) and cyclical community punishment (across partnerships) can support full cooperation (efficiency) as a subgame perfect equilibrium, but only for sufficiently patient players (high discount factor). The milder cyclical punishment is less effective at deterring deviations in equilibrium (requires higher patience) but more effective at incentivizing punishment off-equilibrium (punishment is less costly, so more credible). In contrast, the classic grim punishment can sustain cooperation for less patient players when the temptation payoff is low, but fails when temptation is high. The length of partnerships (number of repeated rounds with the same partner) affects these thresholds: longer partnerships make cooperation harder to sustain for impatient players but make punishment more credible for patient players. The paper provides explicit mathematical conditions for these effects.
