  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how the structure of repeated interactions (number of rounds per partnership, random rematching frequency), the type of punishment available (direct, community, grim), and player patience (discount factor) interact to determine whether punishment can sustain efficient (fully cooperative) outcomes. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling direct punishment (via temporary partnerships) can increase the set of conditions under which efficiency is sustainable, but only if players are sufficiently patient. The effectiveness of punishment in raising efficiency depends on the interplay between the temptation payoff, the cost/structure of punishment, and the frequency of rematching. The results are most directly applicable to adjacent settings (repeated PD with random matching), but the logic may transfer to PGG-like environments with similar punishment structures. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or lab data, but its explicit equilibrium conditions and comparative statics are informative for predicting when punishment will improve efficiency.

- source: Laclau, M., & Tomala, T. (2017). Repeated games with public deterministic monitoring. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, reward_exists, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper provides a general theoretical framework for understanding how punishment and reward mechanisms can be used to sustain efficient outcomes in repeated games with public monitoring. It shows that the set of sustainable payoffs is characterized by admissibility and joint rationality, and that explicit punishment phases (using approachability) can deter deviations. However, the efficiency achievable in equilibrium is limited by the monitoring structure: if deviations are not detectable or not attributable, efficiency may be strictly less than the social optimum. The paper gives explicit constructions of equilibrium strategies with main (cooperation), punishment, compensation, and reward phases, and proves that for sufficiently patient players, any payoff in the interior of the admissible and jointly rational set can be supported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the efficiency of repeated games with punishment, especially in settings with imperfect public monitoring. It provides a theoretical upper bound on efficiency as a function of the monitoring structure and the ability to implement punishment phases. The explicit construction of punishment and reward phases shows how punishment can be used to sustain cooperation, but also highlights that efficiency is limited by the possibility of undetectable or unattributable deviations. For the prediction task, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends critically on the monitoring structure: when monitoring is sufficiently informative, punishment can support high efficiency; when it is not, efficiency is bounded away from the optimum. The results are general and apply to a wide class of repeated games, including but not limited to public goods games.

- source: Aramendia, M. (2006). Asymmetric finite punishments in repeated games. *Economics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in repeated games, including social dilemmas and Cournot oligopoly, AFP strategies—where punishment is finite and asymmetric—can sustain any feasible and individually rational payoff profile when players are sufficiently patient. The punishment phase is designed so that nondeviators are always better off than the deviator, and after a finite number of periods, cooperation resumes. This approach is less restrictive than some standard equilibrium refinements and can support efficient outcomes (e.g., cartel profits) even in cases where other concepts (like WRP) cannot. The results are illustrated with examples including the prisoner's dilemma and Cournot oligopoly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that finite, asymmetric punishment strategies can sustain efficient, cooperative outcomes in repeated games, even when standard trigger or minimax punishments are not credible or too harsh. For the prediction task, it suggests that the design of the punishment phase (finite duration, asymmetry favoring nondeviators) can be critical for supporting high efficiency, especially as the number of players increases or when renegotiation-proofness is problematic. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide direct quantitative predictions for specific experimental public goods games, but they do indicate that efficiency can be maintained with appropriately designed punishment even in large groups or complex environments.

- source: Olcina, G., & Calabuig, V. (2015). COORDINATED PUNISHMENT AND THE EVOLUTION OF COOPERATION. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the long-run efficiency of a team trust game with coordinated punishment depends critically on the institutional parameters of punishment: the effectiveness of coordinated punishment (damage inflicted if enough punishers act), the cost of punishment, and the strength of peer punishment (peer pressure on non-punishers). If the effectiveness of coordinated punishment is high enough (damage parameter λ ≥ 0.5) and peer pressure is strong (peer punishment cost to defectors is high relative to cost of coordinated punishment), then the only stable long-run outcome is a fully cooperative, efficient culture with no observed punishment (because the threat is credible). If these conditions are not met, the system converges to a non-cooperative, inefficient culture. The model also shows that increasing the effectiveness of punishment, lowering its cost, or increasing peer pressure all make the efficient outcome more likely. The results are robust to the dynamics of preference transmission, and the model provides explicit boundaries for the basins of attraction of efficient vs inefficient cultures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling effective, coordinated punishment (with sufficient effectiveness and/or low enough cost) and strong peer punishment will increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, even when the baseline (no-punishment) efficiency is low. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, and the mechanisms (threshold punishment, peer punishment, endogenous preference evolution) are highly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The explicit parameter boundaries (e.g., λ ≥ 0.5 for effectiveness, peer punishment cost vs coordinated punishment cost) can inform quantitative predictions about when punishment will be effective. The model also highlights that if punishment is not sufficiently effective or peer pressure is weak, enabling punishment may not improve efficiency. The results are most directly applicable to trust/investment games with team-based punishment, but the logic is transferable to PGG variants with threshold or coordinated punishment.

- source: Nirjhor, M. S. A., & Nakamaru, M. (2023). The evolution of cooperation in the unidirectional division of labour on a tree network. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a division-of-labour game on a tree network, without sanctions, the only stable equilibrium is full defection (premierD), resulting in low efficiency. Introducing a defector sanction system (where defectors are punished with probability and severity) can make full cooperation or mixed equilibria stable, depending on the punishment parameters and cost/loss structure. As the effective punishment (probability times fine) increases, the system transitions from defection to mixed states to full cooperation, thus increasing efficiency. The premier sanction system (punishing the root node for any defection) is less effective at promoting cooperation. The model provides explicit stability conditions for each equilibrium, showing how efficiency depends on the sanction system and network structure. Adding more levels to the network (making it deeper) hinders cooperation and efficiency, while adding more parallel branches has an indeterminate effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in public-goods-like division-of-labour games on tree networks, enabling a defector-targeted punishment system increases group efficiency compared to the no-punishment baseline, provided the punishment is sufficiently strong (high probability and/or fine). The effect is mediated by the cost of cooperation, the loss from defection, and the network structure. The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency (no punishment), introducing punishment will increase efficiency if the punishment is strong enough, but the effect may be partial (mixed equilibria) if punishment is moderate. The model also predicts that deeper (more levels) networks make it harder for punishment to achieve full cooperation. These insights can inform predictions of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency, especially in settings with hierarchical or supply-chain-like structure.

- source: McBride, M., Kendall, R., D'Orsogna, M. R., & Short, M. B. (2016). Crime, punishment, and evolution in an adversarial game. *European Journal of Applied Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, unlike standard social dilemma games (such as the public goods game), the adversarial game studied here can have multiple equilibria, including both efficient (no-crime, high-payoff) and inefficient (high-crime, low-payoff) outcomes. The presence of punishment (reporting and conviction) transforms the game from a pure social dilemma into a coordination game, where the effectiveness of punishment depends on the collective willingness to report. The Informant strategy (defectors who also report) is critical: under imitation dynamics, any presence of Informants is sufficient to reach efficiency, but under best response dynamics, Informants are necessary but not sufficient. The model shows that too severe punishment or high retaliation risk can undermine the emergence of efficient equilibria. Thus, the design of punishment institutions and the social context (e.g., support for reporting) are crucial moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like adversarial games is highly sensitive to the details of the punishment mechanism, the available strategy set (especially the presence of Informants), and the evolutionary dynamics. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, but only under certain parameter regimes: moderate punishment severity, low retaliation risk, and the availability of strategies that combine defection and punishment. The findings caution against assuming that punishment will always improve efficiency; instead, the effect is conditional and can be negative or null if punishment is too harsh or if key strategies are unavailable. The results are most directly applicable to adversarial games with crime and reporting, but the mechanism insights may transfer to public goods games with similar punishment structures.

- source: Vanderschraaf, P. (2016). In a Weakly Dominated Strategy Is Strength: Evolution of Optimality in Stag Hunt Augmented with a Punishment Option. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that adding a costly punishment option to a two-player Stag Hunt game (a coordination game with a payoff-dominant and a risk-dominant equilibrium) dramatically increases the basin of attraction for efficient, cooperative outcomes under evolutionary replicator dynamics. This means that, in evolutionary terms, populations are much more likely to end up in efficient, high-payoff states when punishment is possible, even though the punishment strategy is weakly dominated. The effect is strongest when the punishment inflicted on defectors is at least as large as the cost to the punisher. The results are robust across a range of parameterizations, and the paper provides both analytical and numerical (simulation) support. The findings are interpreted as theoretical support for the hypothesis that altruistic punishment can sustain cooperation and efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in two-player coordination games structurally similar to public goods games, the introduction of a costly punishment option can substantially increase the likelihood of efficient, cooperative outcomes, as measured by the basin of attraction under evolutionary dynamics. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment in a game with similar structure (even if punishment is costly and weakly dominated) should be expected to increase average efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, especially when the punishment inflicted is at least as large as the cost to the punisher. However, the results are derived for two-player games and evolutionary (not repeated-game) settings, so direct quantitative extrapolation to standard multi-player repeated public goods games should be done with caution. The qualitative direction of effect—punishment increases efficiency—is strongly supported.

- source: Liu, S. Y., Zhang, C. Y., Li, K., & Zhang, J. L. (2022). Exploring the inducement for social dilemma and cooperation promotion mechanisms in structured populations. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that in structured populations playing iterated prisoner's dilemma with memory-one strategies, increased randomness and heterogeneity in network topology (e.g., moving from regular lattice to scale-free networks) lead to a higher prevalence of defectors and lower collective average payoff (social welfare). The key behavioral parameter is p4, the probability of cooperating after mutual defection; low p4 is associated with 'dangerous' strategies that dominate in more random/heterogeneous networks and drive payoffs toward the minimum. The authors introduce two mechanisms: (1) enforcement, which forbids p4 < 0.3, and (2) punishment, which imposes a fine on agents with p4 < 0.3. Both mechanisms, especially with sufficient punishment intensity, eliminate dangerous strategies and raise the collective average payoff substantially, regardless of network structure. The effect is robust across random regular, ER random, and BA scale-free networks.
  prediction_guidance: Although the paper studies the iterated prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game, the structure is closely related, and the main outcome—collective average payoff as a fraction of the maximum possible—is directly analogous to efficiency. The findings provide strong evidence that introducing punishment (with sufficient intensity) in a repeated social dilemma robustly increases group efficiency, especially in environments where network structure would otherwise favor defection. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment targets low willingness to cooperate after mutual defection. The paper also highlights that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its strength: weak punishment may not eliminate low-cooperation strategies, but moderate to strong punishment does. These insights should inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment in repeated, networked social dilemmas, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to network-induced defection.

- source: Luo, J. S., & Zhao, M. (2013). Proportional cost for punishment enhances spatial reciprocity in evolutionary games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that proportional punishment (where the cost is distributed across a proportion of the population and defectors are fined based on their payoff) enhances both cooperation and average payoff in spatial prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games. As the proportion of the population bearing punishment cost (p) and the strength of punishment (beta) increase, the average payoff rises and more individuals achieve higher payoffs. The effect is robust across both game types. The paper provides detailed analysis of how payoff distributions shift with punishment parameters, showing that punishment reduces the number of individuals with very low or very high payoffs, concentrating payoffs in a higher, narrower range.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatial pairwise games (PDG and SD), introducing a proportional punishment mechanism increases average payoff (efficiency) compared to the baseline without punishment. The effect is stronger as the proportion of the population involved in punishment and the punishment strength increase. While the setting is not a public goods game, the adjacent nature of the games and the explicit analysis of average payoff and its distribution make the findings relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar environments. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment (especially with higher coverage and strength) will increase efficiency relative to the control. However, the model is theoretical and does not address all PGG design dimensions (e.g., no chat, no reward, no explicit round structure), so direct quantitative transfer should be cautious.

- source: Celik, G., & Peters, M. (2016). Reciprocal relationships and mechanism design. *Canadian Journal of Economics-revue Canadienne D Economique*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in environments where players cannot enforce punishments for non-cooperation, reciprocal contracting (where players can make commitments contingent on others' commitments) allows for a larger set of efficient outcomes than centralized mechanism design with the same punishment constraints. The key mechanism is that players can use contract offers to signal private information, which can lower the reservation utility of deviators and thus make more efficient outcomes individually rational and incentive compatible. The analysis is theoretical, with the Cournot duopoly serving as a worked example. The results show that, under certain conditions (private values, single crossing, transferable utility), the incentive compatibility constraints can be simplified, and efficient outcomes can be implemented with appropriate transfer schemes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that, even in the absence of enforceable punishment, mechanisms that allow for reciprocal commitments and information revelation can achieve higher efficiency than mechanisms that do not. For the downstream prediction task, the main implication is that the absence of punishment does not necessarily preclude efficient outcomes if the game design allows for reciprocal contracting and signaling. However, the results are derived in a general mechanism design context (e.g., Cournot duopoly, collusion, marriage, etc.) rather than in standard public goods games. The findings suggest that, in environments structurally similar to public goods games but with richer contracting and signaling possibilities, efficiency can be higher than predicted by models that only consider enforceable punishment or centralized mechanisms. The paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency changes when punishment is enabled or disabled in standard PGGs, but it does clarify the theoretical boundaries of what is possible in related environments.

- source: Annen, K. (2011). Lies and slander: truth-telling in repeated matching games with private monitoring. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that community enforcement mechanisms, where players can report on others' behavior and the group can punish defectors, can support efficient cooperation in repeated matching games with private monitoring. When experience-sharing (reporting) is exogenous and truthful, cooperation can be supported for any group size, and the minimal discount factor required does not depend on the number of players. When experience-sharing is endogenous (players choose what to report), personal punishments are needed to incentivize truth-telling, which reduces the severity of community punishment and increases the minimal discount factor required for cooperation. The ability to sustain efficient cooperation thus depends on the number of players and the matching rule in the endogenous case, but for large enough groups, community enforcement remains more effective than personal enforcement alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling community punishment (via experience-sharing/reporting) increases the efficiency of repeated matching games with private monitoring, especially in large groups. However, the efficiency gain is moderated by the need to incentivize truthful reporting, which introduces personal punishments that reduce the overall effectiveness of community enforcement. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is more positive in larger groups and when experience-sharing is exogenous or can be made truthful at low cost. When experience-sharing is endogenous, the efficiency gain from punishment is still present but smaller, and the minimal discount factor required for cooperation is higher. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent, especially on group size and the structure of information sharing.

- source: Uchida, S., Sasaki, T., Yamamoto, H., & Okada, I. (2024). Evolution of Pay-It-Forward in the Presence of the Temptation to Free-Ride. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in a donation game with institutional punishment, the evolution of cooperation and pay-it-forward strategies is strongly affected by the cognitive model of the players. Under EUT, only strong punishment (high fine or high probability) can sustain cooperation, and pay-it-forward strategies are not stable. Under PT, even weak or infrequent punishment is sufficient to deter free-riding, because players overweight small probabilities of being punished. This leads to the emergence and stability of pay-it-forward strategies and higher average payoffs (efficiency) in the population. The model shows bi-stability, with both cooperative and non-cooperative equilibria possible depending on parameters and initial conditions, but overall, punishment increases efficiency more under PT than under EUT.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games (here, a donation game) will increase group efficiency, especially when players are subject to prospect-theoretic biases. The effect is more pronounced when punishment is mild or infrequent, as real human players may overweight the risk of being punished. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, and the payoff-based outcomes are directly analyzed. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (even with low probability or low severity) should be expected to increase efficiency, particularly if the control game has low efficiency due to free-riding. The model also highlights the importance of psychological factors (probability weighting) in moderating the effect of punishment.

- source: Povey, R. (2014). Punishment and the potency of group selection. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that embedding a punishment system within a group selection framework reduces the evolutionary success of altruism. While punishment can statically deter selfishness and improve immediate social welfare, it simultaneously weakens the group selection mechanism that would otherwise promote the evolution of intrinsic altruism. This leads to a lower equilibrium level of altruism and, in the long run, can reduce dynamic social welfare. The model shows that the requirements for group selection to sustain altruism become more stringent when punishment is available, and that the use of punishment may have negative dynamic welfare implications, even if it improves static outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling punishment in a group environment may increase static efficiency (short-term group payoff) but can reduce dynamic efficiency (long-run welfare) by undermining the evolution of intrinsic cooperation. The model is not a standard public goods game but is adjacent, focusing on sequential punishment and group selection. The findings imply that, in environments where intrinsic motivation is important for long-run efficiency, introducing punishment may have ambiguous or even negative effects on overall efficiency, especially over evolutionary or cultural timescales. The paper is most informative for settings where the persistence of cooperation and welfare over time is of interest, rather than immediate, one-shot efficiency gains.

- source: Nirjhor, M. S. A., & Nakamaru, M. (2023). The evolution of cooperation in the unidirectional linear division of labour of finite roles. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a linear division-of-labor game, the introduction of a defector sanction system (punishing defectors with a fine, with some probability of detection) can stabilize the all-cooperation equilibrium and thus maximize group efficiency, provided the expected punishment is sufficiently high relative to the cost of cooperation. The first role sanction system (punishing only the first role regardless of who defects) is less effective and only promotes cooperation when the cost of cooperation decreases downstream. The benefit from cooperation does not affect the stability of cooperation or efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions under which each equilibrium is stable, showing that punishment can transform a system from one where defection is stable (and efficiency is low) to one where cooperation and high efficiency are stable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical support for predicting that, in public-goods-like environments with sequential or asymmetric roles, introducing a punishment mechanism that targets defectors (with sufficient expected punishment) will increase group efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust to the number of roles (player count) and the cost structure, provided the expected punishment exceeds the maximum cooperation cost. The findings suggest that the design of the punishment system (who is punished, detection probability, and fine size) is critical for achieving efficiency gains. The results are most directly applicable to settings with sequential dependencies and asymmetric roles, but the logic may transfer to some PGG variants. The model does not address repeated finite rounds, communication, or stochastic group sizes, so predictions should be cautious if those features are present.

- source: Watve, M. G., Damle, A., Ganguly, B., Kale, A., & Dahanukar, N. (2011). Blackmailing: the keystone in the human mating system. *Bmc Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a hidden-strategy game modeling human mating, the combination of punishment (social sanctions) and policing (gossip/observation), when stabilized by the possibility of blackmailing, leads to the stable coexistence of monogamy and other strategies, with monogamy maintained at high frequency. This results in higher average fitness (efficiency) for the population. If punishment is present but blackmailing is not possible, policing collapses due to second-order free-riding, and monogamy cannot be maintained. Thus, the introduction of punishment (with blackmailing) increases efficiency relative to the baseline with no punishment or with punishment alone. The model is robust to variations in the opportunity for blackmailing and to gender differences in payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (with sufficient severity and the possibility of side-payments/blackmailing) in a public-goods-like environment increases group efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition. The model is not an exact PGG but is structurally analogous, with cooperation (monogamy) as the group-beneficial act and defection (EPM) as the selfish act. The results suggest that punishment alone may not be sufficient unless stabilized by additional incentives (e.g., blackmailing), but when these are present, efficiency is higher. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is costly and subject to second-order free-riding, and where additional mechanisms (like blackmailing) can offset these costs.

- source: Heller, W. B., & Sieberg, K. K. (2008). Functional unpleasantness: the evolutionary logic of righteous resentment. *Public Choice*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation can be sustained in populations where some individuals are willing to punish cheaters, even if those punishers are themselves not cooperative. The evolutionary stability of cooperation depends on the relative costs of punishing, being punished, and being cheated. When the cost of punishing is less than the cost of being cheated, punishment can support a stable mix of cooperators and punishers, increasing overall efficiency compared to a world with only cheaters. If punishment is too costly, cheaters dominate and cooperation collapses. The model demonstrates that the existence of punishment mechanisms—regardless of the punishers' motives—can improve group welfare and efficiency in collective action settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in collective action games (including public goods game variants) can increase efficiency, as measured by average group payoff, provided the cost of punishing is not too high. The findings suggest that the presence and effectiveness of punishment mechanisms are key moderators of the efficiency gains from punishment. While the model is not an exact public goods game, its structure and payoff logic are closely related, and its results are directly relevant for predicting when punishment will improve efficiency. The paper does not address empirical game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, or communication, but it does provide strong evidence that the cost and effectiveness of punishment are critical for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Larson, J. M. (2016). The evolutionary advantage of limited network knowledge. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated prisoner's dilemma with gossip-based punishment, the ability to sustain full cooperation (and thus maximize group efficiency/fitness) depends on both the structure of the social network and the precision of individuals' knowledge about that structure. When all individuals have precise knowledge of the network, it is harder to sustain full cooperation because some individuals can be identified as weakly connected and thus are tempting targets for defection. When individuals have only coarse knowledge (e.g., know only the average neighborhood size), it is easier to sustain full cooperation, as targeting is not possible. The minimum discount factor required to sustain cooperation is lower under coarse knowledge than under precise knowledge, making full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) more robust. The model thus predicts that limited network knowledge facilitates the enforcement of cooperation through punishment, leading to higher group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining high efficiency in repeated social dilemmas depends not only on the presence of punishment but also on the information structure—specifically, the precision of network knowledge. In environments where individuals have only coarse knowledge of the network, punishment is more effective at sustaining full cooperation and thus maximizing efficiency. If a public goods game or similar environment allows only limited information about the network or about others' positions, the model predicts a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency. Conversely, if players have precise knowledge of the network, the positive effect of punishment on efficiency may be reduced. The paper does not provide direct empirical estimates but offers clear theoretical mechanisms and comparative statics relevant to prediction.

- source: Nasrallah, W. F., & Cheaib, K. A. (2016). An equilibrium model of how regulative and normative institutions influence micro-economic and organizational behavior. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The theta-pi model demonstrates that both punishment (regulative institutions) and empathy (normative institutions) can increase the efficiency of economic interactions by expanding the set of transactions that are mutually beneficial and deterring predation. The model provides explicit conditions under which markets are efficient (all cooperate), collapse (all defect or abstain), or are path-dependent/oscillatory. Punishment (pi) is especially effective in high-temptation, low-sucker-loss environments, while empathy (theta) is more effective when losses from being cheated are high. When some actors are exempt from punishment (elites), efficiency declines and can collapse if the exempt group is too large. Overlapping institutions and partial coverage can lead to complex dynamics, including cycles of cooperation and predation. The model quantifies the marginal value of increasing institutional strength and provides phase diagrams for different transaction types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (increasing pi) in a public-goods-like environment will increase efficiency, especially when the temptation to defect is high and the cost to victims is not overwhelming. The model clarifies that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its universality and the structure of payoffs, and that partial or selective enforcement can undermine efficiency. The findings suggest that, all else equal, increasing the probability or effectiveness of punishment will shift the equilibrium from market collapse or low efficiency to high efficiency, provided the institutional coverage is broad. The model also highlights that empathy-based mechanisms can substitute for punishment in some environments, but are less effective when temptation is high and losses are low. For prediction tasks, the model's phase diagrams and threshold conditions can be mapped to game design dimensions such as punishment cost, effectiveness, and universality, supporting the expectation that enabling punishment will generally increase efficiency in public-goods-like games.

- source: Wang, S. X., Cao, M., & Chen, X. J. (2025). Optimally Combined Incentive for Cooperation Among Interacting Agents in Population Games. *Ieee Transactions on Automatic Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the optimal protocol for combining reward and punishment to promote cooperation is time-invariant and depends on the cost-to-benefit ratio and the initial and target cooperation levels. When the initial cooperation level is high, pure punishment is most cost-effective; when it is low, pure reward is more efficient; and for intermediate cases, a specific mix of reward and punishment minimizes the total cost. The minimal cost protocol is analytically derived for both well-mixed (complete graph) and regular network populations. The results are robust across different network structures. The findings are directly relevant to predicting the efficiency of institutional punishment and/or reward interventions in repeated social dilemma settings, though the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma rather than the public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency of punishment (and reward) interventions in repeated social dilemma games, especially when the outcome of interest is the cost required to achieve a target level of cooperation. While the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma rather than the public goods game, the structure and logic of institutional incentives are closely related. The results suggest that the efficiency of punishment depends critically on the initial cooperation level, the cost-to-benefit ratio, and the network structure. The paper does not report group payoff or surplus in the standard PGG sense, but its focus on cumulative cost as a measure of efficiency is highly relevant for predicting the cost-effectiveness of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Zhang, Y., Hao, Q. Y., Qian, J. L., Wu, C. Y., & Bi, Y. (2024). The influence of the heterogeneities of social institutions and individuals' tendency to establish social institutions on cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneous social institutions (pool punishment, anti-social punishment, and social security/reward) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma framework can promote the evolution of pro-social groups and suppress second-order free-riding. The model shows that increasing the cost of institutional investment and the growth factor of the institutional pool generally promotes cooperation and higher average payoffs, but only up to an optimal point. The introduction of a non-wasteful model with an upper limit on individual punishment/reward further improves the efficiency of the system by reducing resource waste and allowing pro-social groups to survive under harsher social dilemmas. The network (spatial) structure enhances these effects compared to well-mixed populations. While the main outcomes are strategy proportions, the paper also analyzes average payoffs, showing that institutional punishment and reward mechanisms can increase group welfare compared to baseline scenarios without such institutions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of punishment and reward institutions—including their cost, effectiveness, and resource allocation rules—can significantly affect the efficiency (average payoff) of groups in social dilemma games. While the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma rather than a standard public goods game, the mechanisms are closely related, especially for pool punishment and institutional reward. The findings suggest that enabling punishment (and reward) institutions, especially with non-wasteful resource allocation and optimal parameter settings, will generally increase group efficiency compared to control conditions without such institutions. The results also highlight the importance of network structure, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the presence of anti-social punishment as moderators. However, the lack of direct efficiency ratios and the use of a prisoner's dilemma rather than a PGG limit the direct quantitative transferability to PGG-like environments.

- source: Rubin, H. (2022). When it pays to punish in the evolution of honesty and cooperation. *Synthese*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper uses evolutionary game theory and simulations to analyze how different forms of costly punishment (punishing lying vs. punishing defection) affect the evolution of honesty and cooperation in a repeated signaling/donation game. It finds that: (1) Punishment of lying increases honesty but often reduces the prevalence of cooperation, because the costs of punishing and being punished can outweigh the benefits of increased honesty. (2) Punishment of defection (as in standard strong reciprocity models) increases cooperation and group success, consistent with prior findings in stag hunt and repeated PD games. (3) The effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency depends on game parameters (number of rounds, benefit of donation, lie detection probability, punishment cost and effectiveness). (4) When both types of punishment are available, the results are intermediate. The paper highlights that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive and can be negative when punishment targets lying rather than defection.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important cautionary evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in games with multi-stage cooperation (e.g., signaling plus donation). If punishment targets lying (dishonest signaling), it may increase honesty but can reduce overall cooperation and thus efficiency, especially when punishment is costly and lie detection is imperfect. If punishment targets defection (failure to cooperate), it is more likely to increase cooperation and efficiency, as in standard public goods or stag hunt models. For prediction tasks, the design dimension 'punishment target' (lying vs. defection) is critical. The paper also shows that the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the number of rounds, and the benefit of cooperation all moderate the effect. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not always a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency (with punishment), especially when punishment targets lying.

- source: Ntemos, K., Pikramenos, G., & Kalouptsidis, N. (2022). Dynamic Information Sharing and Punishment Strategies. *Ieee Transactions on Automatic Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in dynamic information sharing games with two agents, cooperation (information sharing) is not sustainable in finite-horizon settings, but can be sustained in infinite-horizon settings using punishment strategies (CGT). The equilibrium region where cooperation is optimal is characterized, and its size increases as the cost of sharing decreases or the discount factor increases. Theoretical results show that the maximal equilibrium region is unique and can be computed iteratively. Simulations confirm that lower costs and more informative observations lead to larger cooperation regions. Thus, enabling punishment strategies increases the efficiency of the system (total expected information gain minus cost) compared to the baseline where no sharing occurs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment strategies in repeated/dynamic information sharing games can increase efficiency (total payoff) compared to the baseline with no punishment, provided the game is sufficiently long (infinite horizon) and the cost of sharing is not too high. The effect is more positive when the cost of sharing is low and the discount factor is high. The model is adjacent to public goods games: the 'public good' is information, and the payoff is information gain minus cost. The results suggest that, in similar dynamic games, enabling punishment will increase efficiency, especially as the game becomes more repeated and the cost of cooperation decreases. However, the model is limited to two players, binary actions, and does not include monetary payoffs or group sizes greater than two.

- source: dos Santos, M., & Knoch, D. (2021). Explaining the evolution of parochial punishment in humans. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment can promote cooperation and increase group average payoff (efficiency) when it is targeted at cheats and used to enforce cooperation. However, the evolution of parochial punishment (harsher punishment of out-group cheats) depends on the ecological scenario. Parochial punishment evolves (and can support high between-group cooperation and group payoff) when groups interact collaboratively and conflict occurs with random groups, or when cooperation is difficult to enforce. In contrast, when conflict occurs with the same group as prior interactions, punishment becomes spiteful (targeting all out-groups, including cooperators), which undermines between-group cooperation and does not improve efficiency. The model shows that both collaborative intergroup relations and the structure of conflict are key moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent: it can be positive (by promoting cooperation) or negative (by reducing group payoff through excessive or misdirected punishment).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games, especially in settings with group structure, third-party punishment, and group-level selection. It shows that the efficiency effect of punishment depends on whether punishment is targeted at cheats (promoting cooperation and group payoff) or is spiteful (reducing group payoff by punishing all out-groups). The presence and structure of intergroup conflict, the difficulty of enforcing cooperation, and the ability to form collaborative intergroup relationships are key moderators. For prediction, the model suggests that punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when it is used to enforce cooperation (especially when cooperation is hard to sustain), but can reduce efficiency if it is misdirected or used spitefully. The findings are most relevant for games with group structure, repeated interactions, and third-party punishment, but less directly transferable to standard linear PGGs without these features.

- source: Hu, B., & Du, Y. X. (2023). Integrated analysis of employee cooperation and conflict behaviors in the context of digital technology. *Simulation-transactions of the Society for Modeling and Simulation International*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and magnitude of punishment (penalty for conflict) is a key determinant of stable cooperation in a group setting modeled as an evolutionary game. If the penalty for conflict exceeds half the cost of cooperation, cooperation becomes the evolutionary stable strategy, and the group achieves higher average perceived payoffs. If the penalty is lower, the group exhibits unstable, turbulent behavior with frequent reversals between cooperation and conflict, and the average perceived payoff from cooperation is often lower than from conflict. The model also shows that increasing the proportion of cooperators and the perceived benefit of cooperation stabilizes group behavior. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and simulation, not on real-world or laboratory data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that increasing the penalty for non-cooperation (punishment) above a critical threshold (half the cost of cooperation) can shift group behavior from unstable, low-payoff states to stable, high-cooperation, higher-payoff states. For prediction tasks, the presence and magnitude of punishment cost and effectiveness are critical moderators. However, the outcomes are modeled as perceived payoffs and behavioral states, not as direct group efficiency ratios. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is a salient, well-understood, and enforceable mechanism, and less so for empirical prediction of actual group efficiency in experimental PGGs.

- source: Patrzyk, P. M., & Takác, M. (2017). Cooperation Via Intimidation: An Emergent System of Mutual Threats can Maintain Social Order. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that in agent-based models of repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with post-game fighting (punishment), aggressive strategies (harassers) become evolutionarily stable and promote high rates of mutual cooperation (CC) in small groups with strong deterrence. The mechanism is that agents develop reputations for toughness, deterring defection and reducing the need for actual fighting. In large groups, anonymity undermines this effect, and cooperation rates fall. When third-party (moralized) punishment is allowed, coalitions can enforce cooperation, but only under certain conditions (small groups, justified defection rules). The models suggest that punishment can increase group-level cooperation and, by proxy, efficiency, but only under specific structural conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that enabling punishment in repeated PD-like environments can increase cooperation rates (and thus efficiency) compared to control, especially in small groups with strong deterrence and information spread. The effect is weaker or absent in large, anonymous groups. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is reputation-based and where agents can observe and remember others' aggressive acts. The results are not from public goods games but from repeated PDs with post-game punishment, so transfer to PGGs should be cautious but is likely informative for adjacent settings. The paper does not report efficiency as a direct ratio of group payoff to maximum, but CC rates can be used as a proxy for high group welfare.

- source: Hernandez, I., Cohen, D., Gruschow, K., Nowak, A., Gelfand, M. J., & Borkowski, W. (2022). The Importance of Being Unearnest: Opportunists and the Making of Culture. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that (1) more severe punishment for noncooperation reduces the amount of voluntary cooperation in a population, both in agent-based simulations and in a human experiment; (2) raising the standard for what counts as sufficient cooperation to avoid punishment also reduces voluntary cooperation; (3) the presence of opportunists is necessary for strong reciprocators to thrive, but when punishment is too severe, opportunists proliferate and strong reciprocators decline; (4) these dynamics help explain why tight or collectivist cultures with strong norms and sanctions may have lower social capital (voluntary cooperation) than expected. The main outcome is the amount of voluntary cooperation, not direct group efficiency or total payoff, but the results imply that harsher punishment can reduce overall welfare by crowding out voluntary, uncoerced cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments structurally similar to public goods games with punishment, increasing the severity of punishment or raising the bar for what counts as sufficient cooperation can reduce the amount of voluntary cooperation, and thus likely reduce group efficiency or welfare. The presence of punishment is necessary for sustaining cooperation, but making punishment too severe or standards too high backfires. For prediction tasks, this suggests a non-monotonic or negative effect of increasing punishment severity on efficiency, especially when punishment is harsh or standards are high. However, the paper does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible payoffs, so the mapping to efficiency is indirect. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of punishment severity and standards on voluntary cooperation and, by extension, on group efficiency in similar multi-agent, multi-round games.

- source: Nakamura, M. (2019). Rare Third-Party Punishment Promotes Cooperation in Risk-Averse Social Learning Dynamics. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that risk-averse individuals in evolutionary social dilemma games can achieve stable, high levels of cooperation with only rare third-party punishment. Both exogenous (authoritative) and endogenous (peer) punishment models are analyzed. Theoretical results show that the required frequency of punishment to maintain cooperation is much lower for risk-averse individuals than for risk-neutral or risk-prone individuals. Among punishment variants, punishing all defectors or punishing one defector (as a warning) are both effective in promoting cooperation, while punishing only one-sided defectors is less effective. The models predict that, under risk aversion, group payoff and cooperation rates are higher with punishment than without, and that the cost of maintaining cooperation via punishment can be minimized.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in two-player social dilemma games with third-party punishment, enabling punishment (even rarely) can substantially increase cooperation and group payoff, especially when individuals are risk averse. The findings suggest that the efficiency gain from punishment is robust to low punishment frequency and that the structure of punishment (punish all vs. punish one) can moderate the effect. However, the results are derived from theoretical models, not empirical data, and the games are not public goods games but two-player dilemmas. The paper is most informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in environments where risk aversion is salient and punishment is rare.

- source: Matsuzawa, R., & Tanimoto, J. (2018). Sanctions triggered by jealousy help promote the cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that jealousy-motivated sanctions (where agents punish neighbors with higher payoffs, regardless of strategy) can promote cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. The effect depends on the network structure: in lattice networks, higher sanction efficiency (penalty per cost) promotes cooperation, while in scale-free networks, lower efficiency (higher cost per penalty) can be more effective. However, the paper notes that even when cooperation is achieved, the continued use of jealous sanctions (due to persistent payoff inequalities) can reduce total social payoff, potentially lowering efficiency. The main reported outcome is the cooperation fraction, not direct group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, but the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game. The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation, but may not always improve efficiency due to the cost of sanctions, especially when punishment is motivated by jealousy and can be directed at cooperators as well as defectors. The effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to depend on network structure and the cost-effectiveness of punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that punishment may increase cooperation but could reduce efficiency if sanctioning is costly and not well-targeted.

- source: Rumble, A. C., Willcox, K., Imada, H., & Yansen, D. (2022). Beyond Reciprocity: Forgiveness, Generosity, and Punishment in Continuing Dyadic Interactions. *Journal of Theoretical Social Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper synthesizes evidence from simulation and behavioral studies of dyadic repeated games (mainly Prisoner's Dilemma and its continuous variants) to argue that punishment, while sometimes increasing cooperation, generally reduces average payoffs and is less effective than generous or forgiving strategies for maintaining cooperation and maximizing outcomes. Costly punishment is found to lower group welfare and does not restore cooperation as effectively as non-punitive responses. The review highlights that punishment also carries social and emotional costs, making it less desirable in ongoing dyadic relationships. Generosity and forgiveness, by contrast, are more likely to sustain cooperation and higher payoffs, especially when noise or uncertainty is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and literature-based evidence that, in dyadic repeated games (especially noisy or continuous-choice environments), enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency or group payoff compared to control conditions without punishment. The negative effect is attributed to both the direct material cost of punishment and the escalation of retaliation cycles, as well as social/emotional fallout. The review suggests that, for prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (especially if costly and in dyads) should be expected to have a less positive or even negative effect on efficiency, unless the punishment is very cheap or the environment is highly structured to avoid retaliation spirals. The findings are most directly relevant to dyadic (two-player) repeated games, and less so to larger public goods games, but the mechanisms discussed may generalize to small groups.

- source: Yang, Q. X., & Yang, Y. L. (2025). Effects of an update mechanism based on combinatorial memory and high-reputation learning objects on the evolution of. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of a social monitoring mechanism (SMM) that rewards judges based on the observed cooperation rate among participants leads to higher levels of cooperation and lower corruption in the simulated two-layer network. The effect is robust across different parameter settings, including reward/fine amounts, the social reward criterion, the presence of zealots, and the strength of the reward factor. SMM is more effective than simply increasing rewards/fines in the absence of SMM. The model is applied to both Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift Game variants, showing similar qualitative results. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation/corruption rates).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which reward third-party punishers based on group cooperation rates can increase cooperation and reduce corruption in environments structurally similar to the Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift Game. However, because the outcomes are reported as cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be interpreted as informative about behavioral mechanisms rather than direct efficiency effects. The model is not a standard public goods game, but the two-layer structure and the focus on punishment/reward mechanisms are adjacent to PGG-like environments. The findings suggest that adding a social monitoring/reward mechanism for punishers may increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not directly quantified.

- source: Jiang, X. H., & Zheng, W. Q. (2024). Dynamic strategies for collaborative governance of rural environments: a simulation study on the most effective mechanisms. *Environment Development and Sustainability*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that under a static reward and punishment mechanism (SRPM), the system does not reach a stable equilibrium; strategies of all parties fluctuate periodically. Under a dynamic reward and punishment mechanism (DRPM), the system converges to a stable mixed-strategy Nash equilibrium (MSNE), interpreted as an evolutionarily stable strategy (ESS). Increasing the upper limit of punishments for illegal emissions by village enterprises significantly increases the probability of legal emissions and reduces the need for strict government regulation and active supervision by villagers. Increasing the upper limit of rewards for villagers' active supervision increases their supervision probability but may reduce legal compliance by enterprises and government regulatory effort. The DRPM is recommended for more effective and stable governance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that dynamic, behavior-dependent punishment mechanisms (analogous to more flexible or responsive punishment rules in public goods games) can stabilize cooperative behavior (legal emissions) and reduce the need for costly monitoring and enforcement. However, the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff. The main outcomes are strategy probabilities, not payoffs. The findings suggest that increasing punishment effectiveness (punishment_tech) and using dynamic punishment rules may improve stability and compliance, but the effect on efficiency is not directly quantified. The results are most relevant for understanding how punishment design (cost, tech, dynamic vs static) may affect behavioral outcomes in multi-agent governance games.

- source: Wu, Y. E., Li, Z. Q., Liu, Z. Y., Cui, B. W., & Yin, A. R. (2024). Third party interventions promote cooperation on the interdependent networks: A perspective based on prospect theory. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of third-party intervention (punishment and/or reward) in a networked PD/SD game increases the fraction of cooperators in both the intervention and dispute layers. The effect is stronger when the intervention cost is lower and the intervention intensity (punishment/reward coefficients) is higher. The results are robust across different network topologies (square lattice and ER random networks) and game types (PD and SD). However, the paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare; all results are in terms of cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that third-party punishment and reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas, and that the effect is moderated by the cost and intensity of intervention. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The results are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not payoffs. The model is also based on the PD/SD rather than a standard PGG, and is simulation/theory-based rather than empirical.

- source: Huang, Y. K., & Inohara, T. (2015). Group-separations based on the repeated prisoners' dilemma games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper provides a theoretical framework for understanding how the ability to sustain cooperation via punishment (trigger strategies) in repeated pairwise prisoners' dilemma games depends on the discount factor and the players' mixed strategies. It shows that as the discount factor increases, more pairs can sustain cooperation, leading to larger groups of mutually cooperating players. The main findings are the mathematical characterization of the group-separation structure as a function of the discount factor, and the identification of intervals where complete-group-separations (where all within-group pairs can sustain cooperation) are possible. However, the paper does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants, as it does not analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is theoretical: it shows how the ability to sustain cooperation via punishment in repeated pairwise games depends on the discount factor and the heterogeneity of players' strategies. For prediction tasks, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) is sensitive to the discount factor and the similarity of players' strategies, but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on efficiency effects. The model is also for pairwise repeated PDs, not for n-player public goods games.

- source: Goodman, J. R. (2023). The problem of opportunity. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based models of the Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment (strong reciprocity), cooperation can be maintained at high levels when only overt defectors are present. However, when agents can mimic cooperators or defect covertly (evading detection and punishment), the population can be dominated by individuals who are not true cooperators, even though the observed rate of cooperation remains high. The models highlight that the opportunity to defect undetected undermines the effectiveness of punishment-based strategies in ensuring genuine prosociality. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but raises concerns that high observed cooperation rates in experimental games may not indicate high group welfare or internalized prosocial preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical caution for interpreting high cooperation rates in punishment-enabled games as evidence of increased efficiency or group welfare. It shows that punishment can maintain high cooperation in simple models, but that the introduction of undetectable defection strategies (mimicry, covert defection) can allow free-riding to persist without reducing the observed cooperation rate. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment may not always translate to higher efficiency, especially if the game design allows for undetected defection. However, the models are dyadic PDs, not public goods games, and do not report efficiency or group payoff, so direct quantitative prediction is not possible from this paper.

- source: Christoforou, E., Anta, A. F., Georgiou, C., Mosteiro, M. A., & Sánchez, A. (2013). Crowd Computing as a Cooperation Problem: An Evolutionary Approach. *Journal of Statistical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment for cheating in a repeated master-worker (principal-agent) setting with reinforcement learning leads to faster convergence to a state where all workers cooperate (i.e., provide correct answers), compared to no punishment. However, punishment is not strictly necessary for eventual correctness if other conditions (such as sufficient reward relative to aspiration and cost) are met. The system is robust to high tolerance for defectors, but as group size increases, high tolerance can reduce the probability of achieving full cooperation. The main outcomes are the probability of cheating and the system's convergence to correctness, not direct measures of group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it shows that punishment reduces the average cheating probability and accelerates convergence to a fully cooperative state. In settings where efficiency is closely tied to the rate of cooperation (e.g., if only correct answers generate group benefit), these results suggest that punishment would increase efficiency, especially in the short-to-medium term. However, the absence of explicit payoff or efficiency data limits the direct applicability of these findings to the prediction task. The effects of group size, tolerance, and aspiration are also informative: higher group size and higher tolerance to defectors can undermine the effectiveness of punishment in achieving full cooperation.

- source: Zhao, X., & Zou, H. (2025). Research on the Evolutionary Game of Multi-Body Co-Innovation in Green Innovation Ecosystems. *Polish Journal of Environmental Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that government-imposed rewards (subsidies) and punishments (fines) both promote the stability of collaborative innovation among the three actors. There is an optimal benefit-sharing ratio for collaboration, and the effect of government intervention is strongest when both rewards and punishments are used together. The model shows threshold effects for punishment severity, and that the stability of cooperation is sensitive to the distribution of benefits and the magnitude of policy interventions. However, the main outcomes are the evolutionary stability of cooperation, not explicit efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task. It shows that introducing punishment (and/or reward) mechanisms increases the stability and likelihood of cooperation in a multi-actor game with public-goods-like features. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the change in efficiency from enabling punishment. The results suggest that, in similar multi-actor public-goods-like environments, punishment (especially when combined with reward) is likely to increase cooperation and system stability, which may be associated with higher efficiency, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Qu, X. L., Zhou, C. L., Cao, Z. G., & Yang, X. G. (2016). Conditional dissociation as a punishment mechanism in the evolution of cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Conditional dissociation, modeled as a punishment mechanism where players can break up with defectors and must wait before re-entering the game, promotes the evolution of cooperation in an iterated Prisoner's Dilemma. The effect is stronger when the punishment is more severe (longer waiting time) and when the population's lifespan is longer. Allowing mixed strategies (as opposed to only pure strategies) reduces the probability of ending up with a fully cooperative population. The model is clean, excluding other cooperation-promoting mechanisms, and shows that both intolerant cooperators and tolerant defectors can be stable outcomes. The main outcomes are the frequency of cooperative vs. defective strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (conditional dissociation) on cooperation in repeated pairwise social dilemmas. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that punishment mechanisms that impose a cost on defectors (and sometimes on punishers) can promote cooperation, especially when the punishment is more severe and when the game is longer.
