The absence of direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction, but the results support the general idea that increasing punishment severity (e.g., higher punishment cost or effectiveness) can increase cooperation, though possibly at the expense of overall efficiency if punishers also incur costs. The negative effect of mixed strategies on cooperation may also be relevant for predicting outcomes in more flexible or noisy environments.

- source: Li, J. Y., Wu, W. H., Li, Z. Z., Wang, W. X., & Zhang, B. Y. (2023). Data-driven evolutionary game models for the spread of fairness and cooperation in heterogeneous networks. *Frontiers in Psychiatry*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that costly punishment, when amplified by network heterogeneity and increased inequality aversion (e.g., via oxytocin), can spread through a network and promote higher levels of fairness and cooperation. This effect is observed in both the ultimatum game and the two-stage prisoner's dilemma with punishment, but not in the trust game, where the absence of punishment means that increased trust does not diffuse. The models fit experimental data well for behavioral outcomes (offers, cooperation rates, punishment rates), but do not directly analyze or report group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that punishment mechanisms are more effective than reward/trust mechanisms for promoting prosocial behavior in networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction that enabling costly punishment in networked social dilemmas (such as public goods or prisoner's dilemma variants) increases cooperation and fairness, especially when network heterogeneity allows central nodes to amplify the effect. However, because the paper is theoretical and focuses on behavioral outcomes (not efficiency or group payoff), it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The models suggest that punishment is more effective than reward in promoting prosocial outcomes, but efficiency implications must be inferred from increased cooperation and fairness, not directly measured. The paper is most useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators (e.g., network structure, inequality aversion), but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Wen, Y. J., He, Z. X., Shen, C., & Tanimoto, J. (2025). Indirect punishment can outperform direct punishment in promoting cooperation in structured populations. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that, in structured populations modeled as a square lattice, indirect punishment (punishing defectors who harm one's neighbors) can outperform direct punishment (punishing one's own defectors) in promoting cooperation, but only when both the cost and fine of punishment are low. Outside this narrow parameter region, direct punishment may be more effective, both may be equally effective, or neither may improve cooperation. The mechanism is that indirect punishment can impose more total fines on defectors without greatly increasing the cost to punishers, but only when punishment is cheap. The main reported outcomes are cooperation rates and the distribution of strategies; group efficiency or total payoff is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how the cost and scope of punishment affect cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that indirect punishment can be more effective at promoting cooperation than direct punishment under low-cost, low-fine conditions, but not otherwise. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only adjacent, as the main outcomes are behavioral. The findings may inform expectations about when punishment is likely to increase efficiency, but direct quantitative mapping is not possible from this paper alone.

- source: Li, B. F., Zhang, X., Shi, Z. Y., & Wei, W. (2023). Justice strategy can promote cooperation with the joint of dynamic aspiration in social dilemma. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a justice strategy (where players punish defectors at equal cost) alongside dynamic aspiration updating can suppress or eliminate defectors and promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The effectiveness depends on the initial aspiration level: higher aspirations lead to fewer defectors and more justicers. The justice strategy can dominate even when initially rare, especially when defectors form clusters. The model is agent-based and simulation-driven, with outcomes reported as the stable fractions of each strategy. No aggregate payoff or efficiency outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment (even when costly to the punisher) can reduce defection and promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas, especially when combined with dynamic aspirations. However, because the outcomes are reported as strategy fractions rather than group efficiency or payoff, the paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is also a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, though the mechanisms are related. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral pathways by which punishment can affect cooperation, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Wang, W., He, Z. X., Li, X. G., & Shi, L. (2023). Adaptive exit facilitates the evolution of cooperation in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game with punishment. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to adaptively exit the game based on their payoff relative to aspiration can significantly increase the prevalence of cooperation, even when punishment is weak. There is an optimal aspiration level at which adaptive exit best promotes cooperation. Adaptive exit protects cooperators from exploitation by defectors and reduces the burden of punishment on punishers by increasing defectors' exit probability. The model shows that adaptive exit can eliminate second-order free-riding and that the interplay between exit and punishment is crucial for the evolution of cooperation. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that adaptive exit mechanisms and the interaction between exit and punishment can strongly affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of strategy frequencies and not group efficiency or total payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that enabling exit (or similar mechanisms) may increase cooperation and reduce the cost of punishment, but do not provide quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment.

- source: Patrzyk, P. M., & Takác, M. (2017). Cognitive adaptations to criminal justice lead to paranoid norm obedience. *Adaptive Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that evolutionary pressures can lead to agents developing a cognitive bias that causes them to overestimate the probability of being observed and punished for norm violations. As a result, agents often cooperate (obey norms) even when they are not actually being observed, especially in environments with high uncertainty and/or strong punishment. The rate of cooperation increases with the magnitude of punishment, the probability of being observed, and the uncertainty in classifying situations as observed or not. However, much of this cooperation is 'irrational' in the sense that it is not based on accurate assessment of risk, but on evolved bias. The model does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that institutional punishment and cognitive bias can sustain high levels of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that increasing punishment magnitude and the probability of observation can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma settings, even when actual monitoring is imperfect. However, because the model is based on the Prisoner's Dilemma (not a public goods game) and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments is limited. The findings suggest that punishment can be effective in promoting cooperation, especially under uncertainty, but do not quantify the impact on group efficiency or surplus. The model also highlights the importance of cognitive biases in sustaining cooperation, which may be relevant for interpreting experimental results in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Sun, D. H., & Kou, X. L. (2014). Punishment Effect of Prisoner Dilemma Game Based on a New Evolution Strategy Rule. *Mathematical Problems in Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment (in the form of a nonzero payoff for mutual defection, parameter p) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can, under certain evolution rules, destabilize the all-defect equilibrium and promote the emergence and persistence of cooperation. The effect of punishment is nuanced: for some parameter ranges, higher punishment increases the fraction of cooperators, while in others, it stabilizes C-D structures and can reduce the final fraction of cooperators. The main reported outcomes are the fractions of cooperators and the stability of different local structures (C-C, C-D, D-D), not aggregate payoffs or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and does not use a public goods game or a direct variant. Its findings are about the dynamics of cooperation and the stability of strategy structures in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with punishment. While it provides insight into how punishment can affect the emergence and stability of cooperation, it does not provide quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its relevance to the prediction task is limited and indirect.

- source: Yan, Z. Y., Zhao, H., & Li, L. (2025). Dynamic role-switching in hypergraphs: Enhancing cooperation via adaptive punishment and reinforcement learning. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that periodic update rules with Q-learning and a punishment mechanism can substantially increase the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. Lower punishment costs and shorter update intervals are especially effective at promoting cooperation. The model is robust across different network structures. However, the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff, and the results are based on agent-based simulations rather than empirical experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms, when combined with learning and periodic update rules, can increase cooperation rates in spatial games. However, because the main outcome is cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that lower punishment costs and more frequent updating are likely to make punishment more effective at promoting cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency in related models, but this is not directly demonstrated.

- source: Guo, T., He, Z. X., & Shi, L. (2023). Self-organization in mobile populations promotes the evolution of altruistic punishment. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that migration in spatially structured populations promotes the evolution and prevalence of pro-social punishment (altruistic punishment) and cooperation, especially when individuals migrate to maximize potential gains rather than avoid losses. There is an optimal population density for this effect. Migration can sustain pro-social punishment even when punishment efficiency (fine/cost ratio) is low, as long as the absolute amount of punishment is high. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the prevalence of pro-social punishment and cooperation is interpreted as a positive outcome for the evolution of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that enabling migration or spatial self-organization can enhance the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation, especially under certain migration preferences and population densities. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings are most relevant for understanding the mechanisms by which migration and spatial structure interact with punishment, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Guan, K. X., Chen, Y. Y., Zheng, W. J., Zeng, L. L., & Ye, H. (2022). Costly signals can facilitate cooperation and punishment in the prisoner?s dilemma. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a prisoner's dilemma with costly signaling, punishment as a costly signal can become prevalent if the signal is strong and noise is low, but this does not necessarily increase cooperation. Without punishment, cooperation can dominate if the cost-benefit ratio is favorable and noise is low. The introduction of punishment (when its cost is converted into a strong costly signal) increases the frequency of punishment and reduces defection, but does not increase cooperation. The model does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly inferred.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment in two-player prisoner's dilemma games with costly signaling, but does not directly address efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that punishment can reduce defection and increase the prevalence of punishment strategies if the punishment signal is strong, but does not necessarily increase cooperation or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games, the relevance is limited and indirect, as the model is not a PGG and does not report efficiency outcomes.

- source: Huo, H., & Liu, X. Y. (2024). Behavioral decision-making of government, agricultural product producers, and consumers on agricultural product quality and safety regulation in a digital environment. *Frontiers in Public Health*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that severe government penalties for non-compliance (punishment), sufficient incentives, and reduced costs for producers to build traceability systems are key to achieving stable cooperation among stakeholders. Depending on the relative size of these parameters, the system can stabilize in different equilibria: (Not regulate, Not build, Satisfied), (Regulate, Not build, Satisfied), or (Regulate, Build, Satisfied). The presence and strength of punishment (penalties) are critical in shifting the equilibrium toward higher compliance by producers. However, the outcomes are reported as stable strategy profiles, not as explicit efficiency or group payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the presence and severity of punishment (penalties) can shift the equilibrium from non-compliance to compliance in a multi-actor regulatory game. However, because the model does not report group efficiency or explicit payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to directly predict efficiency changes due to punishment in public-goods-like games. The findings suggest that increasing punishment can increase compliance, but the translation to efficiency or group payoff is not quantified. The model structure and parameter dependencies may inform qualitative expectations about the effect of punishment in similar multi-actor regulatory environments.

- source: Wang, J., Liu, Z., Xu, Y., & Li, X. P. (2025). Dynamic evolution in multi-player networked trust games with graded punishment. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a graded punishment mechanism in a networked trust game simulation can effectively reduce untrustworthy behavior and increase the density of investment and trust. The effect of punishment depends on both the cost to punishers and the severity of punishment. Graded punishment outperforms traditional punishment in eliminating untrustworthy agents and increasing simulated group trust and total wealth. However, if punishment costs are too high, investment and trust collapse. The results are sensitive to network topology, with regular lattices supporting higher total wealth than scale-free networks. All findings are based on agent-based simulations, not real-world or laboratory experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theory-based evidence that graded punishment mechanisms can increase simulated group trust and total wealth in a networked trust game, especially when punishment is not too costly. However, because the study is simulation-based and uses a trust game variant (not a standard PGG), its findings should be interpreted as suggestive rather than directly predictive for real-world or experimental PGGs. The results highlight the importance of the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and suggest that graded (rather than uniform) punishment may be more effective at sustaining cooperation and group payoff in networked environments. The paper does not provide empirical data for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, but its modeling results may inform hypotheses about the role of punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and network structure.

- source: Jones, M. A. (1999). The effect of punishment duration of trigger strategies and quasifinite continuation probabilities for Prisoners' Dilemmas. *International Journal of Game Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the ability to sustain cooperation in indefinitely repeated Prisoners' Dilemma games depends critically on the duration of punishment (in trigger strategies) and the properties of the continuation probability sequence. Longer punishment durations (including the grim trigger) make it easier to support cooperation in equilibrium. If the continuation probability is 'quasifinite' (i.e., its geometric average tends to zero), then no amount of punishment can sustain cooperation, and only the noncooperative equilibrium exists. The paper provides explicit mathematical conditions for when cooperation is possible, and shows how to compute the minimum punishment duration needed for cooperation given the discount factor and continuation probability. These results are framed in terms of equilibrium existence and structure, not direct efficiency or payoff outcomes, but imply that punishment can enable higher group payoffs when the conditions are met.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how punishment duration and the probability of future interaction (continuation probability) affect the possibility of sustaining cooperation in repeated social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, the results suggest that increasing punishment duration (severity) generally increases the likelihood that punishment will improve efficiency, provided the continuation probability is not too low. If the effective game horizon is short (quasifinite), punishment will not improve efficiency. While the analysis is for Prisoners' Dilemma rather than public goods games, the logic and mathematical structure are closely related and can inform predictions about when punishment will be effective in increasing efficiency. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency changes, only conditions for the existence of cooperative equilibria.

- source: Steimanis, I., Hofmann, R., Mbidzo, M., & Vollan, B. (2020). When female leaders believe that men make better leaders: Empowerment in community-based water management in rural Namibia. *Journal of Rural Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of conditional neutral punishment—where agents punish neighbors with the opposite strategy if their own payoff is below the neighborhood average—substantially increases the fraction of cooperators in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is robust to variations in the temptation to defect and the cost and effectiveness of punishment. Cooperative punishers form protective clusters that shield cooperators, while antisocial punishment (defectors punishing cooperators) is largely ineffective. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperation, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional neutral punishment can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation rates typically—but not always—imply higher efficiency, especially in prisoner's dilemma-like settings. However, without explicit efficiency or payoff data, the magnitude and reliability of efficiency gains from punishment in this design cannot be directly inferred. The spatial structure, conditionality of punishment, and the absence of communication or reward mechanisms are important contextual features.

- source: Liu, J. Q., & Zhang, Q. W. (2025). Evolutionary dynamics of a probabilistic punishment mechanism with environmental feedback in regular networked Prisoner's Dilemma. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that a probabilistic punishment mechanism with environmental feedback can lead to three types of system states: (1) stable full defection with low probability of effective punishment, (2) stable full cooperation with high probability of effective punishment, and (3) bi-stability where both outcomes are possible depending on initial conditions. Increasing the speed of environmental feedback (i.e., how quickly the probability of effective punishment updates in response to behavior) can shift the system from defection to cooperation in the bi-stable regime. The model highlights the importance of punishment effectiveness, punishment cost, network degree (number of neighbors), and feedback speed in shaping cooperation dynamics. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how probabilistic punishment and environmental feedback affect cooperation in a networked Prisoner's Dilemma, which is adjacent to public goods games. It suggests that punishment mechanisms with high effectiveness and fast feedback can promote cooperation, especially in structured populations. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation frequency and not efficiency or group payoff, the paper offers only indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-like games. The findings may inform the expected direction of efficiency changes (via increased cooperation), but quantitative predictions for efficiency require caution.

- source: Wang, X. Y., Zhang, L., Du, X. R., & Sun, Y. L. (2017). Evolving cooperation in spatial population with punishment by using PSO algorithm. *Natural Computing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing punishment in spatial evolutionary games (n-player IPD and ISD) increases the proportion of cooperators and punishers, and decreases the proportion of defectors and loners, especially at lower cost-to-benefit ratios. The effect of punishment is stronger in the ISD game, where defection would otherwise dominate. Larger group sizes and higher cost-to-benefit ratios reduce cooperation. The network structure (regular, Moore, Von Neumann, small-world, complete) and neighborhood size also affect cooperation rates. The length of history record (memory) has a non-monotonic effect on cooperation. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can increase cooperation rates in spatial evolutionary games, and that the effect is moderated by group size, cost-to-benefit ratio, and network structure. However, because the outcomes are not efficiency or payoff-based, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The results suggest that, in similar spatial games, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation, especially in smaller groups and at lower cost-to-benefit ratios, but the effect on efficiency is not directly established.

- source: Shi, Y. T., Liu, B., Kou, X. L., & Han, X. (2014). Punishment and Feedback Mechanism for the Evolution Game on Small-World Network Based on Varying Topology. *Mathematical Problems in Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that a punishment and feedback mechanism—whereby neighbors of frequent defectors break links with them and form new links with each other—can help maintain a stable fraction of cooperators in a dynamic small-world network playing an n-round Prisoner's Dilemma. The mechanism is robust to the number of rounds played, but its effectiveness diminishes as the average degree (connectivity) and network size increase. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. The model is not a public goods game but is related in its focus on cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. Its main relevance is in showing that punishment mechanisms based on breaking social ties can help sustain cooperation in networked Prisoner's Dilemma settings, but the outcome is measured as the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or payoff. The model is adjacent to PGGs, and the findings may be indirectly informative for understanding how network structure and punishment interact to support cooperation, but they do not provide quantitative or directly transferable evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Bosworth, W. (2022). Social Power and Non-cooperative Game Theory. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper argues that the credibility of threats and offers (punishments and rewards) is central to understanding social power and governance in non-cooperative games. It shows that resources, reputation, and coordination problems determine the ability to credibly punish or reward, and thus to exercise power. The analysis highlights the importance of commitment mechanisms and tipping points in reputation for sustaining credible threats. However, the paper does not provide empirical findings or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical insights into how punishment and reward mechanisms may affect cooperation and social power in collective action settings, emphasizing the importance of credibility, resources, and coordination. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its value for prediction is limited to high-level mechanism discussion and may inform the interpretation of empirical results from other sources.

- source: Li, B. Q., Wu, Z. X., & Guan, J. Y. (2022). Critical thresholds of benefit distribution in an extended snowdrift game model. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In both infinite and finite structured populations, the critical proportion of benefits that defectors can exploit (while still maintaining stable cooperation) increases with the total resource available, but saturates at a limit. (2) Introducing incentives for cooperators and punishments for defectors increases this critical threshold, meaning that inequality (the share defectors can take) can become worse with such mechanisms. (3) In more highly connected populations, the critical threshold is lower, so inequality is less severe. (4) There is a critical value of incentive/punishment magnitude above which the relationship between resource size and critical threshold reverses. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but rather the conditions for stable coexistence of cooperation and defection.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment (and reward) on the sustainability of cooperation under unequal benefit distributions in a snowdrift game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It suggests that introducing punishment (and reward) can increase the threshold of tolerated inequality, potentially making outcomes less fair for cooperators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting that punishment can have counterintuitive effects on the distribution of benefits and the persistence of cooperation, especially under varying network structures and resource levels.

- source: Toelstede, B. (2020). The Absence of Policing and the Political Economy of Contribution and Defection. *Democracy & Security*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in large groups, the disappearance of institutional punishment leads to a delayed decline in public goods contribution and a rapid increase in defection, while the reappearance of punishment leads to a delayed recovery of contribution and a rapid decline in defection. The length of these delays depends on factors such as trust in the punisher, transparency, media behavior, and regime support. The paper highlights the asymmetry between small and large groups, and between peer and institutional punishment, in terms of the speed and nature of behavioral responses. It also discusses the role of blame assignment and political manipulation in shaping these dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. However, it offers theoretical and qualitative insights into how the effect of punishment on contribution (and by extension, efficiency) may be moderated by group size, institutional structure, trust, and information transparency. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that in large, real-world groups, the positive effect of punishment on contribution (and thus efficiency) may be delayed or attenuated compared to small, transparent, lab-like settings. The paper is not suitable for direct quantitative prediction but may inform qualitative expectations about moderators.

- source: Ziegler, R. (1997). The normative structure of solidarity and inequality. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in both dyadic and collective solidarity games (including public-goods-like settings), strategies that combine defection with active sanctioning of others (Hypocritical Cooperation) can robustly enforce high levels of solidarity (cooperation), provided the costs of sanctioning are not too high. However, this can also lead to 'overcompliance' or excessive norm enforcement unless checked by counterstrategies (Full Opposition). The evolutionary outcome depends on the mix of strategies, the costs and effectiveness of sanctions, and the distribution of 'poor' and 'rich' in the population. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that informal punishment can sustain cooperation under certain conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that informal punishment mechanisms can sustain cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially when the cost of sanctioning is not prohibitive and when the population includes actors motivated to enforce norms even while defecting themselves. However, the findings are based on simulation and focus on the prevalence of strategies and 'level of solidarity' rather than explicit group efficiency or payoff. The results suggest that the effectiveness of punishment in raising efficiency will depend on the cost and effectiveness of sanctions, the strategic mix in the population, and the degree of inequality. The paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency, so its value for direct prediction is limited to qualitative insights about when punishment is likely to be effective.

- source: Ibrahim, A. M. (2022). The conditional defector strategies can violate the most crucial supporting mechanisms of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that conditional defector strategies—cheaters who sometimes cooperate (e.g., to share dispersal costs or pay to escape punishment)—can successfully invade and dominate populations even when mechanisms like punishment, kin selection, and spatial structure are present. In the models, when cheaters can share dispersal costs, they can migrate and outcompete cooperators, leading to cooperator extinction. Similarly, when cheaters reduce their own payoff to avoid detection and punishment, they can dominate despite the presence of punishment mechanisms. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of agent types and extinction events, not direct efficiency or group payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a cautionary theoretical perspective: the presence of punishment mechanisms does not guarantee the maintenance of cooperation or high efficiency if conditional defector strategies are possible. The models are adjacent to public goods games and do not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, so direct quantitative prediction is not possible. However, the findings suggest that in environments where agents can adaptively evade punishment (e.g., by reducing their own payoff to avoid detection), the expected efficiency gain from enabling punishment may be much lower or even negative, as cheaters can still dominate. For prediction tasks, this paper highlights the importance of considering the sophistication of defector strategies and the limitations of punishment as a stabilizing mechanism.

- source: Andrews, J., & Davidson, D. J. (2013). Cell-Gazing Into the Future: What Genes, Homo heidelbergensis, and Punishment Tell Us About Our Adaptive Capacity. *Sustainability*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that punishment is a fundamental mechanism for stabilizing cooperation in large groups, enabling the evolution and maintenance of pro-social norms and group-level adaptation. It synthesizes evidence from evolutionary theory, ethnography, and game theory to claim that humans' unique adaptive capacity is rooted in our ability to cooperate, which is supported by both formal and informal punishment of defectors. The paper does not present new empirical findings or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes, but it does highlight the importance of punishment for sustaining cooperation and adaptive capacity in complex social systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms are important for sustaining cooperation in group settings, which is relevant to public goods games and their variants. However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in experimental games. The arguments may inform the expectation that enabling punishment will generally increase cooperation and potentially efficiency, but the paper does not address how specific game design dimensions (such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost) moderate these effects. Thus, its value for quantitative prediction of treatment efficiency is limited to high-level theoretical justification for including punishment as a positive moderator of cooperation.

- source: Wylie, J., & Gantman, A. (2024). Cooperation, domination: Twin functions of third-party punishment. *Social and Personality Psychology Compass*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that codified rules and third-party punishment have 'twin functions': they can promote cooperation and social order, but also serve as tools for domination, discrimination, and oppression. The authors review literature showing that third-party punishment can increase cooperation and group payoffs in experimental games, but also highlight evidence that punishment is often motivated by self-interest, ingroup favoritism, and can be used to harm outgroups or marginalized individuals. The paper calls for a broader theoretical understanding of punishment that includes its role in maintaining social hierarchies and facilitating discrimination, not just cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. However, it is useful for contextualizing the dual roles of punishment mechanisms: while they can increase cooperation and efficiency under some conditions (as shown in the literature it reviews), they can also be used in ways that undermine fairness or group welfare, especially when institutionalized. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent and may be moderated by group composition, institutional legitimacy, and the potential for discriminatory enforcement. It does not provide parameter-level evidence for any specific game design dimension.

- source: Syi (2014). Hidden patterns of reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper formalizes and compares four second-order action rules for direct reciprocity (e.g., Tit-for-tat, Contrite Tit-for-tat, Pavlov, Grim Trigger) and four second-order assessment rules for indirect reciprocity (e.g., Image Scoring, Simple Standing, Stern Judging, Shunning). It shows that these rules can be paired and exhibit similar patterns, especially in their relationship to punishment. The analysis highlights that moderate punishment policies (e.g., Simple Standing, Stern Judging) are most successful at maintaining cooperation, while extreme policies (e.g., Shunning, Grim Trigger) can undermine cooperation. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical evidence about efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and mechanistic insight into how different punishment and reciprocity rules may affect cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. Its value for the prediction task is limited to theoretical framing: it suggests that moderate, justified punishment rules are more likely to sustain cooperation (and thus potentially higher efficiency), while overly harsh or overly lenient rules may be less effective. However, these insights are not tied to specific game design dimensions or empirical efficiency data.

- source: Zabyelina, Y. (2020). The Long Arm of Anti-corruption: Extraterritoriality and Anti-corruption Targeted Sanctions. *Global Policy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper reviews two books: one theorizing extraterritorial anti-corruption enforcement as a collective action problem, and another analyzing the legal and human rights aspects of targeted anti-corruption sanctions. It highlights that extraterritorial enforcement and targeted sanctions can incentivize cooperation and deter corruption, but also notes the risks of politicization, lack of compensation for victims, and potential human rights concerns. The review does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related quantitative outcomes, and does not present experimental or empirical data on public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. While it discusses the theoretical role of incentives and sanctions in overcoming collective action problems, it does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes in experimental or empirical settings. Its relevance is limited to high-level theoretical framing of punishment as a tool to address free-riding in public goods provision, but it does not inform quantitative prediction tasks based on game design dimensions.

- source: Fischborn, M. (2023). The Evolutionary Roots of Moral Responsibility. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that moral responsibility judgment evolved as an adaptation that enabled groups of cooperative individuals to hold free riders responsible more safely by acting in a coordinated way. It distinguishes between two senses of justification in responsibility judgment (demand and permission) and hypothesizes that the permission sense evolved to allow individuals to assess social support for punishment, making collective punishment episodes safer and more stable. The account is situated within Tomasello's two-step theory of the evolution of morality and Boehm's ethnographic work on hunter-gatherer punishment practices. The paper critiques Mameli's account of second-order punishment and meta-emotional dispositions, arguing that coordinated group punishment (rather than second-order punishment) is the key evolutionary innovation. The paper does not provide empirical evidence or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes, but offers a conceptual framework for understanding the robustness and negative bias of responsibility practices.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence or data for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is a theoretical account of the evolutionary function of punishment and responsibility judgment, emphasizing the importance of coordination and social support in making punishment episodes safer and more stable. While it discusses the role of punishment in stabilizing cooperation and addressing free rider problems, it does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Therefore, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited to providing conceptual context about why punishment may have evolved to support cooperation, but it does not inform quantitative or design-specific predictions about efficiency effects in experimental games.

- source: Mariano, P., & Correia, L. (2015). THE GIVE AND TAKE GAME: ANALYSIS OF A RESOURCE SHARING GAME. *International Journal of Applied Mathematics and Computer Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in the Give and Take game, the existence of costly punishment (costs to both the punisher and the punished for the 'take' action) creates a strong incentive for cooperative, efficient sharing of the resource. When punishment costs are high enough, multiple Nash equilibria exist in which players alternate giving the resource, maximizing average payoffs and achieving Pareto optimality. If punishment is cheap or absent, exploitative strategies dominate and efficiency is lower. Simulations confirm that strategies which give the resource (cooperate) are more successful when punishment is costly, even if there is no bonus for giving. The game thus demonstrates that built-in, costly punishment can sustain efficient cooperation in resource sharing dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in two-player resource sharing games with built-in punishment, increasing the cost of punishment (to both punisher and punished) increases the efficiency of the game by supporting cooperative equilibria. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment with sufficiently high costs will increase efficiency relative to the control (no punishment) condition. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, turn-taking, indivisible resource games, but the mechanism—costly punishment deters exploitation and supports efficient cooperation—may generalize to adjacent public goods or resource sharing environments.

- source: Feinberg, Y., & Kets, W. (2014). Ranking friends. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of social interactions—specifically, the ranking profiles that determine the order in which players seek help—critically affects the ability to sustain full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) in repeated helping games. When punishment is available (either community or bilateral), full cooperation can be sustained if and only if incentive constraints are satisfied. These constraints are least restrictive (i.e., efficiency is most easily sustained) when expected costs are equalized across players (Latin square ranking profiles). Under bilateral punishment, even stricter symmetry (BFF) is required. The paper provides explicit formulas for the minimum discount factor required to sustain efficiency under each regime. Thus, the design of the interaction structure and the punishment regime jointly determine whether efficiency is achievable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated helping games with sequential requests and costly helping. It shows that the ability of punishment to sustain efficiency depends on the structure of interactions (ranking profiles) and the punishment regime. When the structure equalizes expected costs (Latin square), punishment is most effective at sustaining efficiency. If the structure is unbalanced, punishment is less effective, and efficiency may not be sustainable. The results are theoretical but provide explicit conditions and formulas that can be mapped to efficiency predictions in similar repeated public-goods-like environments, especially where helping is sequential and punishment is either community-wide or bilateral. However, the model is not a standard PGG, so direct quantitative predictions for standard PGGs should be made with caution.

- source: Lie-Panis, J., Fitouchi, L., Baumard, N., & André, J. B. (2024). The social leverage effect: Institutions transform weak reputation effects into strong incentives for cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that institutions, by leveraging contributions from individuals (motivated by reputation), can create strong enough incentives (via punishment, monitoring, or rewards) to sustain cooperation in settings where reputation alone would fail. The efficiency of the institution (how much incentive is generated per unit contribution) is a key moderator: high efficiency greatly expands the parameter space where cooperation and high payoffs are possible. The model also shows that when the population is patient (values future reputation), more individuals contribute, further increasing institutional effectiveness. However, when cooperation is easy, the institution can be wasteful, as its costs are not offset by additional gains in cooperation or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling efficient institutional punishment or monitoring in a public-goods-like environment will increase group efficiency and payoffs, especially when baseline (control) efficiency is low due to hard cooperation problems. The model clarifies that the effect of punishment depends critically on the efficiency of the institution (punishment technology), the cost of contributing to the institution, and the patience of the population. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that when institutional punishment is efficient and the population is sufficiently future-oriented, enabling punishment will lead to large increases in efficiency relative to control. However, if cooperation is already high in control, the marginal benefit of punishment may be negligible or even negative due to unnecessary costs.

- source: Dubey, S. P., Kedar, G. D., & Ghate, S. H. (2021). A COMMUNICATION NETWORK ROUTING PROBLEM: MODELING AND OPTIMIZATION USING NON-COOPERATIVE GAME THEORY. *International Journal of Applied Mathematics and Computer Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a non-cooperative network routing game with a punishment mechanism (increased cost for exceeding link capacity), the efficiency of outcomes (minimized total cost) depends on the structure of the cost function and the distribution of user demands. When the cost function is linear in the number of packets, all Nash equilibria are efficient (Pareto optimal). When the cost function is convex, only some Nash equilibria (about 50% of preferred focal Nash equilibria) are efficient, and this depends on the relative size of user demands. The punishment mechanism (cost increase) can deter greedy strategies and promote more efficient, balanced transmission strategies, but does not guarantee efficiency in all cases. The model is adjacent to public goods games in that it involves individual versus collective use of a shared resource, but differs in the details of the payoff and punishment structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment mechanisms implemented as increased costs for overuse can improve efficiency in resource-sharing games, but the effect depends on the cost function and the distribution of demands. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that punishment (costly overuse) can align Nash equilibria with efficient outcomes under certain conditions (e.g., linear cost), but not always (e.g., convex cost). The findings are most relevant for adjacent settings to public goods games where the 'punishment' is a proportional cost rather than a direct deduction or fine. The results support the idea that the design of the punishment mechanism (cost structure) and the symmetry of player demands are important moderators of efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Shinya, O., Yusuke, I., & Hiroki, T. (2016). The Condition for Generous Trust. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated trust games with uncertainty about partner intention and competence, neither pure punishment nor pure generosity alone can sustain high efficiency. Generous trust (forgiveness) becomes viable and can support efficient outcomes when uncertainty is moderate and is combined with moderate punishment. If uncertainty (incompetence) is too high, efficiency collapses. The agent-based simulation shows that a mix of matching generosity (forgiving in partner selection) and exit generosity (forgiving in relationship maintenance) strategies coevolve and support higher efficiency, but only above a threshold of partner competence. The results highlight the importance of balancing punishment and forgiveness to achieve efficient cooperation under uncertainty.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in trust-game-like environments depends critically on the level of uncertainty (competence) and the balance between punishment and forgiveness. In environments with moderate uncertainty, introducing moderate punishment alongside generosity increases efficiency compared to either strategy alone. However, if uncertainty is too high, even punishment cannot sustain efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the efficiency gain from punishment will be highest when the environment allows for some, but not excessive, uncertainty, and when punishment is not too strict or too lenient. The findings are most directly relevant to trust games and adjacent repeated dyadic games, rather than standard public goods games, but the mechanisms may transfer to PGG variants with similar uncertainty and partner selection features.

- source: Rauwolf, P., & Bryson, J. J. (2018). Expectations of Fairness and Trust Co-Evolve in Environments of Partial Information. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary simulations of a one-shot trust game with partner choice and partial information, both trust (willingness to invest with unknown partners) and costly punishment (rejecting profitable but unfair offers) can co-evolve and are adaptive, maximizing average payoffs. The benefit of punishment for efficiency only arises when trust is also present, and only in environments where information about partners is partially but not fully available. When information is fully transparent or there is no partner choice, punishment does not increase payoffs. The effect is robust to changes in the number of partners, population size, investment multiplier, selection intensity, and mutation rates. The model predicts that as the value of investment increases, the advantage and prevalence of fairness norms and punishment also increase.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency benefit of punishment in trust-game-like environments depends critically on the presence of both partner choice and partial information. If a public-goods or trust game is designed with multiple potential partners and only partial information about others' behavior, enabling punishment (the ability to reject unfair offers) can increase group efficiency, but only if players are also willing to trust unknown partners. If either partner choice or trust is absent, punishment does not improve efficiency. The model also predicts that the efficiency benefit of punishment increases with the investment multiplier (analogous to MPCR in PGGs). For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is strongly moderated by information structure, partner choice, and the value of cooperation.

- source: Castro, L., Serrano, J. M., & Toro, M. A. (1998). Conceptual capacity to categorize and the evolution of altruism. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the Assessor strategy, which incorporates a punishment-like mechanism (reprobation) based on observed or reported selfish behavior, achieves higher fitness (i.e., greater group efficiency or payoff) than standard reciprocal strategies like Tit-for-Tat or Observer Tit-for-Tat when competing against always-defect strategies. This advantage is robust across both unstructured and family-structured populations, provided certain thresholds of cooperation frequency are met. The Assessor strategy is especially effective when individuals can observe or be informed about others' reprobation of selfish behavior, allowing them to withhold cooperation from defectors more efficiently. The model predicts that such punishment-like mechanisms can support higher levels of cooperation and group payoff than reciprocity alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment or sanctioning mechanisms (here, reprobation) in repeated social dilemma games can increase group efficiency/payoff relative to control conditions without such mechanisms. The findings are most directly relevant to iterated prisoner's dilemma settings, which are adjacent to public goods games, and suggest that the ability to observe and act on others' punishment signals is a key moderator. For prediction tasks, the results imply that, all else equal, introducing a punishment mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency, especially when players can observe or learn about others' punishment actions. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide quantitative effect sizes or empirical validation.

- source: Chassy, P., Cole, J., & Brennan, C. (2025). Evolution of confrontation and cooperation in simple organisms as a function of environmental resources and cost of a conflict. *Expert Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The simulations show that the confrontation rate in a population is highly sensitive to the magnitude of punishment (cost of conflict) and reward (resource availability). As punishment increases, confrontation rates decrease, and organisms become more cooperative. The adaptation is driven by the evolution of gene profiles that determine the probability of confrontation. The model also finds that memory (even a simple one-step memory) has a limited but significant effect on confrontation rates, especially as punishment increases. The study provides evidence that risk-taking and confrontation are shaped by both environmental parameters and genetic/cognitive factors, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that increasing the cost or effectiveness of punishment in a confrontation/cooperation environment reduces the frequency of confrontational (defecting) behavior and increases cooperation. While the model is not a public goods game, the findings suggest that in environments where punishment is more costly or effective, populations evolve to be more cooperative, which would likely increase efficiency in analogous PGG-like settings. However, the lack of direct efficiency or group payoff measures limits the ability to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes from design dimensions. The results are most useful for understanding the qualitative direction of punishment effects in related strategic environments.

- source: Marco, J., & Goetz, R. (2024). Public policy design and common property resources: A social network approach. *American Journal of Agricultural Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that social network structure (cohesiveness) and the severity of the resource dilemma jointly determine the effectiveness of informal (social pressure) enforcement in promoting cooperation. Network-oriented policies can reduce the minimum share of compliers needed for full cooperation, but only up to 26% in the calibrated case. Legal enforcement (fines/subsidies) can complement informal enforcement, but subsidizing the costs of social pressure may eliminate stable partial cooperation. The number of agents has only minor effects unless changed drastically. The model suggests that policy effectiveness depends on initial conditions (stock, share of compliers, network structure) and that mixed enforcement strategies should be applied cautiously, as they may interact in non-additive ways.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the efficiency gains from enabling punishment (here, social pressure) in public-goods-like common resource games depend on network cohesiveness, the perceived severity of the dilemma, and the cost structure of punishment. The model suggests that punishment can increase the basin of attraction for cooperation and reduce the critical mass of compliers needed, but the effect is bounded. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that enabling punishment will generally increase efficiency (move the system toward the socially optimal steady state), but the magnitude of the effect is limited by network structure and agents' perceptions. The findings are most relevant for settings with costly, decentralized punishment and binary contribution choices, and less so for standard lab PGGs with explicit monetary punishment. The results are qualitative and parameter-dependent, not direct empirical estimates.

- source: Furuzono, Y., Yamakawa, T., Ohnishi, S., & Kaneko, T. (2013). Combined effects of social pressure from the inside and outside of fishing communities promote cooperation for stock management. *Ecological Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a coupled socioeconomic-bioeconomic model of a fishing community, where individuals choose to cooperate with management or not, and non-cooperators experience psychological punishment ('social pressure') that depends on both internal (community-based) and external (outside community) sources. The model shows that when both internal and external social pressures are sufficiently strong, the system can reach full cooperation, resulting in sustainable fish stocks and maximum sustainable yield (MSY)—a proxy for group efficiency. If only one type of social pressure is strong, or both are weak, the system may stabilize at lower cooperation and lower stock levels. The model highlights the importance of both community relationships and external societal pressure in promoting cooperation for resource management.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that psychological punishment mechanisms (social pressure) can increase group efficiency in common-pool resource settings analogous to public goods games, especially when both internal and external sources are present. While the model is not a standard PGG and does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff, it shows that the presence and strength of punishment-like mechanisms (social pressure) are critical moderators of group outcomes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling both internal and external punishment/sanction mechanisms is likely to increase efficiency, while the absence or weakness of either reduces the effect. The model also suggests that the effect of punishment is moderated by group size (player count), as social pressure per individual decreases with larger groups.

- source: Pin, P., & Rogers, B. W. (2015). Cooperation, punishment and immigration. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the punishment (expulsion) rate for immigrant defectors increases the overall cooperation rate in society, but decreases the cooperation rate among citizens. This is due to an equilibrium substitution effect: as immigrants are more strongly incentivized to cooperate, the overall level of cooperation rises above its 'natural' level, making defection more attractive for citizens. The model shows that the net effect of punishment is smaller than would be predicted if equilibrium effects were ignored. The paper does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but expected payoffs for different agent types are derived. The results are robust to heterogeneity in agent preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment targeted at a subset of players (immigrants) can increase overall cooperation rates, but may also induce more defection among the untargeted group (citizens), reducing the net effect on cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff may be less positive than expected if substitution effects are present. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so its quantitative value for predicting efficiency is limited. The model is adjacent to public goods games, as it is based on a networked prisoner's dilemma with binary (all-or-nothing) choices and dynamic linking, rather than a standard PGG. The findings are most relevant for understanding equilibrium behavioral responses to targeted punishment policies.

- source: Armstrong, K., Zhong, Y. M., Bhide, S., Grant, S. B., Birkland, T., & Berglund, E. Z. (2024). Simulating the emergence of institutions that reverse freshwater salinization: An agent-based modeling approach. *Journal of Hydrology X*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The agent-based model demonstrates that self-organized institutions with sanctions (fines for defectors, monitoring) can emerge and stabilize resource use, keeping salt concentrations below harmful thresholds when defection rates are low and innovation rates are moderate to high. High defection rates undermine institution effectiveness, leading to resource degradation. The model highlights the importance of cooperation, monitoring, and sanctions in sustaining common-pool resources, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff as in public goods games. The findings are most relevant for understanding the emergence and stability of sanctioning institutions in resource dilemmas, rather than for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, conceptual support for the idea that punishment and monitoring can improve resource outcomes in social dilemmas, especially when defection is not too prevalent and agents can innovate or imitate successful strategies. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate standard PGG design dimensions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGG-like games, this paper offers contextual and mechanistic insights but not direct predictive evidence. Its main value is in illustrating how sanctions and institutional emergence can stabilize cooperation and resource quality in agent-based models of common-pool resource dilemmas.

- source: He, J. J., Liu, J. S., Qiu, Z. P., Nanzayi, N. E., & Zhang, M. (2021). How Does the R&D Cooperation Path of Supply and Demand Network Enterprises Evolve?. *Ieee Access*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of cooperation in R&D networks depends on the balance of knowledge spillover, government reward, government penalty (punishment), cooperation cost, and cooperation risk. Increasing government penalty (punishment) and reward, and decreasing cost and risk, promote the stability of cooperation. The model shows that under certain parameter regimes, cooperation becomes evolutionarily stable, while in others, selfishness dominates. The effect of punishment (penalty) is to accelerate and stabilize cooperation, but the paper does not quantify efficiency or group payoff. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulation of the evolutionary game model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theory-based evidence that introducing or increasing punishment (government penalty) in a social dilemma-like environment can promote cooperation, especially when combined with rewards and low costs/risks. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results suggest that, in similar networked cooperation settings, punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates, which may translate to higher efficiency if payoffs are aligned with cooperation. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative direction of punishment effects and the importance of cost, risk, and reward as moderators.

- source: Dong, D. K., & Li, S. (2023). How a Smart regulatory platform can improve the property fee adjustment dilemma. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that under traditional regulation, the game between homeowners and property management tends to a 'prisoner's dilemma' with mutual defection (betrayal). Introducing a smart regulatory platform with government oversight, information transparency, and punishment for non-cooperation shifts the evolutionary stable strategy toward mutual cooperation. Stronger regulation and higher punishment increase the speed and likelihood of cooperation, while higher property fee increases make cooperation less likely. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulation of the evolutionary game, not on empirical data. The outcomes are expressed in terms of cooperation rates, not explicit efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theory-based evidence that introducing punishment and regulatory oversight in a repeated social dilemma can increase cooperation rates, especially when punishment is strong and information is transparent. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a standard public goods game. The results suggest that, in similar environments, enabling punishment and increasing regulatory strength would likely increase cooperation and, by implication, could improve efficiency, but this is not directly measured. The model's structure and findings may inform predictions about the effect of punishment in PGG-like environments, especially regarding the importance of punishment strength and transparency, but the lack of direct efficiency outcomes limits its predictive value for the target task.

- source: Pei, H. Y., & Yan, G. H. (2022). Punishment and reputation based partners-switching promotes cooperation in social networks. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that a mechanism combining behavioral punishment (severing ties with defectors) and reputation-based partner selection leads to high levels of cooperation in a dynamic networked prisoner's dilemma. The frequency of partner switching (time scale ratio W) is a key moderator: higher W leads to higher cooperation, and there is a critical W above which cooperation dominates. The effect is robust to temptation to defect and network degree, though higher values of these parameters require higher W for cooperation to prevail.
