The effect of the confidence increment parameter (alpha) is non-monotonic: smaller alpha can lead to higher cooperation under some conditions, but too small an alpha or high temptation to defect undermines cooperation. Common aspirations in the group support higher cooperation than heterogeneous aspirations. There is a threshold effect for aspiration: below the threshold, increasing aspiration increases cooperation, but above it, cooperation collapses. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on confidence and aspiration mechanisms in spatial prisoner's dilemma games may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how psychological or updating mechanisms affect cooperation, but not for the efficiency prediction task as defined. The absence of payoff or efficiency outcomes and the lack of punishment or reward mechanisms mean this paper should not be weighted for the core prediction task.

- source: Vukov, J. (2014). Accurate reactions open up the way for more cooperative societies. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that agents with more accurate and detailed memory (mem-1 strategies) can achieve much higher levels of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma than agents with only reactive memory. This effect is robust across a wide range of temptation-to-defect values, provided that the copying process is accurate (low error/noise). When copying errors increase, cooperation collapses. The paper interprets these results as showing that cognitive sophistication enables more stable and widespread cooperation, which in turn is associated with higher average payoffs, though payoff is not the primary outcome. There is no explicit punishment or sanctioning institution; instead, retaliation and forgiveness are embedded in the strategy space.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only indirectly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not study public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms. Instead, it analyzes how cognitive sophistication in memory and strategy (allowing for retaliation and forgiveness) affects cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The findings suggest that mechanisms enabling accurate, context-sensitive responses (such as retaliation or forgiveness) can promote cooperation and, by implication, higher payoffs, but this is not directly mapped to efficiency or to institutional punishment. The results may be useful for understanding the role of memory, strategy complexity, and error in sustaining cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment interventions on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Khatun, K., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Stability of pairwise social dilemma games: Destructive agents, constructive agents, and their joint effects. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in pairwise social dilemma games, introducing destructive agents (who opt out and harm others) does not promote cooperation, in contrast to their effect in public goods games. Instead, destructive agents destabilize or replace defection (in prisoner's dilemma and stag-hunt) or undermine cooperation (in harmony and chicken games). Constructive agents (who opt out and benefit others) can have similar destabilizing effects if their own payoff is high, but if their payoff is lower than what they give, they mainly destabilize defection without harming cooperation. When both agent types are present, the system exhibits complex multistable equilibria, but cooperation is not promoted. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using replicator dynamics and stability analysis. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not model punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes, and its focus is on pairwise (dyadic) games rather than multiplayer public goods games. The closest relevance is in its discussion of how certain opt-out strategies (destructive/constructive agents) affect the stability of cooperation, but not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper should not be weighted strongly in predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding the limits of voluntary participation and opt-out mechanisms in pairwise games, but not for the core prediction task.

- source: Lu, S. N., & Wang, Y. (2024). Adjustment of link weights based on tolerance promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Biosystems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to reduce interaction intensity with neighbors who defect beyond a tolerance threshold increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. However, if the tolerance threshold is too high (i.e., individuals are too forgiving), cooperation is undermined. The effect is stronger when more individuals in the population are 'environmental adaptors' who adjust their interaction intensity. The main outcome is the equilibrium frequency of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The findings are robust to changes in network size and initial cooperator fraction.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about mechanisms that may promote cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, specifically through adaptive partner selection or ostracism based on tolerance for defection. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game. The results suggest that mechanisms allowing individuals to reduce interaction with defectors (after a tolerance threshold) can increase cooperation rates, but excessive tolerance undermines this effect. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not analyze punishment costs, efficiency, or public goods structures.

- source: Lu, S. N., & Wang, Y. (2025). Experience-driven learning and interactive rules under link weight adjustment promote cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that incorporating both direct and indirect experience in interaction and strategy update rules, as well as introducing tolerance in link weight adjustment, significantly promotes cooperation in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma simulations. High sensitivity to defection (low tolerance) and greater weight on indirect experience both increase the frequency of cooperation and the average payoff of cooperators relative to defectors. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total welfare outcomes. The findings are based on simulation results and are not directly transferable to public goods games with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms allowing individuals to avoid defectors (via adaptive link weights and social learning) can increase cooperation and the average payoff of cooperators in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma settings. However, it does not study public goods games, does not implement explicit punishment or sanctioning, and does not report group efficiency or total welfare. Thus, its relevance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is limited and should be considered only as weak, mechanistic context.

  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of a 'three-faced' strategy—one that defects against cooperators, punishes defectors, and cooperates with its own kind—can suppress defection and promote cooperative behavior in a population, depending on parameter values. Low role-switching costs or high government rewards for punishing defectors favor the spread of the three-faced strategy and/or cooperators. High punishment costs or high temptation to defect favor defectors. The model identifies parameter regimes where cooperators, defectors, and three-faced individuals coexist, or where one strategy dominates. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the evolutionary stability and proportions of each strategy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical evidence about the effects of punishment-like mechanisms in a prisoner's dilemma context with a novel adaptive strategy. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it suggests that punishment-like behaviors (when not too costly and/or when subsidized) can suppress defection and support the maintenance of cooperation, especially when adaptive strategies are possible. The findings may inform qualitative expectations about the role of punishment cost, effectiveness, and incentives, but do not provide quantitative efficiency data for prediction.

Under certain parameter conditions, all enterprises will join the coalition (full cooperation), while under others, none will join. The model shows that the presence of random disturbances (stochasticity) leads to fluctuations in strategies but does not fundamentally alter the stable outcomes. The analysis highlights the importance of effective incentive and punishment mechanisms for coalition stability, but these are discussed at a conceptual level rather than modeled as explicit game interventions. - source: Gong, H. W., Xiang, H. J., Wang, Y. F., Gao, H. J., & Meng, X. Z. (2024). Strategy evolution of a novel cooperative game model induced by reward feedback and a time delay. *Aims Mathematics*.

  findings: The paper finds that in a theoretical evolutionary game with dynamic, feedback-based rewards (but no punishment), the system can exhibit multiple stable equilibria, bistability, and oscillatory dynamics depending on parameters and time delay. Increasing the reward does not always increase cooperation; under some conditions, even maximal rewards do not induce cooperation. The initial state can determine which equilibrium is reached. Introducing time delay can destabilize previously stable equilibria and induce stable limit cycles (oscillations) in the proportion of cooperators and reward intensity. The model does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctions.

It is a theoretical analysis of a different class of evolutionary games (general 2x2 matrix) with dynamic rewards and time delay, focusing on the evolution of cooperation and reward intensity. There is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and no implementation of punishment or sanctions. The findings may be of limited, indirect interest for understanding how dynamic incentives and time delays affect cooperation dynamics, but they do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A. (2023). Inequality leads to the evolution of intolerance in reputation-based populations. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that economic inequality is a strong enhancer of intolerance in reputation-based populations. Intolerant strategies can invade tolerant groups more easily when inequality is high, even if overall economic stress is moderate. Redistribution of wealth can prevent or reverse the spread of intolerance, but only if implemented before intolerance becomes widespread. The model shows that tolerant minorities under economic stress may reduce their willingness to punish defectors or may even help wealthy discriminators. The main reported outcomes are the intention to cooperate and the evolutionary stability of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. It suggests that in reputation-based cooperation environments, inequality and group structure can strongly moderate the effectiveness of punishment-like mechanisms (here, reputation-based exclusion) in sustaining cooperation. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings are most useful for understanding how inequality and group structure might interact with punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Wittman, D. (2005). The internal organization of the family: Economic analysis and psychological advice. *Kyklos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that advice from clinical psychology on setting boundaries and using natural or logical consequences (rather than punishment) in families is consistent with economic reasoning about property rights and efficiency. It argues that punishment systems in families create externalities and deadweight losses, and that structuring choices so that parents are indifferent to children's decisions (the 'indifference principle') minimizes manipulation and strategic behavior. The paper suggests that minimizing emotional involvement and not using punishment leads to more functional, efficient family relationships, but these claims are theoretical and not empirically tested.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative findings relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its arguments are theoretical and analogical, suggesting that punishment may introduce inefficiencies (externalities, deadweight loss) in small-group settings, and that structuring choices to avoid punishment may be more efficient. However, these claims are not tested in controlled experiments or public goods game variants, so the paper offers only indirect, conceptual support for the idea that punishment can reduce efficiency by increasing transaction costs and strategic manipulation.

- source: Pandula, N., Akcay, E., & Morsky, B. (2024). Indirect reciprocity with abductive reasoning. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that abductive reasoning in indirect reciprocity models can, under certain social norms and low error rates, promote higher levels of cooperation than non-reasoning models. For the Scoring norm, abductive reasoning enables stable cooperative states that are not possible without reasoning. For other norms (Simple Standing, Staying, Stern Judging), abductive reasoning can outperform or underperform non-reasoning depending on the error rates and the degree of confirmation/falsification bias. Under the Shunning norm, abductive reasoning leads to only defection. The model is more robust to errors in observation/assessment than to errors in action. The main reported outcomes are the equilibrium frequencies of cooperative strategies and intended cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how reasoning about errors and reputation assignment can affect the evolution of cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it suggests that the cognitive process for assigning reputations (abductive reasoning) and the social norm in use can be critical moderators of cooperation rates. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in PGG-like games with punishment, this paper is only adjacent: it does not model explicit PGGs, group-level payoffs, or direct costly punishment, but it does highlight the importance of error handling and norm choice for sustaining cooperation. Its findings may inform mechanistic priors about the robustness of cooperation to errors and the role of cognitive biases, but cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Liu, L. L. (2018). The dynamic of general compliance with the OECD anti-bribery convention: two interpretative approaches. *Crime Law and Social Change*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that the enforcement of the OECD Anti-Bribery Convention is best understood through a combination of rational-choice and institutional approaches. It highlights the collective action problem inherent in international anti-bribery efforts, likening it to a public goods game with free-rider issues. The rational-choice approach explains 'under-enforcement' as a result of insufficient incentives and ineffective centralized monitoring or sanctioning. The institutional approach emphasizes the role of domestic institutional contexts and 'unconscious enforcement' by agencies like the US SEC, whose evolving missions and regulatory environments lead to increased enforcement over time. The paper suggests that decentralized monitoring involving private sector whistleblowers and inter-country competition may be more effective than centralized mechanisms. However, it does not provide empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical context for understanding how punishment, monitoring, and institutional design might affect cooperation and compliance in public-goods-game-like environments, especially at the international treaty level. However, it does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff effects of punishment or monitoring mechanisms. Its relevance to predicting efficiency in experimental public goods games is limited to its discussion of collective action, monitoring, and sanctioning as mechanisms, rather than direct evidence. It may inform mechanism design or the interpretation of monitoring and punishment variables, but does not support direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

The model predicts that individuals will use both costly and costless signals to communicate their type (altruistic or spiteful), and that the availability of communication can affect the use of costly signals like punishment or reward. The paper discusses, but does not empirically test, the implications for public goods games, including the idea that punishment may serve as a signal of ill will and that communication may substitute for punishment. No direct findings on efficiency or payoff outcomes are provided.

Its main contribution is a theoretical framework for understanding how social preferences and signaling motives may influence the use of punishment, reward, and communication. While it suggests that communication opportunities may reduce the use of costly punishment (and vice versa), and that punishment can serve as a signal of ill will, it does not provide data or direct analysis of efficiency outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by highlighting possible mechanisms and moderators (such as the availability of communication or the signaling value of punishment/reward), but not by providing parameter estimates or outcome data.

Punishment and reward are discussed as norm enforcement tools, but the review does not analyze their effects on efficiency or group payoff. The presence of exemplary cooperators increases the resilience of cooperation and can enhance the 'quality' of cooperation, but the paper does not quantify efficiency or group payoff. The results are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulations of replicator dynamics and spatial evolutionary games.

They highlight that social punishment (e.g., social disapproval) may be more effective than financial sanctions in promoting norm compliance and cooperation, and that non-utilitarian values (e.g., stewardship, feelings of guilt) should be incorporated into NRM incentive structures. However, the paper does not present new empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes directly.

For downstream prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment may depend on whether it is social or financial, the presence of trust and reciprocity, and the integration of non-utilitarian values, but it does not provide direct evidence to calibrate efficiency outcomes.   findings: The paper finds that social preference models (such as inequity aversion and strong reciprocity) are designed to explain prosocial behaviors observed in games like the public goods game, ultimatum game, and dictator game. These models posit that individuals are motivated by concerns beyond self-interest, such as fairness or social image, and that such motives can explain costly prosocial behaviors, including punishment of defectors. However, the chapter argues that these models often use the standard self-interest model as a normative benchmark (i.e., efficiency is still defined in terms of material payoffs), and that the assumption of perfect rationality is retained more for model simplicity than for psychological realism. The chapter also notes that the evidence for perfect rationality in these contexts is weak, and that cognitive biases may play a significant role in observed behaviors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative findings that can be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. However, it is useful for understanding the theoretical underpinnings of why punishment is often assumed to increase efficiency (by sustaining cooperation and public goods provision) in the literature, and why efficiency is typically measured in terms of material payoffs. The chapter also cautions that the actual psychological mechanisms may be more complex than standard models assume, and that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on factors not captured by simple social preference models. For prediction tasks, this suggests that while the literature often expects punishment to improve efficiency, the underlying mechanisms and context-specific factors (such as cognitive biases or heterogeneity in motives) may moderate this effect.

  findings: The paper argues that manifest emotional displays in religious revivals function as hard-to-fake signals of genuine commitment, which help groups identify and exclude hypocrites who might otherwise free-ride on group cooperation. Emotional signaling is proposed as a solution to the problem of Machiavellian opportunists in cooperative settings, supplementing or replacing costly ritual signals.   findings: The paper proposes that motivation crowding (the phenomenon where extrinsic incentives undermine intrinsic or pro-social motivations) can be explained by changes in the framing of a situation. When a task or interaction is framed in terms of monetary gain or punishment, individuals may shift from pro-social or intrinsic motivations to self-regarding ones, reducing cooperation or helpfulness. The essay reviews evidence from behavioral economics and psychology (e.g., dictator games, prisoner's dilemma, effects of fines and rewards) to support this mechanism. It recommends that institutional designers consider how incentives and frames interact, as poorly designed incentives can backfire by crowding out desirable motivations. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual guidance on the importance of framing and motivation crowding for predicting the effects of punishment or incentives in social dilemmas. It suggests that introducing punishment or monetary incentives can sometimes reduce cooperation or pro-social behavior, especially if these incentives shift the frame from normative/moral to gain/self-interest. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically analyze game design dimensions, it cannot be used directly to predict treatment efficiency from control efficiency and design features. Its main value is in highlighting the potential for negative or counterintuitive effects of punishment/reward mechanisms, especially when they alter the perceived frame of the game.

- source: Pedroso, M. (2022). Betting blind: coping with uncertainty through redundancy. *Synthese*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that redundancy in group tasks (such as public goods provision) is beneficial in unpredictable or 'opaque' environments, as it increases the probability that the group will succeed despite individual failures. However, the benefits of redundancy diminish as more redundant members are added, and can disappear if failures are correlated (coupling). High redundancy also increases the incentive to free-ride, making such arrangements evolutionarily unstable unless mechanisms like coercion (punishment) or ignorance of others' strategies are present. The paper discusses, but does not empirically test, the hypothesis that punishment can stabilize high-redundancy cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical background on the conditions under which redundancy (extra cooperators) is beneficial or stable in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that punishment or coercion may be necessary to maintain high levels of cooperation (and thus redundancy) in the face of free-rider incentives, but does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that punishment can be a moderator of cooperation in redundant groups, but does not inform the quantitative relationship between punishment and efficiency. Its value is mainly in highlighting the importance of free-rider suppression mechanisms (like punishment) for the stability of cooperation in redundant settings.

The model is applied to obligate and facultative mutualisms, arguing that metacommunity structure and weight-of-numbers dispersal can allow mutualists to outcompete cheaters at the community level. The paper does not provide empirical or simulated data on efficiency or payoffs, but suggests that mutualistic communities are more productive and can proliferate under MLS1 dynamics.

- source: Bae, M., Shimada, T., & Baek, S. K. (2024). Exact cluster dynamics of indirect reciprocity in complete graphs. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper provides an exact theoretical analysis of how different indirect reciprocity norms (L4 and L6) shape the long-run social structure in large, fully connected populations. Under L6 (stern judging), the population almost always segregates into two mutually hostile clusters as the group size increases, due to the entropy of balanced configurations. Under L4, a subtle change in the assessment rule leads to a high probability of the 'paradise' state, where everyone assesses everyone else positively, especially as group size increases. The findings are about the stationary distribution of assessment configurations and the emergence of social structure, not about efficiency or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. While it is highly informative about the structural consequences of different indirect reciprocity norms (i.e., whether societies segregate or reach universal cooperation), it does not model or report payoffs, efficiency, or welfare. The results may be relevant for understanding the conditions under which cooperation is structurally stable, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions or control efficiency.

- source: Shimura, H., & Nakamaru, M. (2018). Large group size promotes the evolution of cooperation in the mutual-aid game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.

  findings: The paper finds that in the mutual-aid game (an 'all for one' group helping structure), larger group size and more repeated interactions (rounds) both promote the evolution and stability of cooperation, as measured by the expected payoff of conditional cooperators relative to defectors. This is in contrast to the standard public goods game, where larger groups typically undermine cooperation. The effect is robust to implementation and perception errors, and is strongest under certain reputation assessment rules. The paper does not include punishment or costly sanctioning mechanisms; all incentives are mediated by reputation and conditional cooperation. The findings are directly about efficiency (payoff) outcomes, not just cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in mutual-aid games with indirect reciprocity and reputation-based conditional cooperation (but no explicit punishment), increasing group size and number of rounds both increase the likelihood that cooperation (and thus high efficiency) will evolve and persist. The results are not directly transferable to standard public goods games with punishment, but they suggest that the structure of the game (e.g., 'all for one' vs 'all for all') and the presence of reputation-based mechanisms can strongly moderate the effect of group size and repetition on efficiency. Since punishment is not present, the paper does not inform the effect of enabling punishment per se, but it does provide a baseline for efficiency in a related class of games without punishment.

- source: Lee, H. W., & Weng, Y. N. (2025). Granular Q-learning adaptation boosts collective welfare in multi-agent Prisoner's Dilemma. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that more granular, neighbor-specific Q-learning (ID) enables agents to achieve higher collective welfare (average payoff) and maintain cooperation more robustly than aggregated Q-learning (II) in multi-agent Prisoner's Dilemma games. This advantage is most pronounced at intermediate dilemma strengths and in homogeneous networks, but persists in scale-free networks as well. The results are based on extensive simulations varying network structure, dilemma strength, and agent composition. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present; the only difference is the learning architecture. The findings suggest that the granularity of learning adaptation is a key moderator of efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial and scale-free Prisoner's Dilemma environments, the granularity of learning adaptation (neighbor-specific vs. aggregated) is a major determinant of collective efficiency. While not a public goods game or a punishment study, the results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games where the main design dimension is the learning rule or information granularity. The absence of punishment/reward means the findings are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled PGGs, but the payoff-based outcome and the focus on learning architecture offer useful indirect evidence for how cognitive sophistication or information structure can boost efficiency. The results are most applicable to settings where agent adaptation, rather than explicit sanctions, is the main intervention.

- source: Das Bairagya, J., Mondal, S. S., Chowdhury, D., & Chakraborty, S. (2023). Eco-evolutionary games for harvesting self-renewing common resource: effect of growing harvester population. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the realization or prevention of the tragedy of the commons (TOC) in a common-pool resource system depends critically on the relationship between the resource's intrinsic growth rate and the harvesting rates of the population, as well as on the incentive structure encoded in the payoff matrix. When the resource's growth rate is between the low and high harvesting rates, both collapse and partial/complete prevention of TOC are possible, depending on initial conditions and parameter values. Finite population size (carrying capacity) can introduce bistability and limit cycles, making the system's fate sensitive to initial conditions. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, but suggests that such extensions could be studied in future work. The main outcome relevant to efficiency is the long-run resource state, which serves as a proxy for group welfare or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how resource dynamics, harvesting strategies, and population size interact to determine group efficiency in common-pool resource settings. While it does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, it offers a detailed analysis of how efficiency (resource state) depends on game parameters such as harvesting rates, resource growth, and population carrying capacity. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding baseline efficiency outcomes in the absence of punishment or reward, and for highlighting the importance of ecological feedbacks and population size. It does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does suggest that efficiency is highly sensitive to the incentive structure and ecological parameters. The discussion of future extensions to include policing or punishment indicates that the model could be adapted for such analyses.

While explicit punishment is not modeled, the results suggest that mechanisms which promote cooperation (such as influence-based selection) also improve group-level payoffs and resource efficiency.

However, because explicit punishment is not modeled, the direct transferability to punishment-enabled PGGs is limited. Punishment or sanctions are not a focus, and the game structure is distinct from standard public goods games. The main contribution is a theoretical exploration of how cooperation rates fluctuate and may fail to converge in certain stochastic environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants.   findings: The paper finds that infection-reducing (cooperative) behavior, inferred from spatial disease data, increases in response to government-imposed lockdowns and is positively correlated with wealth. The tiered restriction system had limited impact except at the highest levels. Delays in public perception of risk can destabilize behavioral and disease dynamics, leading to oscillations. The model accurately reproduces observed infection rates and spatial patterns, but does not analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes in the sense of public goods games with monetary payoffs or explicit punishment.

- source: Garbus, J., & Pollack, J. (2024). Emergent Resource Exchange and Tolerated Theft Behavior Using Multiagent Reinforcement Learning. *Artificial Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper demonstrates that agents in a foraging environment with periodic forced congregation (via a campfire and night penalty) can learn to exchange resources, leading to higher cumulative group reward (efficiency) than agents in environments without such structure. The emergence of cooperation is sensitive to environmental parameters: with a strong night penalty, agents reliably learn to trade and achieve higher rewards; with a weak or absent penalty, agents do not reliably trade and achieve lower rewards. No explicit punishment or sanctioning is present; instead, agents develop indirect defenses against defection (e.g., exchange protocols that prevent being cheated). The study provides evidence that environmental design, rather than explicit punishment, can be sufficient for the emergence of efficient cooperation in multiagent systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or manipulated. However, it is informative for prediction tasks in that it shows efficient cooperation and high group payoff can emerge in the absence of punishment, given appropriate environmental structure (e.g., forced repeated interaction, resource interdependence, and congregation). For prediction, this suggests that the presence or absence of punishment is not the only moderator of efficiency: environmental design can be equally or more important. The paper does not inform the marginal effect of enabling punishment relative to a control, so it should not be used to estimate the effect of punishment per se.

- source: Laird, R. A. (2018). Sequential interactions-in which one player plays first and another responds-promote cooperation in evolutionary-dynamical simulations of single-shot Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Sequential interactions in single-shot Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift games allow for the emergence of conditional strategies, which can increase the average payoff of the population compared to simultaneous play. In the spatial Prisoner's Dilemma, sequential play greatly expands the parameter range (cost-benefit ratio) over which cooperation and higher payoffs are possible. In the Snowdrift game, sequential play leads to high average payoffs across the full parameter range. The effect is due to the structure of the game and the possibility of conditional responses, not to punishment, reward, or repeated interaction.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. However, it is highly informative about the effect of sequential versus simultaneous play in two-player social dilemmas (Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift), showing that sequential play can increase efficiency by enabling conditional strategies. Since the paper does not include punishment or sanctions, it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it may be useful for understanding the role of game structure and timing in cooperation and efficiency.

- source: Zhu, E. Q., Chen, J., Liu, S. Q., Zhang, X. K., & Liu, C. J. (2025). The influence of heterogeneous investment on the evolution of trust in complex networks. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study shows that in a networked trust game, allowing investors to adjust their investment amounts based on local trust levels (heterogeneous investment) significantly increases total group assets and the prevalence of trust, compared to homogeneous investment. As the heterogeneity parameter alpha increases, the system transitions from collapse (no investors, zero assets) to stable coexistence of investors and trustworthy trustees, with high group efficiency. The effect is robust to initial conditions and persists even under strong social dilemmas. However, the model does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in investment (contribution) can promote efficiency and trust in trust-game-like settings, but cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for the treatment effect of punishment, but may provide indirect context for the role of contribution heterogeneity.

- source: Gros, C. (2022). Collective strategy condensation towards class-separated societies. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that adding an envy-based psychological component to agents' utility functions in a competitive resource allocation game leads to a phase transition: for low envy, agents play diverse pure and mixed strategies, but for high envy, a large fraction of agents adopt the same mixed strategy, forming a lower class. This transition is accompanied by a substantial drop in mean income (efficiency), indicating that envy is detrimental to group welfare. The results are robust to variations in model details and to heterogeneity in envy levels. The paper also estimates, using ultimatum game data, that real-world levels of envy are likely high enough to induce this class-stratified, low-efficiency regime.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that adding a psychological penalty for relative disadvantage (envy) to a competitive resource allocation game reduces group efficiency/welfare, especially beyond a critical threshold. While the game is not a public goods game or a punishment game, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where psychological or social comparison motives are present. However, the paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or public goods provision directly, so its predictive value is limited to adjacent cases where social comparison or envy-like terms are present in the utility function. The main actionable insight is that increasing the weight of social comparison (envy) reduces efficiency, even when the underlying game structure is otherwise unchanged.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The model demonstrates that when participation is voluntary and the payoff to mutual cooperation is stochastic (sometimes exceeding the benefit from cheating), equilibria can exist in which both cooperation and cheating are present, but the expected payoff (efficiency) is strictly positive compared to non-participation. The frequency of cooperation and the value of the game (efficiency) increase as the distribution of cooperative benefits becomes more favorable. The model does not include punishment or sanctions, and thus does not address their effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is a theoretical model of cooperation with voluntary participation and stochastic payoffs, but without any punishment or sanction mechanism. Therefore, it does not inform prediction of treatment efficiency from the game design dimensions plus control efficiency in the context of punishment. However, it does provide theoretical support for the importance of voluntary participation and payoff structure in sustaining efficient cooperation.

- source: Bellucci, G. (2022). A Model of Trust. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper formalizes trust as a trade-off between vulnerability (potential loss) and positive expectations of a partner's behavior, using a computational model applied to the investment game. Simulations show that trustful investors (high trust attitude) invest more and are more likely to recover trust after breaches, but are also more vulnerable to exploitation. Loss aversion reduces investment and makes trust repair harder, while positive expectations and evidence of partner benevolence or competence increase investment. The model predicts that both trust attitude and learning about the partner's behavior jointly determine investment and, by extension, payoffs. The model is extended to consider both benevolence and competence of the trustee, showing that both traits affect investment and expected payoffs. However, the paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or public goods games, and does not report empirical efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is a theoretical modeling paper focused on the investment game (trust game), which is adjacent but not equivalent to the public goods game. The model and its simulations may inform understanding of how psychological factors (trust, expectations, loss aversion) affect investment and payoffs in dyadic trust settings, but do not address punishment, sanctions, or group-level efficiency in multi-player public goods contexts. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

- source: Lu, W., & Liang, S. (2025). The role of strategy-affected emotions in the evolution of cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial three-strategy prisoner's dilemma model with emotion dynamics, increasing the intensity of friendliness (the degree to which strategies affect emotions) leads to higher levels of cooperation and higher average payoffs in the population. The effect is robust across different parameterizations of the game (including donor-recipient and boundary PD variants). Memory length moderates the effect: with low friendliness, longer memory increases cooperation; with high friendliness, longer memory decreases cooperation. The model demonstrates that stable, high-payoff cooperation can emerge through emotion-strategy feedbacks alone, without any punishment or reward mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It is relevant as a theoretical demonstration that high cooperation and group payoff can be achieved through emotion-based mechanisms, without punishment or reward. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects, this paper suggests that alternative mechanisms (like friendliness) can substitute for punishment in promoting efficiency, but it does not inform how punishment would change efficiency relative to a control. The game design dimensions relevant to punishment (punishment_cost, punishment_tech, etc.) are not present or manipulated. Thus, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment or reward.

  findings: The study finds that introducing inter-group competition (external rewards for competing with other groups) increases the level of cooperation within groups, especially as the intensity (density) of inter-group connections increases. Strategic structuring of inter-group connections (e.g., connecting high-degree nodes in one group to low-degree nodes in another) can further enhance cooperation in the favored group. These effects are robust to different network generation models and strategy updating rules. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, and does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is not relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, as it studies a different mechanism (external rewards via inter-group competition) and reports only cooperation rates, not efficiency or payoff. The game structure is adjacent to PGGs (donation game within groups), but the absence of punishment and the focus on non-payoff outcomes limit its value for the downstream prediction task.

- source: McCannon, B. C. (2018). Social norms and cooperation. *International Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that incorporating a preference for social norm compliance into a one-shot prisoner's dilemma can generate cooperative behavior as an equilibrium outcome, even without repeated interaction, communication, or punishment. The likelihood of cooperation depends on the strength of the disutility from norm violation. However, the non-cooperative outcome is always an equilibrium as well. The model is presented as a theoretical explanation for observed cooperation in experiments, but does not itself report empirical results or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes, and is focused on a two-player, one-shot prisoner's dilemma. Its main contribution is to show that social norm preferences can support cooperation in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, except as a reminder that cooperation can arise from norm compliance alone, and that the absence of punishment does not necessarily imply zero cooperation. It does not inform how punishment affects efficiency or how game design dimensions moderate that effect.

- source: Quan, J., Feng, Y. X., Nie, J. C., & Wang, X. J. (2025). Catalyst strategy for the evolution of cooperation in indirect reciprocity. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.

  findings: The study finds that certain conditional cooperation strategies, especially those tolerant of cooperative behavior and harsh toward defection (the 'catalyst strategy'), can promote high levels of cooperation in indirect reciprocity models based on second-order action labels. The presence of noise (action or label errors) reduces cooperation rates but does not qualitatively change the system's state. Larger populations enhance the effectiveness of the catalyst strategy, while smaller populations and higher noise diminish it. However, the model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and efficiency or group payoff is not a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings on cooperation rates in indirect reciprocity models with second-order action labels may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how certain strategy structures (e.g., tolerance and harshness) affect cooperation, but it does not inform predictions about punishment-enabled efficiency. The absence of payoff/efficiency as a central outcome and the lack of a punishment mechanism mean this paper should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2014). Randomness in the network inhibits cooperation based on the bounded rational collective altruistic decision. *Journal of Physics A-mathematical and Theoretical*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial PDG with sequential strategies and bounded rational collective altruistic decision-making, high levels of cooperation can be achieved when the network is regular (low randomness). As network randomness increases, the average frequency of mutual cooperation decreases, especially when some groups have low connectivity. Groups with fewer connections ('straying groups') are less able to evolve cooperative strategies and act as obstacles to overall cooperation. Changes in the number of groups or the number of strategies per group do not significantly affect cooperation, but lower connectivity and longer strategy sequences can slow or inhibit the evolution of cooperation. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods or similar games. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or related mechanisms. Its findings about network structure and bounded rationality may be contextually relevant for understanding cooperation dynamics, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on cooperation frequency rather than payoff or efficiency limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

  findings: The study finds that introducing a community learning mechanism—where individuals preferentially imitate the most successful community members—substantially increases the proportion of cooperators in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma simulation. The effect is strongest when the minimum community size is small and when individuals are more likely to join large, distant communities (low distance cost). The emergence of large, cohesive communities is associated with high cooperation rates. However, the paper does not analyze or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and there are no punishment or reward mechanisms in the model.

  findings: The study finds that allowing players to pay a cost to access information about other players' strategies and payoffs can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is non-monotonic: too little or too much information/cost reduces cooperation. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. The results are based on agent-based simulations, not laboratory or field experiments.

Its findings about information acquisition and cooperation rates may be contextually relevant for understanding how information costs affect cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency under punishment. The paper is best used as contextual background for the role of information in cooperation, not for quantitative prediction of punishment effects.

  findings: The study finds that in a spatial continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma with limited resources, lower total resource and intermediate temptation to defect can promote higher average cooperation (measured by the average characteristic parameter, CP). However, the model does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The results are primarily about the evolution of cooperation and survival under resource constraints.

- source: Tan, S. L. (2019). Proximity inheritance explains the evolution of cooperation under natural selection and mutation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that proximity inheritance in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma model leads to the emergence and maintenance of high levels of cooperation, even in the presence of mutation. Cooperation is favored when offspring are placed within the neighborhood of their parents, and the effect is robust to variations in temptation of defection, selection intensity, and mutation rate. The model demonstrates that dynamic clustering of cooperators can arise, and that cooperation collapses if proximity inheritance is removed (i.e., if offspring are placed randomly or at the site of the dead individual). However, the paper does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes, and the game structure is a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma rather than a public goods game. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how spatial structure and inheritance of proximity can promote cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

  findings: The paper finds that introducing multiple interaction patterns (conditional and stochastic) based on opponent's ability can significantly increase the density of cooperators in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The proportion of individuals using each pattern and the estimation level of partner ability are key moderators: moderate estimation levels maximize cooperation, while too low or too high estimation levels reduce it. - source: Wang, X. J., Song, C. Y., & Yang, Z. P. (2025). Cooperation evolution of public goods games under probabilistic punishment rule for reputational altruism. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The main finding is that PSO-based strategy updating rules can enhance cooperation in continuous strategy systems (where players can choose any cooperation level between 0 and 1), but this effect disappears in mixed strategy systems (where players must choose all-or-nothing cooperation or defection). The weighting parameter ω, which balances historical best and neighbor imitation, has a non-monotonic effect: low ω promotes cooperation when temptation to defect is low, while high ω helps sustain cooperation when temptation is high. The results are robust across weak and true prisoner's dilemma parameterizations. However, the paper does not study punishment, reward, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is adjacent in relevance, as it studies cooperation dynamics in a related social dilemma (prisoner's dilemma) on networks, with a focus on strategy update rules and the divisibility of cooperation (continuous vs. all-or-nothing). The findings may inform expectations about the impact of strategy space (continuous vs. binary) on cooperation, but do not address punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited.

However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.

  findings: The study finds that positive aspiration (preference for cooperation) always increases the fraction of cooperators, regardless of social influence strength. Strong social influence (more neighbors) promotes cooperation only when paired with positive aspiration; otherwise, it can undermine cooperation. The joint effect of positive aspiration and strong social influence yields the highest cooperation rates. However, all results are in terms of the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in a networked weak prisoner's dilemma, populations with more positive average attitudes achieve higher average cooperation levels, while negative attitudes reduce cooperation. Attitude heterogeneity decreases cooperation when the average attitude is positive, but increases cooperation when the average attitude is negative. These effects are consistent across both regular (square lattice) and scale-free (BASF) networks. The model does not include punishment, rewards, or explicit efficiency/payoff outcomes, and focuses on behavioral cooperation rates.

  findings: The study finds that when cooperators are initially placed at the prime-numbered locations of Ulam's spiral on a spatial grid, they can grow to dominate the population under certain parameter conditions (specifically, when the temptation to defect b ≤ 1.33 and grid size n ≥ 23). The paper does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcomes, nor does it include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.

- source: Loch-Temzelides, T. (2021). Walrasian equilibrium behavior in nature. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that the trading behavior of mycorrhizal fungi in exchanging phosphorus for carbon with plant roots is consistent with Walrasian equilibrium, as revealed by the satisfaction of GARP in the observed data. This implies that the resulting allocations are Pareto efficient. The study provides a proof-of-concept that biological markets can achieve efficient outcomes as predicted by general equilibrium theory, even in the absence of human participants or explicit institutional mechanisms. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and revealed preference tests using data from a prior biological experiment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not study public goods, punishment, or sanctions. Its findings are relevant to the efficiency of biological market exchanges and the applicability of Walrasian equilibrium, but not to the design dimensions or efficiency outcomes of PGGs with or without punishment. It provides no evidence on how punishment, player count, or other PGG design features affect efficiency.
